COLLEGE 

HISTORIES 

CAMBRIDGE 


EMMANUEL   COLLEGE 


We  know  what  Master  laid  thy  keel, 

What  Workman  wrought  thy  ribs  of  steel, 

Who  made  each  mast,  and  sail,  and  rope, 

What  anvils  rang,  what  hammers  beat. 

In  what  a  forge  and  what  a  heat 

Were  shaped  the  anchors  of  thy  hope. 

Fear  not  each  sudden  sound  and  shock, 

'Tis  of  the  wave,  and  not  the  rock ; 

'Tis  but  the  flapping  of  the  sail, 

And  not  a  rent  made  by  the  gale. 

In  spite  of  rock  and  tempest's  roar, 

In  spite  of  false  lights  on  the  shore, 

Sail  on,  nor  fear  to  breast  the  sea  ! 

Our  hearts,  our  hopes,  are  all  with  thee  ; 

Our  hearts,  our  hopes,  our  prayers,  our  tears, 

Our  faith  triumphant  o'er  our  fears, 

Are  all  with  thee — are  all  with  thee.

336516 


PREFACE 

The  name  of  Emmanuel  College  is  in  itself  an  indication 
of  the  origin  and  purpose  of  the  foundation.  In  a 
discussion  as  to  the  use  of  the  word  in  Notes  and 
Queries  (8th  Series,  vii.  268,  351,  396),  it  is  shown 
that  it  had  long  been  the  custom  in  the  seventeenth 
century,  and  perhaps  earlier,  especially  among  men  of 
a  Puritan  cast,  to  use  the  word  as  the  heading  of 
letters  or  books  and  records.  One  correspondent  quotes 
2  Henry  VI.  (IV.  ii),  when  the  clerk  of  Chatham  is 
brought  before  Jack  Cade  : 

Cade.  Come  hither,  sirrah,  I  must  examine  thee  :  What 
is  thy  name  ? 

Clerk.  Emmanuel. 

Dick.  They  use  to  write  it  on  the  top  of  letters :  'twill 
go  hard  with  you. 

The  use  of  the  word  as  a  kind  of  badge  is  in 
accordance  with  the  Scripture  phraseology  so  common 
among  the  Puritans  at  all  times,  and  has  survived  in 
the  name  of  many  Nonconformist  chapels  and  some 
churches  to  this  day.  At  the  time  of  the  foundation 
of  the  College  it  had  not  become  vulgarized,  and  was 

vii 


viii  PREFACE 

regarded  with  serious  reverence  as  indicating  a  profound 
sense  of  the  Divine  presence.  The  '  Memorial  for  a 
Son'  drawn  up  by  Sir  Walter  Mildmay,  and  lately 
published  by  the  Rev.  Arundell  St.  John  Mildmay,"'^ 
begins  and  ends  with  the  exhortation  to  '  fear  God  and 
love  Him,'  to  'read  the  Scriptures  daily  and  hear 
sermons  diligently.'  The  whole  of  these  admonitions, 
with  their  worldly  prudence  and  religious  earnestness, 
give  the  clue  to  the  purpose  of  Sir  Walter  in  his 
foundation,  as  I  have  endeavoured  to  show  in  this  book. 
Details  of  College  management,  of  changing  methods 
and  ideals,  of  pecuniary  difficulties  or  increasing  wealth, 
may  have  their  charm ;  but  the  real  interest  of  the 
history  of  Emmanuel  is  that  it  coincides  with,  and 
reflects,  a  great  national  movement.  The  College  rose 
with  the  rising  tide  of  Puritanism,  declined  with  its 
decline;  contributed  even  more  than  its  just  proportion 
to  the  seed-plot  of  the  New  England  in  the  West ; 
shared  to  the  full  in  the  dawn  of  a  more  liberal 
theology ;  and  through  the  days  of  decadence  and 
deadness,  though  it  did  not  escape  their  numbing 
influence,  never  wholly  lost  the  love  of  learning  or  the 
sense  of  duty  to  its  trust.  Throughout  the  eighteenth 
century,  with  decreasing  numbers,  and  for  the  greater 
part  of  the  time  with  straitened  means,  the  College 
never  ceased  to  produce  men  who  gained  distinction  in 
letters'  or  science  or  politics.  In  these  later  days  it  has 
always  kept  up  with,  and  sometimes  led,  the  movements 
in  favour  of  larger  and  more  liberal  views  of  education, 
of  raising  the  standard  and  multiplying  the  subjects  of 
learning  and  research. 

*  College  Magazine,  vol.  v.,  p.  5. 


PREFACE  ix 

Still,  it  is  the  earlier  chapters  in  its  history  that 
must  always  have  the  more  vivid  interest,  because  it 
was  then  that  it  most  obviously  reflects  the  spirit  of  the 
time.  For  the  first  fifty  years  of  its  existence  it  was 
marked  by  extreme  Puritanism,  though  perhaps  with  a 
diminishing  vehemence ;  but  the  beginning  of  the  Civil 
War  found  it  with  a  strongly  Royalist  Master  (Holds- 
worth),  who  committed  the  College  by  paying  ^100 
in  its  name  at  the  King's  demand.  And  when  the 
Restoration  came  it  was  clear  that  the  extraordinary 
change  which  had  come  over  the  nation  at  large  had 
had  its  full  effect  in  the  College.  With  hardly  any  excep- 
tion the  Society  were  ready  to  acquiesce  in  the  change 
and  all  it  involved.  The  Master  (Dillingham)  refused, 
indeed,  to  conform  in  1662,  but  his  objection  was 
neither  to  Royalty  nor  to  Church  authority,  properly 
speaking,  and  his  example  found  few  followers.  The 
movement  had  spent  itself  in  the  College,  as  it  had 
in  the  country.  It  may,  I  fear,  be  thought  that  the 
interest  of  this  subject  has  led  to  a  disproportionate 
space  being  assigned  in  this  book  to  the  earlier  period, 
to  the  neglect  of  later  stages  in  the  development  of  the 
College.  I  can  only  plead  that  as  we  get  nearer  our 
own  time  what  has  to  be  said  seems  more  fitted  for  a 
hand-book  than  a  history,  and  that  what  is  needed  to 
supplement  this  book  is  not  so  much  a  larger  history  of 
changes  of  statutes  and  expansions  of  estates,  but  a 
biographical  catalogue  of  men  educated  within  the 
College  walls.  They,  after  all,  represent  the  success  or 
failure  of  the  foundation,  the  merits  of  which  are  best 
ascertained  by  its  fruits.  But  this  is  an  undertaking 
for  which  I  have  neither  the  capability  nor  the  time. 


X  PREFACE 

I  hope  that  some  younger  man  may  be  found  to  do  for 
Emmanuel  what  has  been  done  for  Caius,  and  is  being 
done  for  King's  and  Christ's. 

Previous  accounts  of  the  College  of  any  considerable 
length  have  been — 

1.  By  Thomas  Fuller,  in  his  History  of  the  University 
of  Cambridge  (1655).  His  quaint  humour  must  always 
give  delight,  and  he  has  the  advantage  of  writing  at 
a  very  interesting  point  of  the  College  history.  But 
his  desire  to  be  epigrammatic  sometimes  makes  him 
oblivious  of  duller  but  necessary  details. 

2.  By  George  Dyer,  in  History  of  the  University  and 
Colleges  of  Cambridge  (1814).  Dyer  entered  Emmanuel 
in  1774  from  Christ's  Hospital,  the  last  year  of  Far- 
mer's tutorship,  to  whom  his  literary  abilities  much 
commended  him.  After  his  degree  in  1778  he  held  a 
number  of  minor  educational  posts,  and  eventually 
settled  in  Clifford's  Inn,  where  he  died  in  1841.  He 
was  a  friend  (and  butt)  of  Charles  Lamb.  His  numerous 
works  in  prose  and  verse  are  of  no  great  value,  but  his 
account  of  Emmanuel  in  the  book  above  mentioned  is 
interesting  from  his  personal  enthusiasm,  and  for  the 
account  of  many  of  its  worthies,  some  of  whom  he 
personally  knew. 

3.  By  C.  H.  Cooper,  in  the  third  volume  of  his 
Memorials  of  Cambridge  (1 858-1 866) .  Every  Cambridge 
man  owes  a  debt  of  gratitude  to  the  extraordinary  in- 
dustry and  accuracy  of  this  great  antiquary.  His  A  thenar 
Cantabrigienses  (1853)  has  also  been  of  great  help  in  the 
early  part  of  the  work,  as  also  his  Annals  of  Cambridge 
(1842). 

4.  By  Willis  and  Clark,  in  the  Architectural  History 


PREFACE  xi 

of  the  University  of  Cambridge  (1886),  which  contains 
the  best  account  of  the  College  buildings. 

5.  By  Atkinson  and  Clark,  in  Cambridge  Described 
and  Illustrated  (1897). 

6.  The  Emmanuel  College  Magazine  was  started  in 
May,  1889,  under  the  editorship  of  Dr.  Adam,  now  Senior 
Tutor,  and  continued  under  that  of  Mr.  J.  B.  Peace, 
the  Bursar.  It  appears  once  a  term,  and  contributions 
have  been  made  to  it  by  many  members  of  the  College, 
past  and  present,  on  points  of  interest  in  the  history 
of  the  College,  as,  for  instance,  by  Dr.  J.  J.  Raven, 
Vicar  of  Fressingfield  (Bancroft's  old  home),  who  is  the 
author  of  a  History  of  Siiffblk,  and  an  enthusiast  as  to 
all  that  concerns  the  College  and  the  Archbishop.  By 
no  one  have  more  numerous  and  valuable  contributions 
been  made  than  by  the  editor,  Mr.  Peace,  himself.  The 
result  is  an  accumulation  of  facts  forming  a  fairly 
complete  account  of  the  College,  which,  if  collected, 
would  make  a  much  better  book  than  mine.  I  have 
been  largely  indebted  to  this  storehouse  of  information. 
The  account  of  Hubbard's  tutorship  (pp.  139-151),  and 
of  the  early  endowments  of  the  College  in  the  Appendix, 
are,  the  former  mainly,  and  the  latter  wholly,  contribu- 
tions by  Mr.  Peace  to  this  Magazine.  For  leave  to  use 
them,  and  for  much  other  help  in  correcting  proofs 
and  pointing  out  sources  of  information  in  the  College 
Treasury,  I  owe  him  incalculable  gratitude.  The  Index 
I  owe  to  my  wife. 

E.  S.  SHUCKBURGH.

XV 


CHAPTER  I 

THE  SITE  OF  THE  COLLEGE  AND  THE  DEVELOP- 
MENT  OF  ITS  BUILDINGS 

The  continuity  of  English  life  is,  perhaps,  by  nothing 
more  vividly  illustrated  than  by  the  antiquity  of  the 
occupation  of  certain  portions  of  the  soil  for  definite 
public  objects,  especially  in  connexion  with  religion  or 
education. 

Emmanuel  College,  Cambridge,  has  been  founded 
about  320  years,  but  it  stands  on  ground  held  for  eccle- 
siastical and  quasi-educational  purposes  (with  a  brief 
interval)  for  more  than  650  years.  The  Dominicans  or 
Black  (Preaching)  Friars  came  into  England  first  in 
1221,  and  some  time  between  that  date  and  1240  they 
had  possession  of  a  house  and  church  standing  about 
200  yards  south  of  Barnwell  Gate,  at  the  point 
where  the  King's  Ditch  turned  to  the  north  in  the 
direction  of  the  river,  down  what  is  now  Hobson's 
Street.  The  west  end  of  the  church  looked  upon  the 
highroad,  which  came  to  be  called  Preachers'*  Street, 
after  the  friars,  and  is  now  St.  Andrew*'s  Street,  while 
on  the  south  side  of  it  ran  a  road  passing  through  the 
present  site  of  the  principal  court  of  the  College  in 
the  direction  of  Newmarket.     In  1240,  by  a  writ  of 

1 


2  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

Henry  IIL,  an  inquisition  was  held,  in  consequence  of 
which  the  friars  were  allowed  to  acquire  from  the  town 
this  road  and  some  land  beyond  it,  giving  up  in  return 
some  land  on  the  north  side  of  the  church,  which  is 
now  Emmanuel  Street.*  The  friars  thus  possessed 
grounds  of  about  eight  acres,  to  which  two  were  subse- 
quently added.  The  Dominican  church  stood  on  the 
site  of  the  present  hall  and  combination-room,  and  the 
buildings  for  the  accommodation  of  the  friars  were 
apparently  between  the  church  and  Emmanuel  Street, 
the  present  library  (at  right  angles  to  the  church) 
occupying  the  site  of  some  considerable  building, 
perhaps  the  refectory. 

The  friars  appear  to  have  taken  part  in  the  Uni- 
versity life  of  the  time,  and  to  have  proceeded  to 
degrees  in  divinity.  The  names  of  a  good  many 
members  of  the  house  are  preserved  who  played  a  more 
or  less  conspicuous  part  in  the  various  controversies 
which  agitated  the  ecclesiastical  world  up  to  the  time 
of  the  Dissolution.  Their  chief  benefactor  was  Alice, 
widow  of  Robert  de  Vere,  fifth  Earl  of  Oxford  (ob.  1295), 
whom,  indeed,  they  are  said  to  have  regarded  as  their 
foundress.  They  were  not  one  of  the  wealthy  founda- 
tions, though  they  enjoyed  a  pension  from  the  Crown, 
for  their  house  was  dissolved  under  the  Act  of  1536, 
which  only  affected  those  corporations  whose  income 
was  under  c£200.  The  last  Prior,  Gregory  Dodds,  B.D., 
escaped  any  ill  consequences  by  joining  with  fourteen 
of  his  friars  in  surrendering  the  house  to  the  King  on 

♦  It  may  be  remarked  that  by  a  fresh  agreement  with  the  town 
the  College  will  probably  reabsorb  this  ground,  the  road  being 
removed  some  50  yards  to  the  north. 


THE  SITE  OF  THE  COLLEGE  3 

April  16,  1538.  He  was  rewarded  with  the  living  of 
Smarden  in  Kent,  and  though  during  the  dangerous 
period  following  the  Act  of  the  Six  Articles  he  seems 
to  have  been  in  some  peril,  he  survived  it  and  the  two 
next  reigns,  dying  Dean  of  Exeter  in  1570.  Another 
of  the  surrendering  friars,  John  Scory,  had  still  more 
liberal  compensation,  for  he  was  successively  Preacher 
at  Canterbury,  Bishop  of  Rochester  and  of  Chichester ; 
and,  though  he  was  deprived  of  Chichester  by  Mary  in 
1553,  he  was  sent  to  Hereford  by  Elizabeth  in  1559, 
and  died  Bishop  of  that  see  in  1585.  He  seems  to  have 
been  very  vehement  on  the  side  of  his  new  opinions,  and 
during  the  Catholic  reaction  he  saved  his  own  life  by  a 
flight  to  the  Continent,  while  he  signed  a  letter  urging 
those  in  prison  for  their  faith  to  stay  and  suffer  martyr- 
dom. He  must  have  been  almost  the  only  one  of  the 
friars  who  lived  to  see  or  hear  of  the  site  of  his  old 
monastery  being  occupied  by  the  Protestant  foundation 
of  Emmanuel. 

After  the  surrender  of  1538,  the  site  with  the  build- 
ings on  it — no  doubt  the  usual  stripping  of  lead  from 
the  church  and  other  spoliation  having  meanwhile  taken 
place — passed  into  private  ownership,  in  spite  of  a 
petition  from  the  Vice- Chancellor  that,  as  well  as  other 
religious  houses,  it  should  be  converted  into  a  College. 
In  1544  the  ground  and  buildings  were  granted  to 
Edward  Elrington  and  Humphry  Metcalfe,  with  re- 
mainder to  the  heirs  of  Elrington  (April  16).  It  was, 
however,  sold  on  March  2  in  the  next  year  to  William 
Sherwood,  and  in  1581  to  Robert  Taylor  (April  1). 

To  what  uses  the  grounds  or  buildings  had  been  put 
between  1544  and  1583  we  have  no  information.    But  on 

1—2 


y 


4  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

June  12  in  the  latter  year  they  were  conveyed  by  Taylor 
to  Laurence  Chaderton  (already  selected  as  the  Master 
of  the  new  College)  and  his  brother-in-law,  Richard 
Culverwell,  citizen  of  London,  and  by  them  conveyed 
1  on  November  23  of  the  same  year  for  £550  to  Sir 
Walter  Mildmay,  the  founder  of  Emmanuel  College.* 
The  charter  of  Queen  Elizabeth  empowering  Sir  Walter 
to  found  a  College  is  dated  January  11,  1583-84;  his 
deed  of  foundation,  still  preserved,  is  dated  May  25, 
1584 ;  and  on  the  29th  Francis  Chamberlain,  of  Great 
Melton  in  Norfolk,  conveyed  to  the  Master,  Fellows,  and 
Scholars  the  rectory  and  tithes  of  Little  Melton  and 
land  in  Little  and  Great  Melton,  thus  attesting  their 
existence  as  a  corporate  body.  It  consisted  of  a  Master, 
Laurence  Chaderton,  B.D.,  late  Fellow  of  Christ's 
College ;  three  Fellows,  Charles  Chad  wick,  of  Christ's 
College,  William  Jones  and  Laurence  Pickering,  both 
of  Clare  Hall ;  and  four  scholars,  John  Duke,  Richard 
RolfFe,  John  Starkye,  and  Robert  Houghton.  Four 
other  Fellows  were  admitted  in  the  course  of  the  year.t 
Though  the  statutes  given  by  the  founder  are  dated 
October  1,  1585,  entrances  of  Fellow-commoners,  pen-

DEVELOPMENT  OF  THE  BUILDINGS       5 

sioners,  and  sizars  are  recorded  from  November  1 , 1 584, 
as  many  as  eighty  names  being  entered  in  the  College 
register  between  that  date  and  July  1,  1585.*  It 
therefore  appears  that  there  must  have  been  some 
structure  early  in  this  year  capable  of  accommodating 
a  considerable  number  of  men.  The  history  of  the 
development  of  these  buildings  seems  to  begin  with  a 
rapid  adaptation  of  some  of  those  already  existing. 
The  church  was  sufficiently  preserved — at  any  rate,  as 
far  as  the  walls  and  roof  were  concerned — to  be  at  once 
converted  into  a  dining-hall ;  for  in  1586  we  learn  from 
the  Bursar's  accounts  that  the  parlour  at  the  east  end 
of  it,  with  chambers  above,  was  being  fitted  up,  the 
hall  being  referred  to  as  already  in  use.  The  building 
already  noticed  as  having  possibly  been  the  refectory 
of  the  friary  (now  the  library)  was,  it  seems,  in  the 
same  space  of  time  repaired  and  fitted  up  as  a  chapel 
with  chambers  above.  This  formed  the  east  side  of  the 
smaller  court,  the  west  being  a  range  of  buildings  stand- 
ing somewhat  like  the  western  side  of  the  present  New 
Court,  and  with  the  kitchens  and  buttery  facing  on 
Preachers'   Street,   also   with   chambers    above.      The

entrance  was  in  the  middle  of  a  wall  facing  Emmanuel 
Street,*  and  from  it  a  path  passed  between  the  parlour 
and  the  entrance  to  the  chapel  and  Master's  Lodge  into 
the  greater  court  beyond.  The  west  side  of  this  greater 
court  was  the  first  built.  It  was  a  range  facinor 
Preachers'  Street,  standing  much  farther  back  than  the 
present  frontage,  at  both  ends  of  which  were  projecting 
wings.  That  on  the  north  seems  to  have  consisted  of 
chambers,  but  that  on  the  south  was  the  first  library. 
This  was  at  one  time  doubtful.f  But  an  entry  in  the 
Bursar's  accounts  for  the  year  1657  speaks  of  iron  SS 
for  the  library,  and  Loggan's  plan  shows  them  on  the 
south  wing  of  the  range  at  right  angles  to  the  hall. 

The  next  step  taken,  almost  at  once,  was  the  com- 
mencement of  the  '  Founder's  Range '  on  the  south  side 
of  the  greater  court,  opposite  the  dining-hall,  on  the 
same  site  on  which  the  restored  buildings  still  stand. 
This  was,  at  any  rate,  approaching  completion  by  the 
end  of  1587,  for  in  December  of  that  year  the  founder 
sends  a  supplementary  statute,  in  which  he  desires  that 
'  a  single  chamber  of  those  recently  erected — namely, 
that  which  is  at  the  end  of  the  range  towards  the  east, 
between  the  lowest  chamber  and  the  upper  chamber 
next  the  roof — may  be  appropriated  to  such  of  our 
descendants  as  may  be  studying  in  the  College.'  The 
set  of  rooms  still  spoken  of  as  '  Founder's  Chamber,'  and 

*  An  ornamental  doorway  is  shown  on  Loggan's  plan,  on  which

Ano  Domini  1584.'  The  architect  of  these  earliest  buildings  was 
Ralf  Symons,  whose  portrait  is  in  the  gallery,  and  who  had  done 
much  work  in  the  construction  of  Trinity.

recently  occupied  as  such,  is  in  the  same  situation, 
though  the  building  has  been  renewed.  Entries  in  the 
Bursar's  accounts  show  that  building  operations  were 
going  on  through  1588,  stone  being  carted  from  Cam- 
bridge Castle,  and  timber  being  sent  by  various  bene- 
factors. In  the  same  year  the  hall  was  whitewashed, 
the  larger  court  paved,  the  Master's  Lodge  in  course  of 
building,  the  great  gate  completed,  and  the  parlour 
furnished.  The  College  was  now  sufficiently  advanced 
for  a  '  dedication  '  festival,  which  was  accordingly  cele- 
brated in  the  presence  of  the  founder  in  1588.  The 
Bursar's  accounts  show  that  a  great  banquet  was  given. 
Two  does  are  brought  by  the  keepers  (probably  Sir 
Walter's),  a  'crag  of  sturgeon'  is  purchased,  the 
chambers  are  all  '  dressed,'  and  the  street  '  cleansed.' 

The  buildings  of  the  College  remained  in  the  state 
here  indicated,  with  some  unimportant  addition  to  the 
kitchen  range,  until  1632.  Meanwhile,  the  arrange- 
ment of  the  gardens  and  paddock,  with  some  modifica- 
tions afterwards  to  be  noted,  was  not  widely  different 
from  what  it  is  now.  But  there  was  one  building  which 
has  totally  disappeared.  That  was  a  tennis-court.  It 
seems  to  have  existed  from  the  earliest  times  of  the 
College,  perhaps  even  before  the  College  itself  was 
founded.  It  stood  along  the  wall  of  the  Fellows' 
Garden,  parallel  with  the  pond.  It  was  originally 
roofed,  but  the  roof  was  removed  in  1632.  It  con- 
tinued, however,  to  be  much  used.  A  College  order 
of  October  29,  1651,  runs : 

'For  the  better  regulating  of  the  Tenniz-court  it  is 
ordered  by  the  Master  and  Fellows  tmanimi  consensu  that 
the  key  of  it  shalbe  in  the  keeping  of  the  Deane,  who  is 


8  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

to  take  care  that  the  door  be  kept  lockt,  and  never 
suffered  to  play  dureing  the  howers  hereafter  mentioned, 
viz*^  from  one  of  the  clock  till  three  in  the  afternoone  and 
from  eight  of  the  clock  at  night  till  tenn  the  next  morn- 
ing ;  unless  any  of  the  fellows  shall  desire  to  play  there  in 
any  of  these  howers,  who  may  take  any  fellow  commoner 
with  them ;  yet  soe  as  they  cleare  the  Court,  shutt  the 
doore,  and  returne  the  key  to  the  Deane  at  their  comeing 
away.' 

The  tennis-court  seems  to  have  fallen  into  disuse 
early  in  the  eighteenth  century,*  when  the  boundary 
of  the  Fellows'  Garden  was  extended  so  as  to  include  it, 
its  south  wall  forming  part  of  the  new  garden  wall. 
This  south  wall  was  partly  demolished  by  a  fall  of  two 
lofty  elms  in  a  violent  storm  of  March  24, 1895.  When 
the  damaged  portion  of  this  wall  was  being  removed,  a 
small  teapot  was  found  carefully  bricked  up,  containing 
a  halfpenny  of  1773  and  the  following  paper  : 

'  This  wall  was  New  Cacet  Next  the  Master's  Close  in 

Feburey  1810. 

By  Wm.  Overland,  Bricklare. 

Jno.  Farran,  Labourer.

DEVELOPMENT  OF  THE  BUILDINGS       9 

To  return  to  the  buildings.  The  first  great  change 
was  started  in  1632  by  the  erection  of  the  'Brick 
Building,'  or,  as  we  sometimes  now  call  it,  the  '  Old 
Court,'  extending  southward  from  the  eastern  end  of 
the  'Founder's  Range'  to  what  was  then  called  Pits 
Garden,  which  seems  to  be  the  piece  of  land  acquired 
by  the  College  only  in  1899.  The  contract  for  this 
building  is  dated  February  9,  1632-33,  between  John 
Westley,  bricklayer,  and  the  Master,  Dr.  William  Sand- 
croft,  and  the  Fellows.  The  amount  of  the  bricklayer's 
contract  was  £665,  that  of  the  carpenter  (Henry  Man) 
c£^285.  Both  were  to  have  the  use  of  materials  from 
the  tennis-court — the  bricklayer  the  '  tyles  and  lathes,' 
and  the  carpenter  '  the  timber  and  cover.'  The  brick- 
layer was  also  to  have  the  materials  of  the  old  wall, 
which  ran  along  the  site  of  the  new  building.  Bishop 
Bennet,  in  1788,  says  that  £500  had  been  spent  on  it 
since,  and  that  even  so  it  was  in  a  '  tottering  state,  and 
only  kept  together  by  the  strength  of  the  chimneys.' 
He  attributes  this  to  the  original  '  execution '  having 
been  '  shamefully  negligent.'  Certainly,  some  external 
features,  such  as  the  dormers  of  the  garret  windows, 
have  had  to  be  removed,  perhaps  because  they  were 
badly  constructed;  but  either  the  Bishop  took  an 
unnecessarily  pessimistic  view,  or  the  subsequent  repairs 
were  very  effective,  for  the  building  is  still  substantially 
sound  as  well  as  picturesque.  At  any  rate,  bricklayer 
and  carpenter  were  punctual,  the  last  payment  being 
made  on  April  22,  1634,  nor  can  <^20  for  extras  to  the 
bricklayer  be  thought  excessive. 

The  next  great  change  in  building  and  arrangement 
of  the  College  was  started  soon  after  the  restoration  of 


10  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

1660  by  Dr.  Sancroft,  the  future  nonjuring  Archbishop 
of  Canterbury,  who  was  Master  from  August,  1662,  to 
May,  1665.  His  immediate  motives  were  two.  A  con- 
siderable legacy  of  books  from  Dr.  Holdsworth  (which, 
however,  did  not  reach  the  College)  made  it  necessary 
to  have  a  larger  library,  and  the  chapel  was  in  many 
ways  distasteful  to  the  Church  party,  now  in  the  ascen- 
dant, particularly  in  the  fact  that  it  stood  north  and 
south,  instead  of  east  and  west.  He  therefore  designed 
to  build  a  new  chapel  and  library,  though  the  old  chapel 
was  afterwards  used  for  the  latter.  This  plan  had  been 
constantly  in  his  thoughts  during  his  mastership.  In  a 
letter  to  his  old  tutor,  Ezechiel  Wright,  dated  from  the 
College  on  January  17,  1663,  he  says  (speaking  of  his 
hope  to  secure  Holdsworth's  library) : 

'  For  the  performance  whereof,  and  also  for  the  removing 
that  great  mark  of  singularity,  which  all  the  world  so  talks 
of,  in  the  unusual  prospect  and  dress  of  the  Chapel  (different 
from  that  of  other  Colleges),  I  have  it  in  design  to  make 
both  a  new  Library  and  Chapel  too.'* 

Though  his  rapid  promotions — first  to  the  deanery 
of  York  and  then  to  that  of  St.  Paul's — prevented  him 
from  carrying  out  this  work  as  Master  (he  resigned  on 
succeeding  to  St.  Paul's,  May,  1665),  he  did  not  lose 
interest  in  it.  In  one  way  his  connexion  with  St. 
Paul's  probably  influenced  the  final  result;  for  both 
before  and  after  the  Great  Fire  he  was  in  constant 
intercourse  with  Christopher  Wren,  in  surveying  the 
old   Cathedral,  which  was  becoming  ruinous,   and  in 

*  D'Oyly's  Life  of  Sancroft,  vol.  i.,  p.  130.  The  original  letter 
is  in  the  College  treasury,  and  will  be  given  at  length  hereafter. 


DEVELOPMENT  OF  THE  BUILDINGS     11 

making  plans  for  the  new  one  after  the  fire.  It  was  no 
doubt  in  part  owing  to  this  intimacy  that  Wren  was 
asked  to  make  a  plan  for  the  new  chapel,  and  for  the 
cloister  and  noble  gallery  above  it,  which  is  now  such  a 
notable  feature  in  the  College.  Sancroft's  successor  in 
the  mastership  (1666),  Dr.  Breton,  was  eager  to  cany 
out  the  project,  and,  writing  in  January,  1667,  to  San- 
croft,  he  mentions  the  receipt  of  '  the  modell  of  our 
Chapell,"  for  which  he  conveys  the  warm  thanks  of  the 
whole  society.  In  another  letter  (September  24,  1667) 
he  mentions  two  criticisms  on  the  model :  first,  that  it 
did  not  seem  lofty  enough,  and,  secondly,  that  if  there 
was  to  be  no  east  window  the  side-windows  should  be 
larger.  Sancroft  started  the  subscription  list  for  the 
chapel,  and  subscribed  munificently  himself. 

The  work  was  begun  early  in  1668.  In  January  Breton 
writes  to  Sancroft :  '  We  prepare  for  the  foundation  of 
our  Chapell,  and  much  desire  Dr.  Wren''s  advice  upon 
the  place."  In  February  he  went  into  Northampton- 
shire, and  signed  a  contract  with  Simon  Wise,  of  Dean, 
and  Nicholas  Ashby,  of  Ketton,  for  the  supply  of 
stone.  And  in  March  money  is  recorded  as  paid  to 
labourers  for  pulling  down  the  old  wall  on  the  east  side 
of  the  court ;  in  May  more  is  paid  for  pumps  and 
buckets  to  remove  the  water  from  the  foundations. 
Forty  large  trees  were  given  by  the  Earl  of  Westmor- 
land (descendant  of  the  founder)  from  Apethorpe,  which 
were  brought  by  water  to  Barnwell.  Much  other 
timber  was  bought  elsewhere.  Dr.  Breton,  however, 
did  not  live  to  see  the  building  completed.  He  died  in 
1676,  and  was  succeeded  by  Dr.  Thomas  Holbech  (who 
heads  the  list  of  original  subscribers),  but  it  was  not 


12  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

till  September  29,  1677,  that  the  consecration  was  per- 
formed by  Bishop  Gunning  of  Ely.  The  gradual  im- 
provement of  the  internal  fittings  went  on  for  some  ten 
years  more,*  and  the  organ  was  not  erected  till  1682. 
The  painting  by  Giacomo  Amiconi,  a  Venetian,  now 
on  the  altar-piece  was  presented  in  1734  by  Chris- 
topher Nevile,  Fellow-commoner.  As  a  final  act  in  the 
removal  of  the  place  of  worship  of  the  College,  the 
bodies  of  Chaderton  and  Breton  were  removed  from  the 
old  to  the  new  chapel  in  December,  1677.  Along  with 
the  chapel  was  constructed  the  present  cloister  and 
gallery  above  it,  also  from  a  design  by  Wren,  who 
proposed  building  it  of  red  brick.  It  was,  however, 
for  some  unknown  reason  ashlared  from  the  first.  The 
only  other  variation  in  this  cloister  from  Wren's  design 
was  the  widening  of  the  central  arch. 

This  erection  made  the  general  configuration  of  the 
large  court  what  it  is  now.  But  its  aspect  has  under- 
gone two  changes.  First  in  the  Founder's  Range.  In 
1714-15,  on  March  10,  there  was  a  College  order  for  the 
suspension  of  two  Fellowships  because  '  a  good  sum  of 
money'  was  required  for  the  repair  of  the  buildings, 
which  '  in  the  walls,  roofs,  and  tilings  are  much  decayed 
and  out  of  repair.'  Four  years  afterwards  (August  7, 
1719)  another  College  order  directed  that  the  Founder's 
Range  should  be  pulled  down  and  a  new  range  of 
buildings  erected.  Subscriptions  were  invited,  and  a 
total  of  ^2,400  was  raised,  Thomas,  Earl  of  Westmor-

piece  of  carved  oak,  along  with  more  elaborate  altar-rails.

land,  heading  the  list  with  <£*500,  and  his  brother  and 
successor  in  the  earldom,  John,  giving  <i?100.  Besides 
this,  the  College  borrowed  .^1,100,  and  a  College  order 
of  January  8,  1725,  shows  that  this  had  not  been  paid, 
and  that  about  c^SOO  still  remained  owing  to  the  con- 
tractors. In  consequence,  therefore,  '  of  an  insigne 
detrimentum,  by  reason  of  the  great  debts  the  College 
has  contracted  in  rebuilding  that  part  of  it  commonly 
called  the  Founder's  Range,*'  it  was  agreed  not  to  fill  up 
Fellowships  till  such  time  as  the  Master  and  Fellows 
shall  think  proper.  The  debt  was  slowly  reduced,  but 
it  was  not  till  December,  1742,  that  it  was  thought 
possible  to  add  to  the  number  of  the  Fellows,  and  not 
till  October,  1745,  that  the  College  was  declared  free  of 
debt.  The  building  itself  seems  to  have  been  practically 
completed  in  the  summer  of  1724,  when  Lord  and  Lady 
Westmorland  were  entertained  in  the  College,  but 
payments  for  work  of  various  sorts  in  it  go  on  till 
October,  1728.*  The  building  was  on  October  15, 
1811,  gutted  by  an  accidental  fire,  none  of  it  escaping 
except  the  stone  front  next  the  court  and  part  of  the 
inside  walls.  Another  collection  was  made  of  d^4,484, 
and  it  was  rebuilt  in  the  same  style  and  with  the  same 
internal  arrangements,  except  that  the  panelling  of  the 
rooms  was  not  restored.  It  has  not  been  altered  since, 
except  that  the  candles  which  thirty  years  ago  dimly 
flickered  on  the  staircases  have  been  supplanted  first  by 
gas,  and  lastly  by  the  electric  light  supplied  by  the 
engine  in  the  paddock. 

*  The  range  was  henceforth  called  the  Westmorland  Building, 
and  the  arms  of  the  Earl  of  Westmorland  were  placed  over  the 
central  staircase  at  a  cost  of  ;^  150. 


14  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

The  next  alteration,  which  finally  brought  the  front 
court  to  its  present  aspect,  was  not  consummated  till 
1769.  This  was  the  demolition  of  the  old  range  facing 
Preachers'*  Street,  on  the  west  of  the  great  court,  and 
the  erection  of  the  present  frontage.  This  had  been 
contemplated  as  early  as  1752,  and  a  plan  was  prepared 
by  Mr.  Burrough  (afterwards  Sir  James)  ;  but  it 
appears  not  to  have  been  approved,  and  a  considerable 
sum  of  money  which  had  been  subscribed  was  returned 
to  the  donors.  One  at  least  of  the  objections  to  the 
scheme  came  from  the  Master  (Richardson),  who  wished 
to  have  the  combination-room  added  to  the  Lodge.  In 
1769  the  scheme  was  revived,  and  in  March  it  was 
resolved  to  adopt  the  plan  of  Sir  James  Burrough.  In 
July,  however,  of  the  same  year  a  new  plan  was  substi- 
tuted, made  by  Burroughs  pupil,  James  Essex,  who 
had  already  done  much  work  in  Cambridge  at  Queens', 
the  University  Library,  St.  Catherine's,  Trinity,  and 
Great  St.  Mary's.  He  had  also  been  employed  by  the 
College,  as  far  back  as  1764,  in  the  alteration  and  repair 
of  the  hall,"*^  and  he  now  designed  the  west  front  as  it 
stands  at  present.  Subscriptions  to  about  <£'3,000  were 
collected,  and  the  work  was  completed  in  1775.  Besides 
the  great  change  in  the  appearance  of  the  College 
thus  brought  about,  the  plan  involved  the  closing  of 
the  great  gate  in  Emmanuel  Street,  which  had  been 
the   principal   entrance   since   the   foundation   of  the 

*  The  order  for  the  alteration  of  the  hall  is  dated  June  24,  1760. 
The  decayed  walls  were  cased  in  stone,  the  eaves  were  replaced  by 
a  stone  parapet,  the  large  outside  lantern  was  removed,  and  inside 
the  original  open  roof  was  closed  by  the  present  ceiling,  and  the 
floor  relaid  with  Ketton  stone.  The  work  cost  ;^i,8oo.  The 
present  oak  floor  was  substituted  in  1876. 


DEVELOPMENT  OF  THE  BUILDINGS     15 

College.*  But  it  was  not  until  1824  that  the  gateway 
itself  disappeared.  The  court  towards  Emmanuel  Street 
had  remained  open  (except  for  a  wall)  all  through  these 
changes,  but  in  this  year  the  present  range  of  buildings, 
called  the  'New  Court,'  was  erected,  and  in  1829  the 
kitchen  range  was  extended  as  far  as  the  back  line  of 
the  1824  building.  At  the  same  time  the  old  Bungay 
Building  of  1613  was  pulled  down,  and  the  present  front 
to  St.  Andrew's  Street  cased  and  embattled. 

Since  that  time  the  alterations  have  been  mostly  on 
the  east  side  of  the  College.  In  1873-74  the  new  Lodge 
was  built,  involving  a  slight  change  in  the  division  be- 
tween the  Master's  and  Fellows'  Garden.  Such  portions 
of  the  old  Lodge  as  remained  standing  were  converted 
into  lecture-rooms  and  chambers,  the  old  lecture-room 
to  the  west  of  the  hall  being  at  the  same  time  added  to 
the  buttery.  In  1886  the  range  of  stable  building  at 
the  east  of  the  paddock  was  demolished,  and  a  hostel 
for  students  built,  which  was  doubled  by  two  new  wings 
in  1893-94,  at  which  time  also  the  house  which  stood 
immediately  east  of  the  pond  (Emmanuel  House)  was 
pulled  down,  and  the  present  Tutor's  house  erected. 

The  object  of  this  sketch  has  been  to  point  out  the 
chief  organic  changes  by  which,  step  by  step,  the 
buildings  as  they  stood  on  the  site  purchased  by  the 
founder  have  assumed  their  present  shape  and  arrange- 
ment.    Elaborate  details  are  given  with  great  accuracy 

*  The  alterations  in  the  western  range  along  St.  Andrew's  Street 
had,  however,  been  gradual,  the  south  wing  being  pulled  down 
along  with  the  Founders'  Range  in  1719;  the  middle  wing,  with  the 
butteries,  in  1769.  The  third  wing— the  Bungay  building— as 
stated  in  the  text,  remained  till  1829  {Emmanuel  College  Magazine, 
vol.  ix.,  p.  90). 


16  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

in  Willis  and  Clark's  Architectural  History  of  the 
University  of  Cambridge  (vol.  ii.),  and  much  light  has 
been  thrown  upon  this  part  of  the  College  history  by 
the  present  Bursar,  Mr.  J.  B.  Peace,  in  his  articles  in 
the  College  magazine,  in  which  he  has  printed  large 
extracts  from  the  College  accounts  and  other  documents 
in  the  treasury.  It  is  clear  that  the  College  owes  much 
to  the  zeal  and  self-denial  of  its  former  Masters  and 
Fellows,  while  the  large  subscriptions  which  were  from 
time  to  time  raised  show  that  it  won  and  preserved  the 
affection  and  loyalty  of  its  members.  The  amenity  of 
its  grounds  also  is  greatly  owing  to  the  care  and  fore- 
sight of  the  authorities  from  the  earliest  times.  Chader- 
ton,  the  first  Master,  was  a  great  planter  of  trees,  and 
rejoiced  to  think  that  he  was  thus  serving  future  ages. 
His  example  was  followed  by  others,  and  though,  of 
course,  many  of  the  best  trees  have  disappeared  from 
time  to  time,  especially  a  famous  cedar  once  in  the 
Fellows'  Garden,  the  ultimate  result  is  that  the  Em- 
manuel grounds,  for  beauty,  variety,  and  shade,  need 
fear  comparison  with  hardly  any  College  in  Cambridge. 


CHAPTER  II 

THE  FOUNDEK  AND  THE  OBJECT  OF  HIS 
FOUNDATION 

The  recoil  from  the  reactionary  policy  of  Mary's  reign 
had  been  quickened  by  the  horror  caused  by  the 
St.  Bartholomew  massacre  and  the  cruelties  of  Alva  in 
the  Netherlands.  The  religious  question  in  England 
was  also  inextricably  mixed  with  politics  by  the  action 
of  Pius  V.  in  issuing  a  Bull  deposing  Elizabeth  (1570) 
and  by  the  presence  of  Mary  of  Scotland  (1567-1587), 
round  whom  frequent  conspiracies  against  the  Queen 
centred.  All  that  there  was  of  energy  and  earnestness 
among  Englishmen  was  drawn  into  the  quarrel,  and  in 
an  ever-increasing  degree  was  found  on  the  side  of  the 
reformed  faith.  But  quickened  life  and  roused  energies 
are  certain  to  entail  diversity  of  opinion.  The  Queen 
would  have  said  '  thus  far  and  no  farther,'  and  would 
have  confined  the  revolt  from  the  ecclesiastical  govern- 
ment of  the  past,  if  not  to  the  limits  prescribed  by  her 
father,  at  least  to  those  set  by  the  legislation  of  her 
own  time.  But  such  limitation  is  beyond  the  power  of 
Sovereigns  or  Parliaments.  Awakened  intellects  and 
consciences  will   find  divergent  outlets,  all   the   more 

17  2 


18  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

surely  when  the  influence  is  not  confined  to  extremists 
and  emotional  natures,  but  reaches  the  most  sober  and 
reasonable  of  mankind.  Thus,  putting  aside  a  return 
to  '  Papistry,'  which  to  a  large  majority  of  Englishmen 
soon  came  to  be  beyond  argument,  there  was  a  growing 
belief  that  the  salvation  of  the  country  depended  on 
the  '  godliness '  of  the  people,  and  that  this  godliness 
could  only  be  secured  by  a  nearer  approach  to  primitive 
Christianity  than  the  Church  as  then  constituted  offered 
to  her  members.  The  more  uncompromising  were  for 
leaving  the  Church,  but  the  soberer  or  more  cautious 
were  for  internal  reformation  and  for  bringing  the 
Church  nearer  to  their  ideal.  P'or  this  purpose  it  was 
necessary  before  all  to  train  a  body  of  preachers,  not 
ignorant  and  violent,  but  at  once  learned  and  zealous, 
instructed  in  all  that  scholars  should  know,  but  trained 
also  to  use  their  learning  in  the  service  of  the  reformed 
faith.  This  last  is  the  spirit  in  which  Walter  Mild  may 
resolved  to  found  Emmanuel.  He  had  always  shown 
interest  in  education : 

'He  was  one  of  the  original  governors  of  the  free 
grammar-school  established  at  Chelmsford  in  March, 
1550-51,  and  gave  an  annuity  of  52s.  to  Christ's  Hospital 
in  London  by  deed  dated  April  10,  1556.  To  Christ's 
College  he,  by  deed  dated  March  10,  1568-69,  gave  £20  a 
year,  payable  out  of  the  Manor  of  Farcet,  co.  Huntingdon, 
for  the  foundation  of  a  Greek  lectureship  and  six  scholar- 
ships, and  for  the  annual  stipend  to  a  preacher  being  a 
Fellow  of  the  College,  B.D.  or  M.A.,  and  student  in 
divinity.  In  1575  he  gave  stone  for  completing  the  tower 
of  Great  St.  Mary's  Church  in  Cambridge,  and  he  warmly 
encouraged  the  foundation  of  the  free  school  at  Middleton 
in  Lancashire '  (Cooper,  Athence,  ii.  55). 


THE  FOUNDER  AND  HIS  PURPOSE       19 

His  belief  also  in  the  importance  of  preaching  may 
be  traced  in  the  records  of  the  Privy  Council,  where 
he  frequently  appears  as  using  his  influence  to  protect 
the  *  prophesyings  ■"  now  cropping  up  in  every  direc- 
tion, often  to  the  alarm  of  the  Government — which, 
perhaps  not  without  reason,  suspected  them  of  political 
tendencies — and  under  the  frown  of  most  of  the  Bishops, 
but,  as  Mildmay  believed,  from  the  natural  and  meri- 
torious desire  of  the  people  to  hear  the  Word  of  God, 
which  they  could  not  do  from  the  ignorant  and  incom- 
petent clergy.*  He  determined  that  he  could  not  do 
better  than  help  to  guide  this  inevitable  tendency  by 
providing  that,  as  the  people  would  have  preachers,  they 
might  not  have  ignorant  and  intemperate  preachers. 

The  first  question  that  arises  is — Why  could  he  not 
have  secured  this  by  endowments  in  existing  Colleges, 
particularly  in  his  own  College  of  Christ's  ?  The  answer, 
in  the  first  place,  is  furnished  by  what  we  know  of  the 
state  of  the  University  in  the  early  part  of  the  reign 
of  Elizabeth.  A  royal  letter  of  March,  1560,  refers 
to  the  fact  that '  the  study  of  divinity  and  the  Scrip- 
tures are  at  this  present  much  decayed  within  the  Uni- 
versity of  Cambridge '  (Cooper,  Annals,  ii.  159).  This 
was  the  natural  result  of  the  frequent  and  arbitrary 
changes  as  to  ecclesiastical  supremacy,  religious  cere- 

*  See  note  in  Mullinger,  University  of  Cavibridge,  p.  183.  From 
the  report  of  the  archdeaconry  of  London  (1562)  '  it  appears  that 
some  of  the  beneficed  clergy  held  three  or  four,  and  one  five,  livings 
together ;  that  few  or  none  of  the  curates  were  graduates ;  that 
many  of  the  Vicars — nay,  and  parsons — were  non-graduates ;  that 
not  above  a  third  part  of  them  were  preachers  ;  that  as  for  their 
learning,  it  was  commonly  set  down  Latine  aliquot  verba  intelligit, 
Latine  ut  cum  que  intelligit,  Latine  pauca  intelligit.  .  .  .  Scarcely  one 
in  a  hundred  is  able  and  willing  to  preach  the  Word  of  God.' 

2—2 


20  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

monies  and  dogma,  during  the  three  previous  reigns. 
The  ablest  and  most  conscientious  men  sought  refuge 
abroad,  and  either  never  returned,  or,  if  they  did, 
returned  with  feelings  embittered  against  the  opposite 
party.  Now  that  the  tide  had  turned  in  favour  of 
Protestantism,  the  Catholics  found  Cambridge  impos- 
sible, and  English  colleges  and  seminaries  were  formed 
abroad — at  Douay,  Rome,  and  elsewhere. 

The  predominant  party  left  in  possession,  however, 
was  by  no  means  at  peace  in  itself.  Intemperate  and 
aggressive  Puritanism  made  itself  more  and  more  con- 
spicuous. In  1565  George  Wythers,  M.A.,  of  Corpus, 
preached  a  sermon  '  in  which  he  urged  the  destruction 
of  all  such  painted  windows  in  the  University  as  were  of 
a  superstitious  character.  Whereupon  followed  a  great 
destruction  of  them  and  the  danger  of  a  greater  by 
some  zealots  there  '  {ibid.,  p.  215).  But  the  most 
intense  feeling  was  caused,  by  the  question  as  to 
wearing  the  square  cap  and  surplice,  '  which  many 
scrupled,  and  chose  rather  to  leave  their  colleges  and 
studies '  (ibid.,  p.  217)  ;*  and  in  the  same  year  nearly 

*  In  the  poem  called  the  Mad  Puritan  in  Pierce's  collection  of 
English  ballads  these  particular  points  are  recalled : 

•  In  the  pure  house  of  Immanuel 
I  had  my  education, 
Where  my  friends  surmise 
I  dazzled  my  eyes 
With  the  light  of  revelation  : 
Boldly  I  preach, 
Hate  a  cross,  hate  a  surplice, 
Mitres,  copes,  and  rochets : 
Come,  hear  me  pray 
Nine  times  a  day, 
And  fill  your  head  with  crochets, ' 


THE  FOUNDER  AND  HIS  PURPOSE       21 

the  whole  of  the  Fellows  and  scholars  of  St.  John's 
came  into  chapel  without  their  surplices  and  hoods,  and 
'withal  made  some  diversity  in  the  manner  of  the 
administration  of  the  communion,  and  so  continued  to 
do'  (ibid.,  p.  219).  The  movement  against  the  surplice 
spread  to  Trinity  and  other  Colleges.  It  was,  of  course, 
only  the  outward  expression  of  an  anti-conformist 
revolt,  which  now  found  its  most  eminent  champion  in 
Thomas  Cartwright,  who  was  so  popular  a  preacher  that 
when  he  preached  at  St.  Mary's  'the  windows  were 
taken  out  to  enable  those  to  hear  him  who  could  not 
get  into  the  church,'  and  who,  as  Lady  Margaret  Pro- 
fessor, 'strongly  attacked  the  Church  establishment, 
contrasting  existing  institutions  and  practices  with 
Apostolic  times.'  His  rapid  deprivation  and  subsequent 
collision  with  the  authorities  brought  out  the  fact  that 
his  opinions  were  shared  by  many  of  the  influential 
statesmen  of  the  day,  such  as  Leicester  and  Walsing- 
ham. 

Mildmay  had  long  left  the  University  when  these 
events  occurred.  There  is  little  doubt,  however,  as  to 
the  side  on  which  his  sympathies  were  likely  to  be. 
But  though  deeply  religious,  he  was  first  of  all  a  man 
of  affairs,  who  all  his  life  had  been  a  Government 
official,  and  had  served  under  such  different  Sovereigns 
as  Henry  VIII.,  Edward  VI.,  and  Mary  I.  without 
incurring  danger  or  disgrace  from  his  religious  opinions. 
Born  about  1522,  son  of  Thomas  Mildmay,  auditor  of 
the  Court  of  Augmentations,  he  had,  after  a  short 
career  at  Christ's,  followed  his  father's  footsteps,  and 
before  the  death  of  Henry  VIII.  had  obtained  some 
subordinate  position  in  the  same  court,  and  before  1546 


22  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

had  been  appointed  one  of  its  two  Surveyors-General. 
The  various  offices  which  he  held  thenceforth,  the  com- 
missions on  which  he  served,  and  the  grants  and  manors 
which  he  received  in  payment,  are  all  set  forth  by 
Cooper  {A  thence,  ii.  51  sq,),  and  copied  thence  into 
the  Dictionary  of  National  Biography.  It  is  enough 
for  our  purpose  to  note  that  he  does  not  appear  to 
have  been  for  a  single  day  unconnected  with  some 
position  of  trust.  He  generally  held  several  at  the  same 
time,  and  in  1566  succeeded  Sir  Richard  Sackville  as 
Chancellor  of  the  Exchequer,  an  office  which  he  retained 
till  his  death  (May  31,  1589).  Such  a  man,  however 
much  he  may  have  sympathized  with  the  religious  views 
of  the  advanced  Puritans,  is  not  likely  to  have  approved 
the  breaches  of  the  law  which  brought  upon  Cartwright 
and  others  so  much  trouble  and  persecution;  he  was 
more  likely  to  have  agreed  with  Bartholomew  Clark,  of 
King''s,  who 

'  complained  what  an  impediment  these  contentions  had 
laid  in  the  way  to  all  useful  and  learned  studies.  That 
these  men  had  by  their  counsels  so  disturbed  all  things, 
that  the  time  which  was  wont  heretofore  to  be  employed 
in  good  arts  and  sciences  was  now  spent  and  consumed  in 
trivial  janglings  de  lana  caprina '  (Cooper,  Afui.,  ii.  224). 

He  was,  indeed,  careful  to  disclaim  any  breach  of  the 
law,  as  in  the  often-repeated  story  of  his  reply  to 
Elizabeth,  who  said  that  she  had  heard  that  '  he  had 
erected  a  Puritan  foundation  :** 

'  No,  madam,  far  be  it  from  me  to  countenance  any- 
thing contrary  to  your  established  laws  ;  but  I  have  set  an 
acorn  which,  when  it  becomes  an  oak,  God  alone  knows 
what  will  be  the  fruit  thereof.' 


THE  FOUNDER  AND  HIS  PURPOSE       26 

Still,  he  had  in  his  mind  to  secure  by  his  foundation 
something  that  he  did  not  think  could  be  got  at  Christ's 
or  elsewhere.  His  College  was  to  be  for  divinity 
students — at  any  rate,  as  far  as  scholars  and  Fellows 
were  concerned,  as  was  indicated  by  the  inscription  over 
the  gateway  already  quoted  (p.  6). 

'I  wish  all/  he  says  in  his  nineteenth  statute,  'to  under- 
stand, whether  Fellows,  scholars,  or  even  pensioners,  who 
are  to  be  admitted  into  the  College,  that  the  one  object 
which  I  set  before  me  in  erecting  this  College  was  to 
render  as  many  as  possible  fit  for  the  administration  of  the 
Divine  Word  and  Sacraments;  and  that  from  this  seed- 
ground  the  English  Church  might  have  those  that  she  can 
summon  to  instruct  the  people  and  undertake  the  office  of 
pastors,  which  is  a  thing  necessary  above  all  others.  There- 
fore let  Fellows  and  scholars  who  obtrude  into  the  College 
with  any  other  design  than  to  devote  themselves  to  sacred 
theology,  and  eventually  to  labour  in  preaching  the  Word, 
know  that  they  are  frustrating  my  hope  and  occupying  the 
place  of  Fellow  or  scholar  contrary  to  my  ordinance.* 

The  ultimate  aim  of  the  founder,  therefore,  was  that  I 
his  scholars  and  Fellows,  after  devoting  their  time  at 
Cambridge  to  the  study  of  theology,  should  become 
preachers  in  various  parts  of  the  country.  The  former 
of  these  objects  he  endeavoured  to  secure  by  enforcing 
residence  with  great  strictness,  and  suspending  all 
payment  of  stipend  during  absence  (except  for  causes 
to  be  approved  by  the  Master  and  a  majority  of  the 
society),  thus  preventing  them  from  occupying  the  time 
which  he  intended  to  be  devoted  to  theological  study  in 
acting  as  private  chaplains  or  tutors  or  in  similar  outside 
work.    The  second  object — that  they  should  eventually 


24  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

become  preachers — he  tried  more  effectually  to  promote 
by  a  supplementary  statute  or  injunction  given  in  1587 
{de  mora  sociorum),  which  enacted  that  a  Fellow  should 
vacate  his  Fellowship  when  of  the  standing  of  D.D. 
(ten  years  from  M.A.),  or,  if  he  actually  took  that 
degree,  at  the  end  of  a  year  after  doing  so.  He  stated 
the  object  of  this  statute  thus  : 

'We  have  founded  the  College  with  a  design  that  it 
should  be  seed-plot  of  learned  men  for  the  supply  of  the 
Church,  and  for  the  sending  forth  of  as  large  a  number  as 
possible  of  those  who  shall  instruct  the  people  in  the 
Christian  faith.  We  would  not  have  any  Fellow  suppose 
that  we  have  given  him  in  this  College  a  perpetual  abode 
— a  warning  which  we  deem  the  more  necessary  in  that  we 
have  ofttimes  been  present  when  many  experienced  and 
wise  men  have  taken  occasion  to  lament,  and  have  sup- 
ported their  complaints  by  past  and  present  instances, 
that  in  other  Colleges  a  too  protracted  stay  of  Fellows  has 
been  no  slight  bane  to  the  common  weal  and  to  the 
interests  of  the  Church  '  (Mullinger,  i.  314). 

This  object  of  the  founder  had  the  full  sympathy  of 
the  first  Master,  Chaderton.  In  a  sermon  preached  at 
St.  Paul's  Cross  in  October,  1578  (the  only  work  of 
Chaderton's  that  I  have  come  across  in  print),  he  had 
denounced  in  scathing  terms  the  idle  and  unpreaching 
clergy : 

'  Where  are  the  lippes  of  those  ministers  which  doe  pre- 
serve knowledge,  or  those  messengers  of  God,  at  whose 
mouthes  his  poore  people  shoulde  seeke  his  lawe  ?  Naye 
rather,  where  be  not  whole  swarmes  of  idle,  ignorant,  and 
ungodly  Curates  and  Readers,  who  neither  can  nor  will 
goe   before   the   deare  flocke   of  Christ   in   soundnes   of 


THE  FOUNDER  AND  HIS  PURPOSE       25 

doctrine  and  integritie  of  life  ?  .  .  .  What  shall  I  saye 
of  our  domme  dogges,*  non-residents,  and  all  those  who 
serve  raortall  and  sinfull  men,  with  symonie,  flattering 
wordes,  and  servile  obedience,  not  lawfully  to  obtaine  one 
roome  in  the  vineyard  of  the  Lorde,  but  two,  three,  foure 
or  moe  places  (which  is  unlawful),  the  which  though  they 
miscall  preferments,  yet  in  deede  they  be  so  weighty 
charges,  that  the  wysest  and  strongest  of  all  is  altogether 
insufficient  for  an  acceptable  discharge  thereof.' 

And  this  sympathy  with  the  founder's  object  con- 
tinued to  the  end.  When,  in  1627,  the  Fellows  peti- 
tioned the  King  to  abrogate  the  statute  (Sidney,  the 
only  other  College  which  had  had  a  similar  statute, 
having  already  obtained  its  abolition),  Chaderton,  who 
had  ceased  for  five  years  to  be  Master,  and  was  residing 
in  the  town,  drew  up  a  paper  addressed  to  the  Vice- 
Chancellor  and  Heads,  arguing  strongly  against  setting 
aside  the  express  design  of  the  founder : 

'  I  am  fully  persuaded,'  he  says,  '  upon  divers  speeches 
which  I  have  heard  at  severall  tymes  from  him,  that  he 
would  rather  not  have  founded  the  College,  than  have 
omitted  this  statute.  Other  statutes  were  suggested  to 
him  by  friends,  but  this  proceeded  soly  from  himself,  and 
he  took  more  time  of  deliberation  to  compose  and  sett 
downe  this  statute  than  any  other.' 

What  has  been  said  will  sufficiently  explain  why 
Mildmay  preferred  erecting  a  new  foundation  to  bestow- 

*  A  sermon  once  a  month  seems  to  have  been  the  rule  in  parishes 
where  the  new  •  prophesyings '  had  not  found  their  way.  Hall's 
Satires,  ii.  5  : 

•  Who  wants  a  Churchman  that  can  service  say, 
Read  fast  and  fair  his  monthly  homily  ?' 


26  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

ing  more  money  on  any  existing  one,  especially  as  his 
own  College  of  Christ's  (of  which  he  had  already  been 
a  benefactor)  was  at  that  time  under  a  Master  who 
had  been  elected  contrary  to  his  advice,  and  whose 
soundness  in  Calvinistic  theology,  or  at  any  rate  whose 
energy  in  promoting  it,  was  thought  doubtful.  He 
had,  too,  in  Chaderton,  as  he  thought,  ready  to  hand 
exactly  the  man  who  could  be  trusted  to  build  up 
a  new  College  by  his  prudence  and  learning,  and  to 
give  it  the  right  direction  in  theology.  This  purpose 
of  the  founder  is  the  key  to  the  early  history  of  the 
College,  which  cannot  be  understood  unless  we  in  some 
degree  seize  his  point  of  view,  and,  whether  agreeing  or 
disagreeing  with  his  opinions,  are  prepared  to  believe  in 
their  absolute  sincerity. 


CHAPTER  III 

THE  MASTEKSHIP  OF  LAUEENCE  CHADEKTON 
(1584-1622) 

Laurence  Chaderton  was  born  (about  1539)  at 
Oldham  in  Lancashire.  His  father,  Edmund  Chader- 
ton, was  in  a  good  position,  and  an  ardent  supporter  of 
the  old  faith.  After  a  youth  much  addicted  to  country 
pursuits  and  field  sports,  Laurence  conceived  a  taste  for 
learning  under  the  tuition  of  Laurence  Vaux,  then  or 
afterwards  incumbent  of  the  Collegiate  Church  of 
Manchester,  a  strong  Papist,  who  suffered  considerable 
hardships  as  a  '  recusant '  in  the  reign  of  Elizabeth.  At 
Chrisfs,  however,  which  he  entered  in  1562,  he  acquired 
a  decided  bent  in  favour  of  Calvinism,  much  to  the 
disgust  of  his  father,  who  wrote  to  him : 

'  If  you  will  renounce  the  new  sect  which  you  have 
joined,  you  may  expect  all  the  happiness  which  an  indul- 
gent father  can  secure  you :  otherwise  I  enclose  a  shilling 
in  this  letter  to  buy  a  wallet.  Go  and  beg  for  your 
living.' 

He  appears  to  have  been  thus  left  with  little  or  no 
support  from  home,  though   the   threat   of  complete 

27 


28  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

disinheritance  does  not  appear  to  have  been  carried  out. 
He  supported  himself  meanwhile  by  teaching,  and  at 
any  rate  he  was  able  to  take  his  B.A.  degree  in  1567. 
Next  year  he  was  elected  a  Fellow,  and  was  ordained 
deacon  by  the  Bishop  of  Lincoln.  In  1571  he  took 
the  M.A.  degree,  and  in  1578  the  B.D. ;  but  he  vacated 
his  Fellowship  in  1576,  apparently  by  marriage  with 
Cecilia,  daughter  of  Nicholas  Culverwell,  Queen  Eliza- 
beth's merchant  of  wines.  He  was  a  good  scholar 
in  theology,  Latin,  Greek,  and  Hebrew,  and  had 
some  knowledge  of  French,  Spanish,  and  Italian.  He 
also  lectured  in  logic.  But  he  was  best  known  as  a 
divine  and  preacher.  His  sermons  at  St.  Clemenfs 
were  much  admired  for  'a  style  of  singular  purity, 
lucidity,  and  manliness ;  a  voice  very  clear  and  pleasing 
and  of  wonderful  flexibility,  accompanied  by  a  great 
dignity  of  manner  and  propriety  of  action.'  He  appears 
usually  to  have  preached  ex  tempoi'e,  and  in  the  preface 
to  the  sermon  delivered  at  St.  Paul's  Cross,  already 
quoted,  he  explains  that  in  writing  it  down  afterwards 
he  could  not  be  sure  of  giving  the  exact  words ;  besides, 
he  warns  his  readers  that  reading  cannot  be  so  profit- 
able as  hearing : 

'  For  it  was  both  the  zeale  of  the  speaker,  the  attention 
of  the  hearer,  the  promise  of  God  to  the  ordinary  preaching 
of  his  word,  the  mighty  and  inward  working  of  his  holy 
Spirit,  and  many  other  things  which  the  Lord  worketh 
most  mercifully  by  the  preaching  of  his  glorious  Gospel, 
which  are  not  to  be  hoped  for  by  reading  the  written 
sermons  of  his  ministers.' 

He  sometimes,  however,  wrote  his  sermons,  and  his 
biographer,  Dillingham,  says   that   he   had   a   goodly 


CHADERTON'S  MASTERSHIP  29 

volume  of  the  MSS.  They  have  not  been  preserved. 
The  astonishing  appetite  for  sermons  at  this  time  is 
illustrated  by  another  anecdote  preserved  by  Dilling- 
ham : 

'  Having  once  preached  for  two  hours,  he  said  that  he 
had  tired  his  hearers'  patience  and  would  leave  off ;  upon 
which  the  whole  congregation  cried  out :  "  For  God's  sake, 
sir,  go  on !  we  beg  you,  go  on !"  He  accordingly  con- 
tinued the  thread  of  his  discourse  for  another  hour,  to  the 
great  pleasure  and  delight  of  his  hearers.* 

The  only  anecdote  of  his  undergraduate  days  is  that 
in  one  of  the  Town  and  Gown  disturbances  (in  which  he 
engaged  with  some  vigour)  he  saved  the  life  of  Ban- 
croft, afterwards  Archbishop  of  Canterbury  and  a  great 
opponent  of  Puritanism,  at  the  risk  of  losing  his  own 
right  hand.  He  thereby  laid  the  foundation  of  a  lifelong 
friendship,  which  is  said  to  account  for  the  fact  that 
Bancroft,  though  he  harassed  Puritans  and  Calvinists 
generally,  was  never  harsh  to  Chaderton  or  to  Emmanuel. 
As  a  Fellow  of  Chrisfs  he  was  an  active  tutor,  and  had 
several  eminent  pupils,  especially  the  celebrated  divine 
William  Perkins,  author  of  at  least  forty  treatises  and 
controversial  tracts.  His  position  as  a  theologian, 
though  distinctly  Calvinistic,  was  moderate  and  tolerant. 
He  felt  great  sympathy  with  the  extreme  party,  who 
were  aggrieved  by  certain  ceremonies  and  doctrines  of  the 
Church,  particularly  in  the  matter  of  the  surplice  and 
cross  in  baptism,  but  he  does  not  seem  to  have  had 
scruples  in  these  things  himself;  rather,  he  regarded 
them  as  matters  indifferent,  while  he  never  separated 
himself   from   the   discipline    of   the   Church   or  the 


80  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

authority  of  the  law.  The  only  controversy  of  a  theo- 
logical nature  in  which  we  know  him  to  have  been 
engaged  was  with  the  French  divine  Peter  Baro,  Lady 
Margaret  Professor,  in  1581,  on  the  question  which  so 
much  agitated  the  two  parties — Arminian  and  Calvinist 
— that  of  justifying  grace  (Fuller,  Hist,  of  Cambridge, 
p.  204).  But  in  this  he  set  a  good  example  to  the  contro- 
versalists  of  the  day  by  the  gentleness  of  his  manner  and 
the  moderation  of  his  language. 

Chaderton,  therefore,  was  exactly  the  man  whom  Sir 
Walter  would  naturally  select — a  Calvinist,  a  preacher, 
accomplished  in  the  three  languages  Latin,  Greek,  and 
Hebrew,  a  fairly  learned  theologian,  in  sympathy  with 
the  advanced  school  of  Puritanism  and  yet  content  to 
stay  in  the  Church  and  abide  by  the  law,  and,  above  all, 
a  man  of  great  firmness  of  character,  and  capable  of 
impressing  all  that  came  in  contact  with  him.  That  he 
commanded  a  wide  confidence  is  shown  by  the  rapidity 
with  which  the  College  filled,  and  the  way  it  maintained 
its  numbei-s  throughout  his  mastership. 

The  College  order  book  enables  us  to  get  some  view 
of  the  manner  in  which  the  earlier  society  was  employed, 
and  the  principles  on  which  they  endeavoured  under  his 
direction  to  form  the  corporate  life.  We  must  observe 
that  the  system  of  a  single  College  Tutor  had  not  then 
begun.  Each  undergraduate  had  a  Tutor,  but  it  might 
be  any  one  of  the  Fellows,  apparently  at  his  choice.  The 
disciplinary  authorities  were  the  Master  and  Dean,  the 
latter  being  appointed  year  by  year.  The  education 
was  in  the  hands  of  Lecturers — a  Head  Lecturer,  a 
Greek  Lecturer,  and  a  Lecturer  in  Logic — each  of  whom 
examined  his  own  class,  and  could  punish  the  youths 


CHADERTON'S  MASTERSHIP  31 

*  when  they  offend.'  For  religious  instruction  there  was 
a  Catechist,  who  not  only  lectured  in  the  chapel,  but 
questioned  the  students  publicly.  Even  then  the 
question  of  compulsory  Greek  seems  to  have  been 
started,  for  a  special  order  (1598-1600)  was  needed  to 
ordain  that  *  every  Scholler  under  the  degree  of  a 
Bachelour  of  Arte  shall  be  bounde  to  be  an  audi  tour  of 
the  Greeke  Lecturer,  except  it  shall  seem  goode  unto 
the  Lecturer  with  the  assent  of  the  Master  and  his 
tutor  to  exempt  any  one  particularly.'  It  also  required 
a  special  order  at  the  same  time  to  give  the  Greek 
Lecturer  the  same  authority  in  examining  them,  seeing 
exercises,  and  punishing  defaults,  as  the  Head  Lecturer 
had  'concerning  the  morning  lectures.'  Questionists  are 
to  sit  'to  be  opposed'  on  January  13  and  14  in  the 
parlour  from  1  to  3  p.m.  The  books  necessary  for  them 
to  have  read  are  '  Ramus'  Logick,  Aristotle's  Organoji, 
Ethics,  Politiques  and  Physiques,  and  if  they  can  or 
will  they  may  read  Phrigius  his  Natural  Philosophia.'' 

No  scholar  for  any  cause,  'the  stuards  only  ex- 
cepted,' is  to  go  into  the  kitchen,  and  everybody  is 
to  pay  the  steward  for  his  commons  in  advance. 
Dinner  was  at  11  a.m.,  supper  at  6.  But  the  butler 
was  to  have  the  buttery  open  from  8  to  8.30  a.m. — 
to  give,  I  suppose,  the  morning  draught — again  for  a 
few  minutes  before  3  p.m.,  and  lastly  from  7  to  8  p.m. 
The  College  gates  were  shut  at  ten,  and  a  bell  was 
rung,  after  which  it  was  an  offence  to  be  out.  Not 
only  was  attendance  at  chapel  required,  but  every 
undergraduate  and  Bachelor  had  to  attend  the  prayers 
of  his  Tutor  '  everie  night  at  eight  of  the  clock,  so 
as   everie  Tutor  may  see  him  at  that  time  and  be 


32  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

answerable  for  their  good  behaviour  at  that  hower, 
agreeably  to  the  peace  and  good  name  of  the  said 
Colledge.''  Nor  was  this  the  only  safeguard  of  discipline. 
The  undergraduates  were  expected  to  be  at  work  '  in  the 
calling '  at  all  times  in  the  day  except  certain  hours  of 
recreation,  viz.,  one  hour  after  dinner  and  after  supper — 
i.e.,  12  to  1  and  7  to  8  o'clock — '  unless  some  publique 
exercise  of  learning  or  religion  doe  require  their 
presence.'  The  superintendence  of  the  several  stair- 
cases also  was  divided  among  the  Fellows  '  to  looke  to  all 
that  goe  up  and  downe  his  stairs  to  their  lodging  and 
to  the  latrine ' ;  while  two  Fellows  had  the  special  duty  of 
looking  '  to  disorders  and  all  indecencies  committed  in 
the  Chappel  and  in  the  hall  at  all  tymes,'  two  more  '  to 
disorders  and  ill-behaviour  in  the  court  and  back  sides,' 
and,  lastly,  two  more  '  to  inquire  of  such  as  goe  often  out 
of  the  Colledge  or  tarry  long  forth  or  frequent  houses  in 
the  town';  while  Mr.  Chadwick,  Mr.  Pickering  and 
Mr.  Cock  were  especially  *  to  endeavour  to  teach  sound 
doctrine  and  confute  error,  at  other  times  than  only 
when  they  are  bound  by  statutes  of  the  University  and 
of  the  College  so  to  doe.' 

A  meeting  of  the  society  was  to  be  held  on  the  first 
Tuesday  of  every  month  to  consider  cases  of  discipline 
connected  with  the  office  of  Dean  or  Lecturer.  To  secure 
that  the  doctrines  taught  in  chapel  were  sound,  the 
Fellows  also  agreed  to  meet  at  seven  in  the  evening  of  any 
day  in  which  there  had  been  an  '  exercise.'  At  this  meet- 
ing anyone  might  express  his  dissent  from  anything  that 
had  been  said,  and  the  person  whose  doctrine  was  pro- 
nounced faulty  was  to  be  informed  of  it,  but  he  was  not 
to  be  informed  of  any  individual's  censure :  the  decision 


CHADERTON^S  MASTERSHIP  SS 

was  to  be  that  of  the  whole  body.  That  there  was  a 
danger  of  personal  collision  is  shown  by  a  later  order 
(October  2,  1595) 

'  that  no  fellow  nor  Pensioner,  upon  anie  cause  or  private 
occasion,  shall  either  in  sermon,  commonplace,  or  probleme 
in  the  Chappell,  use  any  speech  or  speeches  which  tend  to 
the  disgraceinge  of  any  particular  person,  or  noting  private 
faults  disgracefullie,  which  can  have  no  sound  spirituall 
use  of  edification,  or  at  least  not  any  good  use  without 
that  ill.  And  besides  our  agreement  we  doe  promise 
before  God  that  we  will  never  use  [nor]  intend  to  use  any 
such  in  our  exercises  hereafter. ''' 

In  spite  of  all  these  precautions  for  the  good  order 
and  moral  benefit  of  the  undergraduates,  there  is  not 
wanting  evidence  that  youthful  human  nature  was  not 
crushed  out  by  Puritan  theology.  Minor  offences  were 
no  doubt  punished  by  the  Tutors.  Graver  ones  sub- 
jected the  culprit  to  being  '  admonished  **  by  the  Master 
in  the  presence  of  the  Dean,  sometimes  assisted  by  the 
Senior  Fellow.  Of  these  admonitions  forty-four  are 
recorded  in  the  long  mastership  of  Chaderton,  an 
average  of  little  more  than  one  a  year.  A  certain 
John  Cook  heads  the  black  list,  being  *  admonished '  on 
October  25,  1586, 

'partly  for  disobedience  to  the  Master  and  unreverent 
abusing  of  the   Fellows  by  his  stout  and  stubborne  be- 

*  At  first  the  public  prayers  seem  to  have  been  in  the  hall. 
There  is  a  record  of  the  first  celebration  of  the  Communion  on 
May  26,  1588,  when  the  attendants  and  absentees  were :
