haviour,  and  partly  for  bending  a  stone  bowe  and  charging 
it  with  a  pellet  with  intent  as  he  saith  only  to  feare  Henry 
Deane,  whom  also  he  abused  with  uncharitable  words,  and 
for  other  unsemely  and  unchristian  behaviour.' 

Next  year  a  student  is  '  admonished  **  for  '  abusing  of 
his  tongue  in  calling  his  fellow  rakell  and  also  for 
abusing  of  his  hands  in  striking  of  him  divers  blowes, 
openly  provoking  him  also  to  strike  again  by  offering 
the  first  blow/  In  the  next  year  (1589)  there  are  two 
admonitions  for  '  railing,  striking,  and  fighting."*  A 
Fellow-commoner  in  1590  is  admonished  for  *  slandering 
Mr.  Richardson,  Fellow  and  Lecturer  of  the  College.' 
In  the  next  year  an  undergraduate  is  admonished  for 
'  disorderly  conduct  in  Chapel,  but  specially  for  talking, 
and  in  the  time  of  prayers,  to  the  great  disturbance  of 
the  whole  company.'  Others  seem  to  have  been  given 
to  field  sports  on  '  Hogmagog  hills  contrary  to  the 
Master's  commandment,'  and  apparently  in  spite  of  the 
owner's  wish,  one  undergraduate  being  admonished  for 

'  miscalling  and  ray  ling  upon  my  Lord  Rutland's  man,  as 
also  for  saying  that  my  lord  himself  was  but  a  chite  and 
that  he  was  maintained  by  Potthunters,  and  further  for 
challenging  my  lord's  man  to  meet  him  in  what  place  he 
would  apoynt.' 

The  offences  get  rather  woi-se  as  time  goes  on.  '  Strik- 
ing with  a  knife,'  '  drinking  in  a  suspicious  house  in 
Barnwell,'  '  being  out  after  ten  and  coming  over  the 
wall,'  'lying  out  of  College,  being  in  an  alehouse,  and 
being  in  company  with  others  who  brake  downe  glasse 
windows,'  *  drunkenness  and  spending  18^  in  ale  and 
other  liquors,'  '  being  out  of  College  after  the  gates  were 


CHADERTON'S  MASTERSHIP  35 

locked  and  going  about  the  streets,  and  for  riding  the 
horses  in  Mr.  Wolfe's  close  and  making  them  break  out 
into  the  course,'  '  being  taken  by  the  Proctor  in  a 
scandalous  house,'  'drinking  excessively  after  nine  or 
ten  in  a  chamber  next  to  the  street ' :  these  offences 
alternate  with  insigms  negUgentia  in  divinis  or  in 
matutinis  precibus,  and  neglecting  *  exercises '  in  the  hall, 
or  prayers,  lectures,  and  '  acts'  in  the  hall,  in  the  years 
between  1600  and  the  end  of  Chader ton's  mastership. 
They  rather  give  the  impression  that  discipline  was 
getting  somewhat  relaxed,  or  perhaps  that  the  under- 
graduates were  being  drawn  from  wider  and  more  various 
strata  of  society.  At  any  rate,  they  have  a  familiar 
ring,  and  link  the  generations  together,  if  not  with 
'  natural  piety,'  yet  at  least  with  natural  sympathy. 

In  the  internal  management  of  the  College  on  the 
ecclesiastical  side  there  was  from  the  first  considerable 
variation  from  the  customs  of  other  Colleges,  which 
must,  at  any  rate,  have  been  permitted  by  Chaderton. 

'  All  other  CoUedges  in  Cambridge  do  strictly  observe, 
according  to  the  laws  and  ordinances  of  the  Church  of 
Englande,  the  form  of  public  prayer  prescribed  in  the 
Communion  Booke.  In  Emm.  Colledge  they  do  follow  a 
private  course  of  public  prayer,  after  their  own  fashion, 
both  Sondaies,  Holy  daies,  and  workie  daies '  (Mullinger, 
p.  313,  from  Baker  MSS.,  vi.  85,  86). 

Again,  at  the  Hampton  Court  Conference  in  1604, 

'  Mr.  Chaderton  was  told  of  sitting  Communions  in 
Emmanuel  College ;  which  hee  said  was  so  by  reason  of 
the  seats  so  placed  as  they  be  ;  yet  that  they  had  some 
kneeling  also'  (Barlow,  p.  105). 

3—2 


36  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

This  state  of  things  continued  in  the  College,  and  in 
1636  a  strong  report  was  sent  to  Laud  : 

'Their  chapel  is  not  consecrate.  At  surplice  prayers 
they  sing  nothing  but  riming  Psalms  of  their  own  appoint- 
ment instead  of  y^  Hymnes  between  the  Lessons.  And 
Lessons  they  read  not  after  y^  order  appointed  in  y^ 
Callendar,  but  after  another  continued  course  of  their 
owne.  All  service  is  there  done  and  performed  (psalmes 
and  hymnes  and  all  if  they  read  any)  by  the  minister 
alone.  The  students  are  not  brought  up,  nor  accustomed 
to  answere  any  verse  at  all.  Before  prayers  begin  the 
Boyes  come  in  and  sitt  downe  and  put  on  and  talke  aloude 
of  what  they  list.  Their  seates  are  placed  round  about 
and  above  the  Communion  Table.  When  they  preach  or 
commonplace  they  omit  all  service  after  y^  first  or  second 
lesson  at  the  furthest.' 

How  far  these  irregularities  were  from  being  contrary 
to  the  opinions  of  a  large  part  of  the  country  is  shown 
by  the  steadiness  with  which  the  numbers  kept  up 
throughout  Chaderton's  mastership.  As  all  students 
had  to  live  within  the  College  walls,  there  was  naturally 
some  variation.  In  the  first  two  years,  or  rather  year 
and  a  half,  there  were  seventy-six  admissions ;  in  the 
next  (1586),  twenty ;  after  that  they  vary  from  twenty- 
seven  to  forty-two  till  1611,  when  they  suddenly  go  up 
to  fifty-eight,  and  from  that  year  to  the  last  of  Chader- 
ton's  mastership  there  is  a  very  high  average,  cul- 
minating in  his  last  year  (1622)  in  the  largest  entry  of 
all — seventy-nine. 

The  personal  influence  of  Chaderton  seems  to  have 
defended  the  College  from  the  frowns  of  royalty,  so 
long,  at  any  rate,  as  James  I.   was  King.     He  came 


CHADERTONS  MASTERSHIP  37 

to  Cambridge  in  1615  (March  7-10),  accompanied  by 
Prince  Charles,  and,  among  other  things,  visited  Em- 
manuel. 

'  Someone/  says  Dillingham,  '  pointed  out  to  him  that 
the  chapel  was  far  out  of  the  eastward  position  ;  where- 
upon Chaderton  remarked  to  the  King  that  he  had  been 
told  that  the  same  was  true  of  the  royal  chapel  at  White- 
hall. The  King  answered  ;  "  God  will  not  turn  away  his 
face  from  the  prayers  of  any  holy  and  pious  man,  to  what- 
ever region  of  heaven  he  directs  his  eyes.  So,  doctor,  I 
beg  you  to  pray  for  me."  * 

If  we  may  trust  a  verse  of  Corbet's  poem  on  King 
James's  visit,  he  was  not  spared  a  service  in  the  chapel. 
After  describing  how  the  other  Colleges  were  painted 
and  adorned  for  the  visit,  he  goes  on  : 

'  But  the  pure  house  of  Emmanuel 
Would  not  be  like  proud  Jezebel, 
Nor  show  herself  before  the  King 
An  hypocrite,  or  painted  thing ; 
But,  that  the  ways  might  all  prove  fair, 
Conceiv'd  a  tedious  mile  of  prayer.* 

The  title  of  '  Doctor '  which  the  King  gives  Chader- 
ton he  had  abstained  from  acquiring  till  two  years 
before,  though  the  founder  had  ordered  that  the 
Master  should  be  a  Doctor  of  Divinity.  But  on  the 
visit  of  Prince  Charles  and  his  brother-in-law,  Frederic, 
Count  Palatine  (the  'winter  King'),  on  March  3-4, 1612, 
he  consented  to  take  the  degree  at  the  special  com- 
mencement held  in  honour  of  the  Princes,  according  to 
Dillingham,  on  the  express  wish  of  the  King,  conveyed 
by  Prince  Charles.    On  which  occasion  he  is  said  to  have 


38  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

so  charmed  the  august  visitors  by  his  conversation  that 
Frederic  could  not  refrain  from  taking  his  hand  and 
leading  him  publicly  through  the  University  with  every 
mark  of  respect.  King  James  had  also  nominated  him 
on  the  first  Cambridge  Committee  for  Revising  the 
English  Version  of  the  Bible. 

The  men  who  formed  the  earliest  society,  no  less 
than  the  first  Master,  seem  to  have  answered  the 
intention  of  the  founder,  both  as  to  their  pursuits  in 
the  University  and  in  their  subsequent  careers  as 
preachers.  Of  the  three  original  Fellows  —  Charles 
Chadwick  (from  Christ's),  William  Jones  and  Laurence 
Pickering  (from  Clare) — the  last-named  seems  to  have 
taken  the  chief  part  in  the  government  of  the  College, 
his  name  occurring  as  Dean  at  the  foot  of  'admonitions' 
at  intervals  up  to  1589.  His  place  was  then  taken  by 
John  Cock  and  John  Richardson  successively,  who  were 
two  of  the  six  Fellows  elected  within  a  year  of  the  founda- 
tion. Charles  Chadwick,  one  of  the  original  three,  was 
Dean  in  1587.  He  was  a  member  of  an  old  Lancashire 
family  (which  may  have  caused  him  to  be  recommended 
by  Chaderton),  and  he,  with  the  other  two,  seems  before 
long  to  have  accepted  Church  preferment.  Chadwick 
was  Rector  of  Woodham  Ferrers  in  Essex ;  Jones,  of  East 
Bergholt*  (1591-1636) ;  and  Pickering — probably  one  of 
the  Northamptonshire  Pickerings,  the  head  of  which.  Sir 
Gilbert  Pickering,  was  afterwards  a  great  Parliamentary 
leader — is  vaguely  said  to  have  had  a  benefice  '  in  the 
West.'  Of  the  six  Fellows  elected  next  after  the  three 
original  members  of  the  society,  Nathaniel  Gilbie  was 
the  son  of  one  of  the  most  violent  of  the  early  Puritans, 

*  The  advowson  of  this  living  was  acquired  by  the  College  in 
X840. 


EARLY  MEMBERS  OF  THE  COLLEGE    39 

Anthony  Gilbie,  who,  after  an  exile  at  Frankfort  and 
Geneva  during  Mary's  reign,  returned  to  England  in 
that  of  Elizabeth,  and  became  Vicar  of  Ashby-de-la- 
Zouch.  Gilbie  was  Dean  in  1592,  and  afterwards  a 
preacher  at  Bedford.  His  most  celebrated  pupil  was 
Joseph  Hall.  Of  the  other  six,  William  Bright  (from 
Christ's)  became  a  preacher  at  Shrewsbury,  John  Grey 
(from  Queens')  in  London.  Of  John  Cock's  career — 
Dean  in  1590 — we  have  no  information ;  but  two  others, 
Branthwaite  and  Richardson,  remained  in  Cambridge. 
Having  been  elected  before  the  supplementary  statute 
of  1587,  they  obtained  dispensation  from  the  necessity 
of  vacating  their  Fellowships  at  the  standing  of  D.D.* 
William  Branthwaite  (from  Clare)  proceeded  Doctor  of 
Divinity  in  1598,  was  one  of  the  Revisers  of  the  Bible 
in  1607-1611,  his  special  part  being  the  Apocrypha. 
He  was  elected  Master  of  Caius  in  1607,  and  died  as 
Vice-Chancel  lor  in  1619.  He  founded  two  exhibitions 
at  Emmanuel,  and  left  £^0  to  the  library  and  some 
plate  to  the  College.  John  Richardson  (also  from 
Clare)  graduated  in  1581,  and  took  his  M.A.  degree 
the  year  after  his  election  at  Emmanuel.  In  1607  he 
was  appointed  Regius  Professor  of  Divinity,  in  1609 
Master  of  Peterhouse,  and  in  1615  Master  of  Trinity, 
though,  according  to  Fuller,  he  only  two  months  before 
had  a  somewhat  curious  encounter  with  James  I.  The 
King,  among  other  things  in  his  visit  to  Cambridge, 
was  present  at  a  special  Act  of  Divinity  in  which 
Richardson  as  opposer  argued  in  favour  of  the  excom- 

*  Thus,  the  petitioners  against  the  statute  in  1627  allege  that  it

munication  of  Kings,  instancing  St.  Ambrose  and  the 
Emperor  Theodosius. 

'  Upon  which  the  King  with  some  passion  returned  : 
"  Profecto  fuit  hoc  Ambrosio  insolentissime  factum  !"  To 
which  Richardson  rejoined :  "  Responsum  ver6  regium  et 
Alexandro  dignum  !  hoc  non  est  argumenta  dissolvere  sed 
dissecare."  And  so,  sitting  down,  desisted  from  further 
dispute/ 

On  his  death  (April  20, 1625)  he  left  all  his  folio  and 
quarto  books  to  Emmanuel.  He  was  one  of  the  earliest 
divines  at  Cambridge  to  support  the  Arminian  doc- 
trines as  to  predestination  against  those  of  Calvin,  in 
which,  probably,  he  was  opposed  to  most  of  the  members 
of  his  College. 

Three  other  early  members  of  the  foundation  may 
here  be  mentioned  who  were  the  immediate  products  of 
the  life  of  the  place  as  formed  by  these  men.  William 
Bedell  was  entered  in  the  first  year  of  the  College 
(1584),  was  elected  scholar  in  the  same  year,  and 
a  Fellow  in  1593,  having  come  from  Essex,  one  of  the 
two  counties  (the  other  being  Northamptonshire)  to 
which  a  preference  was  given  by  the  founder.  At  the 
same  election  James  Waddesworth  was  elected  scholar, 
but,  apparently  from  want  of  means,  did  not  come  into 
residence  till  the  next  year,  when  he  obtained  a  sizar- 
ship.  In  the  year  of  Waddesworth^s  B.A.  degree  (1589) 
Joseph  Hall  entered,  and  was  elected  a  scholar  in  1591, 
and  Fellow  in  1595.  These  three  men  (all  remarkable 
in  their  way)  had  careers  which  thus  began  alike,  but 
ended  very  differently.  They  must  have  been  well 
acquainted.     Bedell  says,  in  his  dedication  of  Copies  of 


EARLY  MEMBERS  OF  THE  COLLEGE     41 

Certain  Letters,  that  he  and  Waddesworth  were  '  chosen 
scholars  in  the  same  election,  lodged  in  the  same 
chamber,  and  after  ministers  in  the  same  Diocese/ 
Though  they  were  friends,  they  had  not  all  the  same 
tastes  in  early  life.  Bedell  stayed  in  the  College  till  near 
the  time  of  his  Doctor's  standing,  acting  as  Catechetical 
Lecturer  and  Bursar.  He  was  a  hard  student,  and 
made  himself  well  known  for  his  mastery  of  Latin, 
Greek,  Hebrew,  and  Arabic ;  and,  though  a  firm  Church- 
man and  not  averse  to  decent  forms  and  ceremonies — 
for  instance,  in  none  of  his  churches  did  he  ever  allow 
the  Communion-table  to  be  brought  down  into  the  body 
of  the  church — was  yet  in  general  sympathy  with  the 
Calvinistic  teaching  of  the  majority  of  his  contem- 
poraries at  Cambridge,  and  was  much  influenced  by 
Chaderton,  of  whom  he  always  speaks  in  his  letters 
with  reverence  and  affection. 

Waddesworth,  who  remained  a  much  shorter  time  in 
the  College,  and  was  instituted  to  the  rectory  of  Pake- 
field  in  Suffolk  in  1598,  having  doubtless  been  engaged 
in  clerical  work  before,  seems  to  have  taken  quite  a 
different  turn  in  theology.  According  to  his  son,  he 
really  became  a  Roman  Catholic  in  1604 ;  but  at  any 
rate  in  the  next  year,  on  going  to  Spain  as  chaplain 
to  Sir  Charles  Corn wal lis,  the  Ambassador,  he  openly 
abjured  the  English  Church,  and  became  an  officer  in 
the  Inquisition  and  English  tutor  to  the  Infanta,  and 
died  in  Madrid  in  November,  1623.  In  a  correspondence 
between  him.  Bedell,  and  Hall  on  this  subject,  whilst 
expressing  warm  affection  for  Bedell  and  gratitude  for 
the  charitable  nature  of  his  letters,  he  complains  of  the 
bitterness  of  Hall, '  whom  I  esteemed  either  my  friend 


42  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

or  a  modester  man  ;  whose  flaunting  epistle  I  have  not 
answered,  because  I  could  not  soil  my  hands  with  a 
poetical  railer.'  Hall,  in  fact,  while  he  stayed  in 
College,  as  he  did  till  1600,  does  not  appear  to  have 
given  his  chief  attention  to  theology.  He  was  rather 
devoted  to  literature,  especially  to  poetry,  and  above 
all  to  satire.  The  three  books  of  Toothless  Satires 
were  published  in  1597,  and  the  three  of  Biting'  Satires 
in  1598.  Other  poems  of  his  were  produced  about  the 
same  time.  He  indeed  '  passed  all  his  degrees  with 
great  applause  "*  (Fuller),  and  gained  credit  also  for  the 
way  in  which  he  read  the  '  public  rhetoric  lecture ' ;  but 
he  did  not  take  Orders  till  just  before  he  left  College, 
and  it  was  not  till  a  later  period  in  his  life  that  he 
began  writing  the  controversial  and  devotional  works 
for  which  he  is  best  known.  His  last  book  of  verse  was 
a  congratulatory  volume  on  James  I.'s  accession — The 
King's  Prophecy,  or  Weeping  Joy  (1603).  He  became 
Vicar  of  Halstead  in  Essex  in  1601 ;  Incumbent  of 
Waltham,  Essex,  in  1608 ;  was  sent  by  James  to  the 
Synod  of  Dort  (1619) ;  and  was  offered  various  Church 
preferments  by  the  King,  ultimately  becoming  Bishop 
of  Exeter  (1627),  and  of  Norwich  (1641).  From  this 
last  he  was  ejected  in  1643,  and  died  at  Higham, 
September  8,  1656. 

Bedell  left  the  College  in  1602  for  St.  MaryX  Bury 
St.  Edmunds.  In  1607  he  went  to  Venice  as  chaplain 
to  Sir  Henry  Wooton,  returned  to  Bury  in  1610,  and 
in  1616  removed  to  the  parish  of  Horningsheath.  In 
1626  he  was  appointed  Provost  of  Trinity  College, 
Dublin,  and  in  1629  Bishop  of  Kilmore  and  Ardagh. 
The  latter  he  resigned,  and  remained  Bishop  of  Kilmore 


JOSEPH  HALL  AND  BEDELL  43 

till  his  death,  February  7,  1642,  having  suffered  con- 
siderable hardship  and  a  short  imprisonment  in  the  Irish 
Rebellion.  In  all  these  positions  Bedell  showed  a 
sturdy  honesty  and  a  determination  to  resist  oppression 
and  correct  abuses,  which  brought  him  into  many  con- 
troversies and  even  legal  proceedings,  as  a  country 
clergyman  no  less  than  as  Provost  of  Trinity  and 
Bishop.  But  his  gentleness  and  purity  of  motive  pre- 
vented these  controversies,  especially  when  they  were 
on  points  of  theology,  from  making  him  personal 
enemies,  and  a  Roman  Catholic  priest  was  heard  to 
exclaim  over  his  grave,  *0h  sit  anima  mea  cum  Be- 
dello  l'  He  was  not  a  prolific  author.  A  poem  on  the 
Powder  Plot,  an  Examination  of  Certain  Motives  of 
ReciLsancy,  several  Latin  translations  of  the  Italian 
works  of  Paolo  Sarpi,  A  Copie  of  Certain  Letters  that 
have  passed  hetxveen  Spain  and  England,  with  coiTe- 
spondence  with  Ward,  Laud,  and  others,  and  a  treatise 
On  the  Efficiency  of  Grace  (both  printed  for  the  first  time 
in  1903),  make  up  almost  all  that  has  survived  ;  though 
when  he  was  in  Venice  he  is  said  to  have  written  an 
English  Grammar,  and  to  have  promoted  the  translation 
of  the  English  Prayer-Book  into  Italian,  as  in  Ireland 
he  pushed  forward  the  translation  of  the  Bible  and 
Prayer-Book  into  Irish. 

On  the  other  hand.  Hall  was  a  voluminous  writer. 
Controversial  tracts  (as  those  which  brought  on  and  con- 
tinued the  Smectymnuus  quarrel),sermons,  epistles,  con- 
templations, paraphrases,  every  kind  of  devotional  and 
expository  work,  fill  up  twelve  goodly  octavo  volumes ; 
and  he  had  the  distinction  in  the  course  of  the  Smec- 
tymnuus controversy  of  drawing  upon  himself  a  ferocious 


44  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

attack  by  Milton  in  his  An  Apology  for  Smectymnuns. 
His  own  tone  in  controversy  is  moderate,  except  in  the 
case  of  Roman  Catholics. 

Another  Fellow  of  Emmanuel  in  these  early  days 
who  was  intimate  with  Bedell,  and  must  also  have 
known  Hall  well,  was  Samuel  Ward,  elected  from 
Christ's  in  1595.  He  remained  in  the  College  till  1599, 
when  he  became  Fellow  of  Sidney,  of  which  College  he 
was  afterwards  Master  from  1609  to  1643,  having  also 
been  Lady  Margaret  Professor  of  Divinity  (1622),  and, 
like  Hall,  one  of  the  delegates  at  Dort,  while,  like 
Chaderton,  Richardson,  and  Branthwaite,  he  was  one  of 
the  Revisers  of  the  Bible  of  1611.  Bedell  kept  up  an 
affectionate  correspondence  with  him  till  the  last  year 
of  his  life,  though  he  appears  to  have  differed  from  him 
to  some  degree  in  the  points  of  controversy  between  the 
Calvinists  and  Arminians.  Although  a  strong  and  rather 
narrow  Calvinist,  he  yet  was  a  firm  Churchman,  and 
refusing  to  take  the  Covenant  in  1643,  was  for  a  time  im- 
prisoned in  St.  John's,  but  presently  returned  to  Sidney, 
where  he  died  in  September  of  that  year.  He  had  long 
been  a  leading  man  in  University  business. 

This  little  group  of  men  must  have  given  a  high 
intellectual  tone  to  the  new  College,  and  have  raised  its 
reputation  in  the  University.  Yet  for  some  reason  the 
grace  of  the  Senate  (July  8,  1586)  appointing  what  we 
should  now  call  a  syndicate  to  arrange  the  admission 
of  Emmanuel  College  into  the  rota  for  appointing 
'  proctors,  taxors,  and  scrutators,'  did  not  have  the 
proposed  effect,  and  this  privilege  was  only  granted  in 
1650,  by  '  the  Committee  for  the  Reformation  of  the 
University '   (Cooper,   Ann.^   ii.    424,   iii.    434).      The 


NEW  ENGLAND  SETTLERS  4^ 

reason  of  this  delay  is  not  known  for  certain,  but  it  has 
generally  been  supposed  to  be  in  some  way  connected 
with  the  peculiarity  in  the  ecclesiastical  practices  of  the 
College.  An  equally  probable  reason  may  be  the 
small ness  of  its  endowment,  which  for  some  years  may 
well  have  made  its  ultimate  success  appear  doubtful. 
Its  numbers  indeed  were  large  and  constantly  growing, 
but  they  were  predominately  of  one  party,  not  by  any 
means  pleasing  to  a  large  section  of  the  University  or 
the  hierarchy.  One  striking  proof  of  this  is  the  number 
of  Emmanuel  men  (some  of  them  Fellows)  who  found 
their  way  eventually  to  New  England,  either  to  avoid 
prosecution  for  nonconformity  or  in  search  of  more 
congenial  surroundings.  This  forms  one  of  the  most 
striking  features  in  the  early  history  of  the  College,  and 
deserves  to  be  treated  in  some  detail. 

Among  the  first  was  John  Cotton,  elected  Fellow 
about  1608,  having  been  previously  elected  to  a  Fellow- 
ship at  Trinity.  He  stayed  long  enough  to  be  Catechist, 
Head  Lecturer,  and  Dean.  Becoming  '  converted ,''  he 
disappointed  his  hearers  at  St.  Mary's  in  the  expectation 
which  they  had  entertained  of  a  brilliant  and  learned 
discourse,  who 

'  discovered  their  vexation  by  their  not  Humming,  as 
according  to  their  sinful  and  absurd  custom  they  had  for- 
merly done,  and  the  Vice-Chancellor  for  the  very  same 
reason  graced  him  not  as  he  did  others  that  pleased 
him.' 

This  plain  sermon  was  the  signal  of  a  change  of  life, 
which  brought  much  trouble  upon  him  in  his  ministry 
at   Boston,   where  he    long   had  a  kind   of  informal 


46  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

divinity  school,  much  frequented  by  intending  ministers. 
Eventually,  to  avoid  the  tender  mercies  of  the  High 
Commission  Court,  he  set  sail  for  New  England,  which 
he  reached  after  a  seven  weeks'  voyage,  landing  at 
New  Boston,  September  3-4, 1633.  There  he  remained 
till  his  death  (December  23,  1652),  having  been  long 
the  leading  divine  in  the  colony  and  the  founder  of 
the  Free  Church, 

Thomas  Hooker,  a  Dixie  Fellow  (1607),  also  passed 
through  a  '  storm  of  soul  "*  while  Fellow  and  Catechist. 
He  afterwards  ministered  at  Chelmsford,  where  his  non- 
conformity brought  him  into  trouble  similar  to  that  of 
Cotton,  being  summoned  before  the  Court  of  High 
Commission  in  1630.  Accordingly,  after  two  years 
spent  as  a  minister  at  Rotterdam  and  a  brief  return  to 
England,  he  accompanied  Cotton  and  Stone  to  New 
England  in  1633.  He  was  the  Luther,  says  Mather,  as 
Cotton  was  the  Melancthon,  of  New  England.  He  died 
July  7,  1647. 

Another  emigrant  was  Francis  Higginson,  who  entered 
at  Emmanuel  in  1622,  and  afterwards  had  a  church  in 
Leicester,  where,  after  some  years  of  '  conformity,'  he 
eventually  lost  his  benefice  by  becoming  a  conscientious 
nonconformist.  He  continued,  however,  to  preach  for 
other  beneficed  clergy.  Bishop  Williams  of  Lincoln 
(1621-1641)  apparently  conniving  at  his  so  doing.  But 
'  when  the  hour  and  power  of  darkness  was  come  from 
Lambeth,'  he  joined  a  number  of  Puritan  emigrants  in 
1629,  invited  by  the  Governor  and  Company  of  Massa- 
chusetts Bay.  He  arrived  at  Salem  Harbour  June  24 
in  that  year,  and  with  others  set  up  a  Reformed  Church 
there,  but  died  in  August  of  the  next  year. 


NEW  ENGLAND  SETTLERS  47 

Thomas  Shepherd  (born  November  5,  1605)  was  two 
years  a  pupil  of  Cockerel  (who  went  to  North  Cadbury 
in  1622).  At  Emmanuel  'the  ministry  of  Mr.  Chader- 
ton  and  Mr.  Dickinson  struck  his  heart.**  He  became  a 
minister  or  '  lecturer '  at  Earls  Coin  in  Essex.  Being 
silenced  by  Laud,  he  became  chaplain  to  Sir  R.  Darling 
in  Yorkshire.  But  he  had  taken  up  the  role  of  exorcist 
both  in  Essex  and  Yorkshire,  and  was  quickly  silenced 
by  the  Bishop  of  Durham  (Moreton).  He  accordingly 
escaped,  and  in  the  autumn  of  1634  took  ship — on  a 
leaky  vessel  called  the  Defence — and  with  some  difficulty 
landed  in  New  England  on  October  3,  and  was '  ordained  ' 
to  the  church  at  Cambridge.  He  was  the  author  of 
many  sermons,  letters,  and  tracts,  the  title  of  one  of 
which  will  show  his  point  of  view.  It  was  printed  in 
London  in  1645,  and  is  called  New  England's  Lamenta- 
ti07is  for  Old  England's  Errors,  Another,  called  The 
Sincere  Convert^  'caus'd  much  Trouble  in  Gracious 
Christians.'     He  died  August  25,  1649. 

Nathaniel  Rogers,  son  of  a  famous  Puritan  minister 
of  Dedham  (1605-1630),  entered  Emmanuel  as  a  sizar, 
May  9,  1614,  and  afterwards  held  several  preferments 
in  England.  In  1636  (June  1)  he  sailed  for  New 
England,  in  order  to  avoid  the  ecclesiastical  censures 
which  he  foresaw,  and  was  '  ordained '  pastor  of  Ipswich, 
Massachusetts  (1638),  where  he  died  July  3,  1655. 

Samuel  Stone  entered  Emmanuel  in  1620,  and  took 
his  M.A.  degree  in  1630 ;  he  therefore  was  only  two 
years  under  Chaderton.  In  the  latter  year  he  went  as  a 
'  lecturer '  to  Towchester,  but,  finding  his  nonconformity 
likely  to  bring  trouble,  he  went  to  New  England  with 
Cotton  and  Hooker,  where  he  arrived   September  4, 


48  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

1633.  He  preached  first  at  Cambridge,  and  afterwards 
at  a  new  settlement  called  Hartford,  after  his  English 
birthplace,  where  he  died  July  20,  1663.  He  did  not 
find  complete  peace  in  America,  either,  for  a  difference 
with  a  church  elder  led  to  a  serious  schism.  By  A 
Discourse  about  the  Logical  Notion  of  a  Congregational 
Church  it  was  '  thought  by  some  that,  as  a  Stone  from 
the  sling  of  David,  he  has  mortally  wounded  that 
Goliath — a  national  political  Church,'' 

Samuel  Whiting,  born  November  20,  1597,  was  an 
intimate  friend  and  '  chamber -mate  '  of  Anthony 
Tuckney  (Fellow  1619,  Master  1645-1653).  At 
College  he  was  influenced  by  the  teaching  of  his  Tutor, 
Yates  (Fellow  1607).  He  entered  in  1613,  and  re- 
mained till  his  M.A.  degree  in  1620.  He  ministered 
first  at  Lynn,  Norfolk  ;  but  being  cited  before  the  High 
Commission  Court  for  nonconformity,  at  the  instance 
of  the  Bishop  of  Norwich  (Harsnett),  in  the  year  of  the 
death  of  James  I.  (1624),  he  removed  to  Skirbich  in 
Lincolnshire.  After  some  years  there,  being  again 
brought  into  trouble  for  nonconformity,  he  sold  his 
property,  and  took  ship  in  April,  1636,  for  Boston  in 
New  England,  from  which  he  went  to  Lynn,  where  he 
acted  as  minister  till  his  death  in  1659.* 

Not  only  did  Chaderton  encourage  the  sort  of  men 
above  described,  and  when  possible  secure  them  as 
Fellows :  he  took  pains  also  to  promote  them  elsewhere, 
when  election  was  rendered  impossible  by  the  rule  against 
having  more  than  one  Fellow  from  a  single  county. 
For   instance,   William    Bradshaw,    who   remained    in 

*  See  list  of  Emmanuel  men  in  New  England  at  the  end  of  the 
chapter. 


THE  BOOK  OF  DISCIPLINE  49 

College  till  his  M.A.  degree  (1595),  but,  being  of  the 
same  county  as  his  contemporary,  Joseph  Hall — who 
was  preferred  to  him — could  not  be  a  Fellow,  was  not 
only  recommended  by  Chaderton  to  a  tutorship  in 
Guernsey  and  a  lectureship  at  Chatham,  but  was  by  the 
same  influence  elected  one  of  the  first  Fellows  at  Sidney. 
This,  however,  was  nearly  proving  fatal  to  him,  for  he 
was  all  but  drowned  in  Harston  Millpool  on  his  way 
from  London  to  Cambridge.  He  experienced  the  usual 
difficulties  of  nonconforming  divines,  but  he  survived  them 
all,  and  managed  to  write  nearly  thirty  treatises  or 
pamphlets  in  support  of  the  Puritan  position  before  his 
death  at  Chelsea  in  1618.  He  was  principally  known 
as  the  supporter  of  the  'congregational'  theory — the 
liberty  of  each  congregation  to  regulate  church-mem- 
bership and  ceremonies.  Personally  he  was  very 
attractive,  and  is  described  by  his  contemporary.  Hall, 
as  '  very  strong  and  eager  in  argument,  hearty  in  friend- 
ship, regardless  of  the  world,  a  despiser  of  compliment, 
a  lover  of  reality."* 

That  Chaderton,  in  spite  of  general  conformity,  was 
in  hearty  sympathy  with  the  most  advanced  Puritans  is 
also  shown  by  the  fact  that  his  name  is  found  among 
those  who  in  1586  declared  their  approbation  of  the 
Book  of  Discipline^  originally  printed  in  Latin  at 
Geneva  in  1574.  It  had  since  been  revised  and  trans- 
lated into  English.  This  translation  was  printed  by 
the  Cambridge  Press  in  1584,  with  a  preface  by  Cart- 
wright,  but  was  seized  by  order  of  Archbishop  Whitgiffc, 
and  the  whole  impression  was  burnt.  It  was  not  printed 
again  till  1644,  from  a  single  copy  found  among  Cart- 
wright's  books  at  his  death  (1603).     But  in  spite  of 

4 


50  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

these  precautions  its  substance  was  still  well  known,  and 
a  declaration  in  its  favour  was  signed  in  158()  by  more 
that  500  beneficed  clergymen  (NeaPs  Puritans^  vol.  i., 
pp.  301,  324,  816).  In  1591  again  Chaderton  joined 
Good,  Whitaker,  and  Barwell  in  petitioning  the  Lord 
Treasurer  '  to  be  favourable  to  the  true  and  peaceable 
preachers  of  the  Gospel,"*  and  in  1595  signed  a  letter  to 
Cecil  denouncing  the  doctrines  of  Baro  as  '  ropish,' 
though  they  were  merely  opposed  to  the  extreme 
Calvinistic  doctrines  as  to  predestination  (Neal,  Puritans, 
vol.  i.,  p.  389).  In  January,  1604,  with  Reynolds, 
Sparks,  and  Knewstubs,  he  was  selected  to  represent 
the  Puritans  at  the  Hampton  Court  Conference.  The 
Puritan  deputies  wei*e  not  thought  to  have  shown  much 
courage  or  address  in  that  business.  But  Chaderton 
does  not  seem  to  have  lost  the  confidence  of  his  party. 
The  College  continued  to  increase,  and  to  be  filled  as  a 
rule  with  the  sort  of  men  for  whom  the  founder  had 
designed  it.  Sir  Walter  seems  to  have  had  special  sym- 
pathy with  the  feelings  which  two  yeare  after  the  founda- 
tion of  the  College  found  expression  in  the  Puritan 
petition  to  Parliament,  complaining  of  the  absence  of 
learned  ministers  and  such  as  were  able  to  preach : 

'  Whereas  the  Scriptures  say  that  ministers  of  the  Gospel 
should  be  such  as  are  able  to  teach  sound  doctrine  and 
convince  gainsayers,  yet  the  bishops  have  made  priests  of 
the  basest  of  the  people,  not  only  for  their  occupations  and 
trades  whence  they  have  taken  them,  as  shoemakers,  barbers, 
taylors,  water-bearers,  shepherds,  and  horse-keepers,  but  also 
for  their  want  of  good  learning  and  honesty.' 

He  had  been  the  member  of  the  Privy  Council  to  whom 
complaints   were    naturally    addressed    by   prosecuted 


ST.  NICHOLAS^  HOSTEL  51 

ministers,  and  there  are  several  instances  of  his  joining 
other  members  of  the  Council  in  attempting  to  shield 
them  from  episcopal  severities.  Chaderton,  therefore, 
had  so  administered  the  new  foundation  as  to  train  up 
the  kind  of  ministers  whose  learning  Sir  Walter 
believed  would  prevent  the  difficulties  produced  by 
intemperate  and  uninstructed  zeal,  while  it  would 
spread  and  defend  the  more  rational  and  fundamental 
truths  of  Calvinism. 

It  was  not  only  ministers,  however,  or  men  of  one 
view  in  politics  and  religion,  that  were  produced  by 
Chaderton.  He  seems  not  long  after  moving  into 
College  to  have  found  the  'Master's  lodgings'*  incon- 
venient, and  he  therefore  purchased  a  house  on  the  site 
of  what  had  once  been  the  Hostel  of  St.  Nicolas,  on  the 
east  side  of  Preachers'*  Street,  between  Emmanuel  and 
Christ's.  It  was  one  of  the  many  hostels  which  either 
disappeared  on  the  rise  of  Colleges  or  were  absorbed  by 
them,  and  Fuller  tells  us  that  in  its  hall  were  given  the 
first  Greek  lectures  delivered  in  Cambridge.  The 
College  had  acquired  it  in  1585,  but  in  1595  conveyed 
it  to  two  persons  who  immediately  afterwards  conveyed 
it  to  Chaderton,  who  would  not  have  been  legally  able 
to  purchase  it  direct  from  the  College.*    He  reconveyed 

*  This  house  was  sold  by  Queens'  College  to  Jo.  Mere  for  /40, 
May  I,  1568;  by  him  to  R.  Standish,  LL.D.,  for  ^43,  July  31, 
1549 ;  by  him  to  Adrian  Scroop  and  Simon  Watson  for  /70, 
November  6,  1558,  who  alienated  it  again  to  Emmanuel  College  for 
/150,  March  27,  1585  (of  which  sum  Sir  T.  Kellegrew  gave  /140). 
The  College  again  sold  it  to  Nicole  and  Bradshawe  for  /200, 
December  2,  1595,  who  conveyed  it  to  Dr.  Chaderton  on  the  nth 
of  the  same  month,  and  he  gave  it  back  to  the  College  by  deed 
March  31,  1603.

52  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

it  to  the  College  in  1603,  but  seems  to  have  treated  it 
all  along  as  a  kind  of  dependeiwe  of  Emmanuel,  in 
which  there  were  chambers  for  students.  His  biographer 
says  that  the  two  brothers  Rich  were  there.  The  elder, 
Robert,  was  afterwards  the  Earl  of  Warwick  who  took 
a  prominent  part  in  the  Civil  War  on  the  Parliamentary 
side.  He  seems  to  have  been  consistent  throughout.* 
But  the  younger  brother  was  the  splendid  but  unfortu- 
nate Earl  of  Holland,  whose  tortuous  policy  lost  him 
the  favour  of  the  King,  and  brought  him  to  the  block 
at  the  hands  of  the  Parliament.  Both  brothers  entered 
in  1603,  and  both  took  an  active  part  in  American 
colonization,  while  Holland  was  also  for  some  years 
Chancellor  of  the  University.  In  the  same  house  was 
also  John  Finch,  afterwards  Lord  Finch  of  Fordwich 
and  Lord  Keeper,  who  entered  in  1596.  He  was  the 
Speaker  forcibly  held  down  in  his  chair  on  a  memorable 
occasion,  who  as  a  judge  also  took  a  leading  part  in 
the  trial  of  Hampden  and  in  declaring  the  legality  of 
ship-money,  and  only  escaped  impeachment  by  the 
Long  Parliament  by  flight  to  Holland.  He  died  in 
1660  without  an  heir.  Thus  the  College  could  count 
among  its  sons  men  who  took  part  on  both  sides  of  the 
great  political  controversy  of  the  seventeenth  century. 

Nevertheless,  the  prevailing  tone  of  the  place  was 
throughout  Chaderton's  mastership  distinctly  that  of 
advanced  Puritanism.  It  was,  in  fact,  too  much  so  for 
the  Fellows  existing  in  1622,  who  thought  that  they 
were  thereby  under  the  frown  of  the  Court.  This  was 
particularly  distasteful  to  them,  because  their  object  at 
this  time  was  to  be  relieved  from  two  disadvantages 
*  His  son,  Lord  Rich,  entered  the  College  in  1626. 


CHADERTON'S  RESIGNATION  53 

under  which  they  lay  by  the  statutes — the  obligation  of 
residence,  and  the  necessity  of  vacating  their  Fellowships 
when  at  the  standing  for  D.D.,  whether  they  had 
obtained  preferment  or  not.  Their  only  hope  of 
getting  rid  of  these  obligations  was  by  royal  injunction. 
Chaderton,  however,  was  always  resolutely  opposed  to  this 
relief,  asserting  that  it  was  of  the  very  essence  of  the  plan 
and  purpose  of  the  founder  that  men  should  not  reside 
permanently  in  College,  but  should  go  into  the  service 
of  the  Church  through  the  country.  But  even  if  he  had 
thought  otherwise,  the  Fellows  believed  that  his  ecclesi- 
astical views  would  not  help  them  with  the  Court,  and 
that  he  had  outlived  such  influence  as  he  once  pos- 
sessed. The  need  of  more  Court  favour,  and  of  some- 
thing to  counteract  their  character  for  *  singularity ,"*  is 
believed  to  have  been  impressed  on  the  minds  of  the 
Fellows  during  the  Kings's  visit  of  1615.  They  now 
thought  that  in  John  Preston,  of  Queens',  they  had  a 
man  who,  while  enjoying  the  confidence  of  the  Puritans? 
and  having  an  orthodox  belief  in  Calvinism,  yet  had 
that  influence  in  high  quarters  which  they  desired. 
Distantly  connected  by  marriage  with  Buckingham,  who 
desired  to  conciliate  the  Puritan  party,  he  had  by  the 
Duke's  influence  been  named  chaplain  to  Prince  Charles 
in  1620,  and  in  February,  1622,  had  been  appointed 
Preacher  at  Lincoln's  Inn.  As  Tutor  of  Queens'  he  had 
filled  the  College,  and  whenever  he  preached  there  was 
a  crowded  audience  ;  and  though  his  sermons  had  on 
more  than  one  occasion  brought  him  into  some  trouble, 
his  'politic'  character  had  saved  him  from  any  bad 
eff*ects.  The  intrigue  in  his  favour  was  conducted  with 
great  secrecy,  if,  indeed,  there  was  an  intrigue.     Dilling- 


54  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

ham,  the  biographer  of  Chaderton,  represents  his  resig- 
nation as  spontaneous,  and  as  prompted  by  his  con- 
sciousness of  failing  powers  and  health,  as  he  was  now 
eighty-seven.  But  as  he  lived  till  November,  1640,  and 
almost  the  whole  time  in  vigorous  health,  this  does  not 
seem  a  sufficient  cause.  Nor  was  it  expected.  Bedell, 
writing  from  Bury  on  November  25,  1622,  says  : 

'  The  news  of  Dr.  Chaderton  s  resigning  and  the  election 
of  Mr.  Preston  to  the  Mastership  of  Emmanuel  was  alto- 
gether unexpected  in  these  parts  :  whereof  I  doubt  not 
there  were  secret  motives  and  perhaps  conditions  more 
than  the  world  knowes  of.* 

The  whole  affair,  in  fact,  had  been  conducted  with 
unusual  secrecy.  Chaderton  resigned  on  September  25, 
and  as  all  the  existing  Fellows  (11)  were  in  residence,  it 
was  decided  that  it  was  unnecessary  to  put  upon  the 
chapel  door  the  statutory  notice  of  the  vacancy.  On 
October  2  Preston  was  elected,  the  College  doors  being 
locked  whilst  the  election  took  place.*     One  motive  for

or  election  till  all  was  done  and  finished.  Dr.  Travers  himself, 
whom  some  might  suppose  likely  to  hear  of  such  a  matter,  heard

CHADERTON'S  RESIGNATION  55 

this  secrecy  was  said  to  be  the  fear  of  a  royal  mandate 
to  elect  Dr.  Travers,  who,  since  his  Fellowship  had 
lapsed,  had  been  living  at  Christ's  College,  and  whom 
for  some  reason  the  Fellows  did  not  wish  to  have.  But 
in  spite  of  Dillingham's  assertion,  there  seems  no  doubt 
that  great  pressure  was  put  upon  Chaderton  to  resign  ; 
that  he  did  it  with  reluctance,  against  the  wish  of  his 
friends  and  wife  (who  lived  till  1631) ;  and  without 
having  any  large  private  resources  to  fall  back  upon. 
This  last  difficulty  was  got  over,  according  to  Ball,  by 
a  letter  to  Chaderton  from  the  Duke  of  Buckingham 
(September  20,  1622),  in  which,  after  assuring  him  that 
the  King  approved  of  Preston  as  his  successor,  he  adds  : 
'  And  for  that  point  of  supply  of  maintenance  I  shall 
(as  I  promised)  take  care  for  to  procure  it,  when  fit 
occasion  shall  be  offered.'  It  does  not  appear,  however, 
that  Chaderton  ever  received  a  pension,  though  Cecil 
offered  him  the  bishopric  of  Chester,  and  perhaps  other 
preferments.  But  he  preferred  a  retired  life,  devoted 
to  reading  and  study,  varied  by  planting,  grafting,  and 
other  botanical  experiments,  in  which  he  took  much 
delight.  Indeed,  Dillingham  asserts  that,  though  Pres- 
ton may  possibly  have  obtained  Buckingham's  letter, 
Chaderton  never  received  it,  and  that  '  no  promise  was 
made  in  it  about  any  allowance  being  made  for  Chader- 
ton's  support,  no  assurance  given,  expected  or  gisked 
for,  nay,  not  so  much  as  known  of  by  the  venerable 
man.'  -  But,  as  Mr.  Mullinger  observes  (p.   570),  '  it 

absent,  thereby  to  signify  the  vacation  to  them, — to  prevent  which 
they  made  choice  of  a  time  when  all  the  Fellows  were  at  home,  to 
call  them  together,  and  make  known  the  resignation  to  all  present 
at  once. ' 


56  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

seems  difficult  to  suppose  that  either  Preston  or  his 
biographer  could  have  concocted  such  a  letter,**  and  it 
appears  likely  that  the  offer  of  the  bishopric  and  other 
preferments,  of  which  Dillingham  speaks,  was  practically 
a  fulfilment  of  the  promise.  When  they  were  declined, 
the  Court  no  doubt  felt  no  farther  obligation,  and 
Chaderton  spent  the  remaining  eighteen  years  of  his 
life  in  retirement,  though  he  seems  always  to  have  taken 
an  active  interest  in  the  affairs  of  the  College,  especially 
in  the  management  of  its  estates.  He  died  on  No- 
vember 18,  1640,  and  was  buried  in  the  old  chapel, 
from  which  his  body  was  afterwards  transferred  to  the 
new  chapel. 

The  end  of  his  mastership  is  a  natural  close  of  the 
first  chapter  in  the  College  history.  With  his  disap- 
pearance a  change  in  its  atmosphere  soon  began  to 
make  itself  evident.

CHAPTER  IV 

THE  MASTERSHIPS  OF  JOHN  PRESTON  (1622-1628) 
AND  WILLIAM  SANDCROFT  (1628-1637) 

The  mastership  of  John  Preston  does  not  appear  to 
have  answered  the  expectation  of  the  Fellows.  The 
obligation  of  continual  residence,  which  prevented  them 
from  making  provision  for  their  future  by  chaplaincies 
or  tutorships,  was  not  relaxed  ;  nor  was  anything  done 
to  relieve  them  from  the  effects  of  the  de  mora  statute, 
so  as  to  allow  them  to  keep  their  Fellowships  until  pre- 
sented to  a  College  living.  Nor  in  other  ways  did  he 
make  his  mark  in  the  College.  Ball,  who  admires  him 
greatly,  can  find  nothing  to  record  of  his  administration 
except  that 

'he  observed  that  the  Scholars  kept  Acts  but  seldome, 
and  accordingly  when  they  came  into  it  performed  it  but 
meanly ;  he  therefore  advised  with  the  fellows  in  it,  and 
after  many  consultations  it  was  resolved  that  the  number 
of  Acts  should  in  a  manner  be  doubled,  of  those  under 
Masters  of  Arts,  which  was  a  great  advancement  to  learn- 
ing of  all  sorts  in  the  Colledge/ 

But,  as  a  matter  of  fact,  Preston  seems  to  have  been 
much  absent  from  the  College  under  various  pretexts — 
duty  at  Court,  as  preacher  at  Lincoln's  Inn,  and  on  the 

68 


PRESTON'S  MASTERSHIP  59 

plea  of  being  wanted  in  London  to  watch  over  the 
interests  of  the  society,  which  was  engaged  in  a  lawsuit 
with  a  Mr.  Ewins  for  the  advowson  of  a  living.  The 
writer  in  the  Dictionary  of  National  Biografhy  says  that 
this  last  employment  was  interpreted  as  being  a  violenta 
detentio  within  the  meaning  of  the  statute  De  residentia 
magistri  (cap.  2).  The  interpretation  as  recorded  in 
the  order-book,  however,  says  nothing  of  this  business, 
but  rests  solely  on  '  the  service  of  the  King  and  Prince.' 
It  will  be  well  to  give  this  order  as  it  appears  in  the 
original  with  autograph  signatures,  especially  as  it  is 
one  of  the  only  two  College  orders  signed  by  Preston : 

'Anno  Domini  l623,  Martii  IP. 

'  Whereas  in  the  statute  Cap.  2  de  Residentia  Magistri, 
there  is  this  clause — nisi  violenta  detentione  impeditus 
fuerit.  Our  Master  before  his  admission  Octob.  S,  l622, 
and  before  he  took  his  oath,  desired  an  interpretation  of 
these  words  :  all  the  fellows  (being  eleven  in  number)  did 
with  one  consent  and  assent  make  this  interpretation,  that 
they  were  to  be  understood  as  well  of  a  morall  as  a  natural 
violence,  and  that  the  service  of  the  Kinge  and  Prince  (to 
whom  he  was  then  a  sworne  servant)  was  to  be  esteemed 
as  a  morall  violence.  This  eight  of  us  (who  are  all  that 
are  nowe  left  in  the  Colledge  of  those  eleven)  do  witness 
and  testify  under  our  hands. 

John  Stoughton. 

Jo.  DoD. 

JoHEs  Gamons. 

Sa.  Hildersham. 

J.  Yates. 

Ric.  Hunt. 

Tho.  Hanscombe. 

Na.  Fowle, 


60  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

'The  same  interpretation  we  all  who  are  now  actuall 
master  and  fellowes  doe  renew  and  confirme  under  our 
hands. 

John  Preston. 

John  Stoughton. 

John  Dod. 

John  Gamons. 

Sa.  Hildersham. 

J.  Yates. 

Richard  Hunt. 

Tho.  Hanscombe. 

Na.  Fowle. 

Anth.  Tuckney. 

Walter  Foster.* 

Preston,  being  thus  authorized  to  be  absent  beyond 
the  statutable  time,  seems  to  have  taken  full  advantage 
of  the  privilege.  Only  four  *  admonitions '  are  signed 
by  him — one  undated,  and  three  on  the  same  day 
(May  24,  1627),  all  for  the  same  offence :  '  notorious 
negligence  at  prayers  in  the  Chapel.'  Only  one  College 
order,  besides  the  above,  is  signed  by  him — namely,  one 
of  August  15,  1625,  for  the  breaking  up  of  the  College 
till  October  17,  upon  '  the  contagion  which  is  at  this 
time  in  the  parish  and  the  fear  of  the  increase  thereof.' 
Business  as  to  the  College  estates  he  seems  often  to 
have  committed  to  Chaderton,  who  was,  it  appears, 
still  living  in  his  house  at  St.  Nicolas'  Hostel  as 
tenant  of  the  College.  He  cared  most  for  preaching, 
and  obtained,  after  a  severe  contest,  a  lectureship  at 
Trinity  Church,  worth  £50 — collected,  it  seems,  by  six- 
penny, contributions  among  the  congregation — which, 
as  having  no  '  cure  of  souls,'  he  could  hold  with  the 


PRESTON'S  MASTERSHIP  61 

mastership.  But  he  was  also  much  with  the  Court, 
and  his  high  favour  with  Buckingham  gave  the  Puritan 
party  hopes  of  better  treatment  at  the  hands  of  the 
King,  while  the  '  prelatical  **  party  hoped  to  win  him 
by  the  offer  of  bishoprics  or  other  preferment.  His 
importance  was  believed  to  be  still  greater  as  James 
neared  his  end,  because  it  was  evident  that  under  the 
new  reign  Buckingham  would  be  all-powerful.  Preston 
was  at  Theobalds  when  the  King  died  (March  27, 1625), 
and  went  to  London  with  Charles  for  his  proclamation. 
There  he  maintained  for  some  time  his  hold  on  Buck- 
ingham, who  is  said  to  have  even  proposed  him  to  the 
King  as  Lord  Keeper,  of  which  office  Bishop  Williams 
was  now  deprived.  Sir  Thomas  Coventry,  however,  was 
appointed,  who  had  acted  as  counsel  for  the  College  in 
their  lawsuit,  and  now  determined  it  in  their  favour. 
This  much  good  the  College  might  in  part  attribute  to 
the  influence  of  their  Master.  It  soon  became  apparent, 
however,  that  his  power  over  Buckingham  was  melting 
away.  The  Duke  disappointed  the  hopes  of  the 
Puritans,  and  began  giving  plain  indications  of  sym- 
pathy with  the  '  prelatists,'  greatly,  no  doubt,  under 
the  influence  of  King  Charles.  Preston  accordingly 
lost  faith  in  him,  and  was  incautious  enough  to  express 
this  feeling  in  a  letter  'to  a  gentleman  of  Northampton,' 
which  letter  by  some  accident  was  dropped  by  its 
bearer  near  Temple  Bar,  and  its  contents  made  known 
to  the  Duke,  who  soon  made  it  manifest  that  Preston 
was  no  longer  in  his  good  graces.  The  Fellows  there- 
fore— or  a  majority  of  them — determined  to  act  for 
themselves,  and  petition  the  Crown  directly  for  a  dis- 
pensation from  the  statute. 


62  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

An  attempt  had  been  already  made  in  1595,  when 
Charles  Chad  wick,  the  Senior  Fellow,  claimed  to  hold  his 
Fellowship  on  the  ground  that  the  supplementary  statute 
was  not  of  equal  validity  with  the  rest.  The  question  was 
referred  to  the  Vice-Chancellor  (Dr.  Goade),  and  was 
formally  tried  by  him,  with  John  Cowel,  LL.D.,  acting 
as  assessor,  on  November  21.  His  decision,  drawn  up  by 
John  Smith,  public  notary,  is  extant.  It  was  decisively 
in  favour  of  the  statute.  Chadwick  appears  at  the  time 
to  have  retaliated  upon  Chaderton  by  asserting  that  he 
had  forfeited  the  mastership  by  not  taking  his  Doctor's 
degree.  But  that  point,  too,  was  given  in  Chaderton's 
favour  on  the  production  of  a  written  dispensation  from 
Mildmay.  This  decree  of  the  Vice-Chancellor  settled 
the  matter.  Chadwick  was  obliged  to  vacate  his  Fellow- 
ship and  to  seek  Church  preferment,  which  he  obtained 
at  Woodham  Ferrers  in  Essex,  and,  however  discon- 
tented, the  Fellows  made  no  more  attempt  to  get  the 
statute  altered  or  more  favourably  interpreted  till  1627, 
when  the  following  petition  was  sent  up  to  the  King : 

'To  THE  King's  most  excellent  Majesty. 

'  The  Humble  petition  of  the  Fellowes  of  Emmanuel 
College  in  Cambridge  shewith 

'  That  whereas  our  last  supposed  statute  of  leaving 
our  places  at  Doctor's  time,  or  within  a  yeare  after,  if  we 
take  our  degree,  hath  been  and  is  a  grievance  to  our 
societie,  the  validitie  of  which  Statute  may  justlie  be 
questioned,  as  being  neither  sealed,  nor  having  witnesses, 
nor  being  delivered  as  the  rest  of  the  statutes  were,  nor 
yet  having  taken  effect  by  forcing  any  to  forsake  their 
places  at  that  time  :  Or,  in  case  it  should  be  graunted  to 


PRESTON^S  MASTERSHIP  63 

be  a  statute,  being  very  hard,  we  being  thereby  exposed 
to  many  and  great  inconveniences,  as  being  destitute  of 
other  meanes  when  we  shall  be  soe  cast  out,  and  hindered 
for  the  present  approveing  our  service  to  any,  by  reason  of 
our  continuance  strictly  required  by  statute,  and  whereas 
no  inconvenience  is  like  to  redound  either  to  the  govern- 
ment or  estate  of  the  Colledge  by  libertye  of  continuance, 
as  appeareth  by  all  other  Colledges  where  they  have  the 
like  priviledge,  and  more  particularlye  in  Sidney  Sussex 
Colledge,  where  the  like  statute  hath  been  abrogated. 

'  That  your  Majestye  would  bee  pleased  to  graunte  a 
reference  of  this  our  grievance  to  the  Heads  of  Colledges, 
or  to  such  of  them  as  your  Majesty  should  be  pleased  to 
assign,  to  consider  whether  or  noe  it  bee  a  statute  of  equal 
validitie  with  the  other  ;  and  if  soe,  whether  a  qualification 
bee  not  requisite  for  grauntinge  libertie  to  us  to  enjoy  our 
places,  till  wee  can  bee  competently  provided  for ;  and 
according  as  in  duty  bounde  wee  ever  pray  for  your 
Majestye's  long,  happy,  and  prosperous  reign/ 

A  reference  to  the  Vice-Chancellor  and  Heads  was  at 
once  granted.  Opposition  came  from  two  quarters: 
first  from  Sir  Henry  Mildmay,  the  founder's  grandson, 
and  secondly  from  Chaderton.  The  former  offered  as 
a  compromise  to  secure  to  the  College  the  advowson  of 
six  livings  of  not  less  than  ^^100  a  year  value,  in  order 
to  provide  for  such  Fellows  as  were  thus  compelled  to 
vacate.  Chaderton  wrote  a  strong  paper,  dated  April  14, 
1627,  of  reasons  against  the  charge  to  be  submitted  to 
the  Vice-Chancellor  and  Heads.  His  objections  were 
grounded  on  the  violation  proposed  to  be  done  to  the 
wishes  of  the  dead  ;  on  the  discouragement  thus  offered 
to  future  founders  and  benefactors ;  on  the  fact  that 


64  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

the  College  already  had  enough  livings  for  the  vacating 
Fellows ;  on  the  fact  that  succession  to  Fellowships  was 
thus  more  rapid  and  the  number  of  learned  ministers  sent 
out  larger,  while  '  the  unprofitable  stay  of  men  in  College 
was  prevented**;  on  the  fact  that  in  other  Colleges  the 
tenure  of  Fellowships  was  limited  ;  and,  lastly,  that  the 
founder  had  declared  that  '  he  would  rather  not  have 
founded  the  College  than  have  omitted  this  statute/ 

The  Vice-Chancellor  and  Heads  at  first  drew  up  an 
answer  in  the  sense  of  Chaderton'^s  protest,  strongly 
advising  against  the  abrogation  of  the  statute.  But 
on  farther  consideration — perhaps  on  a  hint  from 
Buckingham — they  sent  up  a  more  guarded  answer, 
affirming,  indeed,  the  validity  of  the  statute,  but  at  the 
same  time  suggesting  that  the  change  in  the  circum- 
stances of  the  times  might  make  its  suspension  advisable. 
The  whole  business  was  rapidly  concluded.  Chaderton"*s 
protest  is  dated  April  14,  and  on  May  5  a  dispensation 
was  issued  from  the  Court  at  Westminster.  It  is 
grounded  on  the  report  of  the  V ice-Chancellor  and 
Heads : 

'  You  after  due  examination  of  the  business  have  under 
your  hands  and  seals  given  us  to  understand  that  concern- 
ing the  validity  of  the  statute  you  are  fully  persuaded 
of  the  strength  thereof,  but  as  touching  the  convenience 
of  the  said  statute,  however  the  same  was  providently 
ordeyned  at  the  time,  when  there  was  scarcity  of  sufficient 
preachers  to  furnish  the  Church  abroad,  yet  because  that 
reason  now  ceaseth  and  the  Church  is  now  replenished 
with  many  able  men,  you  conceive  a  qualification  neither 
prejudicial  to  the  estate  of  the  Church  in  generall  nor  the 
government  of  this  particular  society.' 


PRESTON'S  MASTERSHIP  65 

The  King  then  goes  on  to  ordain  that  the  statute  be 
wholly  suspended,  giving  five  reasons  :  (1)  Because  those 
who  have  taken  Holy  Orders  (which  calling  they  may 
never  forsake  or  exchange)  are  compelled  either,  instead 
of  being  labourers,  to  stand  idle  in  the  vineyard,  or,  as 
'  lecturers,"'  to  seek  their  livelihood  from  the  benevolence 
and  favour  of  the  people ;  (2)  because  to  the  great  dis- 
paragement of  their  persons  and  degrees  they  are  com- 
pelled to  become  curates  and  mercenaries  under  others ; 
(3)  because  the  statute  enforcing  residence  prevents 
them,  like  other  clergymen,  from  securing  their  positions 
by  services  abroad ;  (4)  because  other  Colleges  allow 
their  clerical  Fellows  to  remain  till  they  get  a  living, 
and  a  far  less  strict  statute  in  Sidney  Sussex  (allowing 
seven  years'  residence  after  Doctor's  standing)  had  been 
rescinded ;  (5)  because  even  in  Emmanuel  dispensations 
had  been  already  obtained,  particularly  by  Dr.  Bran- 
thwaite.  Towards  the  end  of  the  royal  injunction, 
however,  a  proviso  is  made  that,  if  Sir  H.  Mildmay 
fulfilled  his  promise  of  giving  the  College  five  or  six 
advowsons  worth  a  hundred  a  year  within  six  years  of 
the  date  of  the  dispensation,  the  dispensation  is  to  be 
revoked. 

On  December  22,  1640,  a  committee  was  appointed 
to  investigate  the  abuses  of  the  University,  and  in 
virtue  of  their  report  the  King's  dispensation  was 
revoked  on  March  22,  1641.  This  declaration  of  the 
Parliament  was,  however,  either  declared  or  regarded 
as  invalid  at  the  Restoration.  The  dispensation  of 
1627,  therefore,  after  1660,  again  ruled  the  tenure  of 
Fellowships  of  those  in  Orders,  while  laymen  vacated 
theirs  when  they  came  to  be  one  of  the  four  seniors, 

5 


66  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

according  to  the  statute  of  the  founder.  Practically, 
however,  men  henceforth  held  their  Fellowships  till  they 
got  a  College  living.  This  state  of  things  was  recognised 
by  the  Commissioners  (1856-1861).  In  the  statutes  con- 
firmed by  the  Queen  in  Council  on  June  26  of  the  latter 
year,  four  Fellows  had  to  be  in  Orders ;  but  if  there  were 
no  Fellows  willing  to  take  Orders  elections  were  to  be 
made  outside.  Clergymen,  if  unmarried,  could  hold 
their  Fellowships  without  limit ;  a  layman  vacated  his 
at  the  end  of  ten  years,  unless  he  held  a  professorship  or 
certain  College  offices.  The  farther  alterations  made  by 
the  Commission  of  1877-1882  will  be  noticed  later  on. 

According  to  his  biographer,  Preston  was  vehemently 
opposed  to  the  relaxation  of  the  statute  in  1627,  and  he 
certainly  seems  to  have  been  on  bad  terms  with  some  of 
the  Fellows.  But  he  was  now  little  more  in  the 
College,  for  his  health  was  rapidly  failing.  He  moved 
from  place  to  place,  hoping  to  be  cured  by  country  air. 
The  winter  of  1627-28  seems  to  have  been  spent  in  a 
house  on  high  ground  at  Linton,  from  which  he  came  to 
preach  at  Cambridge  on  Sundays.  In  the  spring  he 
went  to  London,  after  having  vainly  consulted  Dr. 
Despontine,  the  Italian  doctor  settled  by  Bedell  at  Bury. 
By  the  advice  of  the  London  doctors  he  stayed  awhile 
at  Newington,  whence  he  removed  to  Hertfordshire,  and 
thence  to  his  native  county  of  Northamptonshire,  to  the 
house  of  Mr.  Dod,  the  clergyman  of  Preston.  Here  he 
was,  it  appears,  mistakenly  treated  by  an  Oxford  doctor 
(Ash worth),  and  succumbed  on  July  20,  1628,  when  not 
quite  forty  years  old.  He  had  been  a  good  deal  away 
from  the  College,  especially  in  1625,  when  there  was  an 
alarm  of  plague.     But  at  the  beginning  of  his  master- 


PRESTON^S  WILL  67 

ship  he  seemed  likely  to  be  much  more  away  still,  for  he 
was  appointed  to  go  as  chaplain  to  Sir  Arthur  Chichester 
into  Germany;  and  a  royal  mandate  was  obtained, 
dated  July  15,  1623,  for  his  Doctor'^s  degree,  that  he 
might  have  a  dignity  suitable  for  the  office  (Heywood 
and  Wright,  ii.  316).  The  embassy,  however,  did  not 
take  place,  but  in  preparation  for  his  journey  he  made 
the  will  that  was  proved  after  his  death.  The  executor 
and  residuary  legatee  was  William,  Lord  Saye  and 
Seal,  and  though  there  is  no  mention  in  it  of  a  legacy 
to  Emmanuel  College,  Preston  appears  from  a  letter  of 
Lord  Saye  and  Seal,  which  has  been  preserved,  to  have 
directed  him  to  '  bestow  the  interest  of  ,^200  upon 
Scolards  that  were  bred  upp  in  the  Colledge  (Em- 
manuel).' Lord  Saye  and  Seal  appears  after  some 
years  to  have  suspended  this  payment  when  'these 
unhappy  times  of  trouble  came.'  At  the  Restoration, 
however,  he  proposed  to  renew  the  payment,  and  to  add 
to  it  c£40  in  view  of  unpaid  interest.  In  his  will  (he 
died  in  1662)  he  directed  his  heir  to  invest  the  money  in 
land  'of  inheritance  to  the  same,  either  to  the  said 
James  or  to  the  Head,  President  and  Fellows  of  the  said 
Colledge,  their  successors  and  assigns,  at  the  discretion  of 
my  said  sonne  James.'  It  was  not  till  1668  that  the 
instructions  of  the  will  were  carried  out.  A  letter  on 
this  subject  to  Breton,  Master  of  Emmanuel,  is  dated 
May  8,  1668 : 

'Dr.  Brittaine, 

'  Mr.  Henry  Lodge,  who  was  formally  recommended 
to  y""  Colledge,  and  to  whom  I  gave  that  annual  allowance 
of  twelve  pounds  given  by  Dr.  Preston,  is  lately  dead.     I 

5—2 


68  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

shall  therefore  desire  you  to  receive  a  brother  of  his, 
Mr.  Sidney  Lodge,  into  his  place,  and  accordingly  I  shall 
pay  to  him  the  same  stipend  for  his  maintenance.  S^  I 
have  signed  and  sealed  that  deed  for  the  use  of  y"^  Colledge, 
and  it  is  left  either  with  Mr.  Nelson  or  Mr.  Finshaw,  whom 
you  know ;  both  their  chambers  are  in  Grayes  Inne. 

'S^  I  am, 

'Y>^  friend, 

*Say  and  Seal.' 

The  deed  here  mentioned  is  extant,  and  secures  to  the 
Master,  Fellows  and  Scholars  of  Emmanuel  College 
an  *  Annuity  or  Rent  Charge  of  £12,  issuing  out  of 
three  parcel  Is  of  Meadow  Ground  in  the  Parish  of 
Drayton  in  Oxfordshire."*  This  rent-charge  was  paid 
regularly  for  some  time,  but  the  land  changed  hands 
several  times,  and  it  began  to  fall  into  arrear.  In  17520 
the  College  unsuccessfully  sued  the  then  Lord  Saye  and 
Seal,  and  from  that  time  it  disappears  from  the  College 
accounts  {Emmanuel  College  Magazine,  vol.  vi.,  No.  2, 
pp.  90-95). 

The  College,  therefore,  did  not  permanently  benefit 
by  Preston's  liberality.  In  other  respects,  though  not, 
apparently,  from  much  personal  superintendence,  it 
continued  to  flourish  under  his  rule.  The  numbers  did 
not  decline.  In  two  of  the  six  years  they  were  above 
the  average  (S2  and  89),  and  among  the  entries  are 
some  interesting  names,  four  of  them  of  men  who  were 
afterwards  Masters  of  other  Colleges — W.  Spurstowe,  of 
St.  Catherine's;  Lazarus  Seaman,  of  St.  Peter's;  William 
Dell,  of  Caius  ;  Benjamin  Whichcote,  of  King's. 

William  Spurstowe  took  his  degrees  at  Emmanuel 
(B.A.  1626,  M.A.  1630),  but  was  elected  a  Fellow  of 


PRESTON'S  MASTERSHIP  69 

Catharine  Hall.  He  resigned  that  on  becoming  Rector 
of  Great  Hampden  in  Buckinghamshire.  He  was  one 
of  the  five  divines  whose  initials  helped  to  make  up  the 
name  Smectymnuus  (uu[  =  w]  +  s).  He  was  fully  in  sym- 
pathy with  the  Parliament  in  its  quarrel  with  the  King, 
and  served  as  chaplain  to  Hampden\s  Green  Coats  in 

1642.  He  took  the  Solemn  League  and  Covenant  in 

1643,  and  in  1645  was  put  into  the  mastership  of 
Catharine  Hall,  on  the  deprivation  of  Ralph  Browning 
by  the  University  Commissioners  of  the  Parliament. 
But  though  his  sermons  and  writings  were  all  of  the 
strictly  Puritan  cast,  his  after-life  is  an  example  of 
the  revulsion  against  extremes  in  politics  and  Church 
government  which  caused  the  anti-Puritan  reaction. 

In  1649  a  subscription  to  'the  Engagement'  was 
required  by  the  Parliamentary  Committee — that  is,  a 
declaration  of  allegiance  to  the  existing  government 
*  without  King  or  House  of  Lords.'  Spurstowe,  who 
had  been  employed  in  negotiations  with  Charles,  and 
strongly  objected  to  his  trial,  was  one  of  those  Masters 
who  declined  to  make  the  declaration,  and  ipso  facto 
vacated  their  places  on  March  23,  1650  (Hey  wood  and 
Wright,  ii.,  530).  At  the  Restoration,  again,  he  was 
made  a  chaplain  to  Charles  H.,  and  preached  once  at 
Court,  yet  he  was  not  prepared  to  conform  to  the  Act  of 
Uniformity,  and  accordingly  vacated  his  vicarage  of 
Hackney  on  St.  Bartholomew's  Day  (August  24,  1662). 

Lazarus  Seaman  (B.A.  1627,  M.A.  1631),  after 
teaching  for  a  time  in  a  school,  was  chosen  lecturer  at 
St.  Martin's,  Ludgate,  and  was  chaplain  to  the  Earl  of 
Northumberland,  and  in  1642  was  presented  to  All 
Hallows',  Broad  Street.     Like  Spurstowe,  he  was  one  of 


70  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

the  Westminster  Assembly  of  Divines,  and  one  of  the 
Presbyterians  deputed  to  treat  with  Charles.  In  1644 
he  was  put  into  the  mastership  of  Peterhouse,  At 
the  Restoration  he  was  turned  out  of  the  mastership, 
but  retained  All  Hallows'*  till  the  Act  of' Uniformity  in 
1662.  He  was  a  Presbyterian,  strongly  opposed  to 
'  separate  congregations,**  and  had  also  been  against  the 
King's  trial.  Yet  Cromwell  respected  him,  and  ap- 
pointed him  a  Visitor  of  the  University  in  1654.  After 
his  resignation  of  All  Hallows'  he  acted  privately  as 
minister  to  a  portion  of  his  old  congregation,  and  in 
1672  built  a  chapel  in  Wood  Street.  He  was  a  man  of 
much  learning,  and  his  collection  of  5,000  books  was 
the  first  library  sold  by  auction  in  England  (1675). 
That,  and  his  prudence  and  caution,  joined  to  consider- 
able controversial  ability,  mark  him  out  as  a  typical 
product  of  the  College. 

William  Dell  (B.A.  1628,  M.A.  1631)  was  secretary 
to  Archbishop  Laud,  but  in  1645,  having  become  an 
Antinomian,  he  was  with  Fairfax  as  a  '  preacher  of  the 
army.'  He  seems  to  have  tried  to  establish  a  kind  of 
unity  by  refusing  to  recognise  all  distinction  of  sects, 
but  did  not  succeed  in  pleasing  any  party,  except, 
perhaps,  the  leaders  of  the  Independents.  He  is  said 
to  have  offered  his  religious  services  to  Charles  on  the 
morning  of  his  execution.  On  May  5,  1650,  he  was 
appointed  Master  of  Caius  in  the  place  of  the  ejected 
Dr.  Batchcroft.  He  was  very  severe  in  this  office  in 
displacing  Royalist  Fellows,  and  denouncing  not  only 
official  University  dress  and  ornaments,  but  the  value  of 
learning  generally  to  the  study  of  Scripture.  He  held 
with  the  mastership  the  living  of  Yelden  in  Bedford- 


PRESTON'S  MASTERSHIP  71 

shire.  From  the  former  he  was  ejected  in  1660  (Batch- 
croft  being  reinstated),  and  from  the  latter  in  1662 
by  the  Bartholomew  Act.  His  writing  and  doctrines, 
though  enjoying  a  certain  notoriety,  appear  not  to 
have  pleased  any  sect  except  the  Quakers ;  and  he  was 
accused  of  inconsistency  in  preaching  against  infant 
baptism,  and  yet  having  his  own  children  baptized; 
against  tithes,  and  yet  taking  <^200  a  year  from 
Yelden  ;  against  Universities,  and  yet  being  Head  of  a 
College.  Yet  he  carried  out  one  of  his  doctrines  by 
securing  that  his  body  should  be  buried  in  unconse- 
crated  ground.  He  probably  would  have  justified 
himself  on  all  these  heads,  but  his  teaching  would  not 
have  been  in  harmony  with  the  views  current  in  his  old 
College. 

Of  Benjamin  Whichcote,  who  entered  in  the  later 
years  of  Preston's  mastership,  I  shall  speak  hereafter. 

Besides  these  divines,  there  entered  in  1624  Henry 
PiERREPONT,  first  Marquis  of  Dorchester,  and  his  brother 
William.  They  were  the  sons  of  the  Earl  of  Kingston, 
and  Henry  (born  1606)  was  called  up  to  the  House  of 
Lords  as  Viscount  Newark  in  1641,  and  for  services  to 
the  King  was  created  Marquis  of  Dorchester  in  1645. 
After  the  war  was  over,  he  compounded  for  delinquency 
by  a  fine  of  one-tenth  of  his  estate,  and  afterwards 
devoted  himself  to  science,  especially  to  medicine  and 
law,  being  entered  at  Gray's  Inn,  and  also  becoming  a 
Fellow  of  the  College  of  Physicians.  At  the  Restoration 
he  returned  to  Court,  and  became  a  Privy  Councillor 
again.  He  seems  to  have  been  a  quarrelsome  person, 
but  really  devoted  to  science.  His  brother  William, 
who  entered   Emmanuel    with   him,  took    the  Parlia- 


72  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

mentarj  side  in  the  war,  but  was  generally  in  favour  of 
peace  and  of  making  terms  with  the  King.  He,  how- 
ever, justified  his  title  of  '  Wise  William  "*  by  keeping 
all  through  on  good  terms  with  Cromwell,  by  giving 
Richard  Cromwell  excellent  counsel,  and  yet  on  the 
Restoration  being  able  to  secure,  not  only  his  own 
safety,  but  that  of  many  others  also.  He  died  in 
1678. 

In  the  last  year  of  Preston's  mastership  John  Harvard 
entered  the  College  (April  17,  1627)  with  Middlesex  as 
his  county  (p.  57).  It  is  curious  and  touching  that  the 
name  of  a  man  who  won  no  distinction  in  life,  and  died 
at  thirty-one  of  consumption  so  far  from  us,  should  yet 
constitute  one  of  our  most  enduring  and  most  valued 
memorials : 

'  That  low  man  seeks  a  little  thing  to  do. 
Sees  it  and  does  it : 
This  high  man,  with  a  great  thing  to  pursue. 
Dies  ere  he  knows  it.* 

John  Harvard  saw  what  he  had  to  do  and  did  it,  and 
it  turned  out  to  be  a  greater  thing  than  he  could  have 
dreamed.  Leaving  England,  we  do  not  know  why — 
perhaps  in  hope  of  escaping  the  deadly  disease  which 
was  in  his  blood  (for  his  brother  Thomas  died  the  year 
he  sailed) — he,  with  his  lately-married  wife,  reached 
New  England,  and  was  admitted  a  townsman  of  Charles- 
town  on  August  6  in  1637.  On  September  14  in 
the  next  year  (1638)  he  died  of  consumption,  leaving 
one  half  of  his  estate  (^79  17s,  2d.),  with  a  library  of 
320  volumes,  for  the  College  which  at  that  time  it  was 
proposed  to  found  at  New  Town   (Cambridge).     Em- 


JOHN  HARVARD  73 

manuel  has  no  record  of  him  except  his  name  in  a 
book  of  receipts,  and  his  autograph  in  a  small  volume 
of  divinity  in  the  College  library — 

'  Only  the  actions  of  the  just 
Smell  sweet  and  blossom  in  the  dust.* 

Little  as  he  ever  thought  it,  his  name  endures  for  ever 
in  the  great  University  of  the  West,  the  beginning  of 
which  was  made  possible  by  his  liberality  and  faith. 

He  had  inherited  a  fair  property  from  his  mother, 
Katherine  Rogers,  who  married  three  times — her  first 
husband,  Robert  Harvard,  a  butcher  of  Southwark, 
having  died  of  the  plague  in  1625.  She  appears 
to  have  died  in  1635,  the  year  in  which  Harvard 
took  his  M.A.  degree.  In  Charlestown  he  ministered 
in  the  '  First  Church,'  where,  it  is  said,  he  '  preached 
and  prayed  with  tears  and  evidence  of  strong  afFection.' 
He  is  described  by  another  contemporary  as  '  a  scholar 
and  a  lover  of  learning,"*  and  by  another  as  '  a  scholar 
and  pious  in  his  life,  and  enlarged  toward  the  country 
and  the  good  of  it  in  life  and  death.""  There  is  no 
record  of  his  having  been  ordained,  but,  as  he  seems  to 
have  been  at  once  received  as  a  clergyman  on  arriving 
in  New  England,  he  had  probably  been  authorized  as  a 
preacher  in  some  way  before  leaving  home ;  and  as  he 
married  the  daughter  of  a  beneficed  clergyman,  he  had 
very  likely  received  episcopal  Orders. 

The  next  Master,  William  SANDCROFr  (1628-1637), 
entered  the  College  in  1596,  and  was  elected  Fellow  in 
1605.  He  was  of  a  Suffolk  family,  and  at  the  end  of 
his  life  had  some  cure  or  house  at  Bury.  His  Fellow- 
ship lapsed  in  1614-15,  and  Chaderton  wrote  warmly  in 


74  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

his  favour  to  the  patron  of  West  Wickam  in  Kent 
(Samuel  Lennard).  On  his  election  to  the  mastership 
in  1628,  he  was  holding  this  or  some  other  benefice,  for 
before  he  would  accept  the  mastership  he  insisted  on 
the  Fellows  making  an  interpretation  of  the  thirteenth 
statute  which  would  enable  him  to  continue  to  hold  it. 
Accordingly,  an  order  on  the  subject  appears  in  the 
order-book  under  date  August  5,  1628.  After  reciting 
the  founder's  statute,  it  goes  on : 

'  Notwithstanding  wee  are  persuaded  that  it  was  neyther 
his  meaning  to  forbid  the  choyce  of  a  beneficed  man  nor 
his  retayning  of  the  said  cure  for  a  convenient  time,  which 
we  conceive  to  be  about  but  not  above  that  time  which  the 
fellowes  are  allowed  to  enjoy  their  places  after  they  have 
induction  into  a  liveing.  This  we  all  agreed  upon  before 
the  admission  of  the  present  Master.  August  5,  l628, 
anno  regni  Regis  Caroli  quarto.* 

This  is  signed  by  all  twelve  Fellows,  of  whom  Anthony 
Tuckney — himself  afterwards  Master — is  one.  It  does 
not  appear,  however,  that  Sandcroft,  in  compliance  with 
this  interpretation,  either  resigned  his  benefice  or  refused 
any  new  one.  His  nine  years  of  mastership  was  a  period 
of  continuous  prosperity  as  far  as  numbers  were  con- 
cerned ;  it  also  witnessed  a  great  increase  in  the  buildings 
by  the  commencement  of  what  is  now  called  the  *  old 
court,"*  the  call  for  which  is  attributed,  in  the  College 
order  of  February  4,  1633,  to  the  necessity 

'  of  enlarging  our  roome,  which  long  time  hathe  been  and 
still  is  too  scant  to  receive  the  number  of  students  in  the 
Colledge,  and  for  bringing  of  them  all  to  keepe  and  lodge 
within  the  walls  (according  to  the  express  words  of  the 


SANDCROFT^S  MASTERSHIP  75 

statute,  cap.  40,  Nullum  pro  pensonario  ad  convictum 
scholarium  aut  sociorum  admitti  vel  in  Collegio  morari 
volumus  qui  non  cameram  habeat  uti  in  dicto  Collegio 
cubet).  It  was  thought  fitt  by  the  Master  and  the 
Society,  and  accordingly  agreed  and  concluded  by  joynt 
and  unanimous  consent  of  the  major  part,  to  have  a  new 
range  of  building  erected  from  the  founder's  chamber  to 
Pit's  garden  according  to  a  plott  drawne  and  covenants 
made  to  that  purpose.' 

He  also  seems  to  have  begun  by  reforming  certain 
irregularities  in  the  entering  of  scholars'*  names  and 
counties  in  the  College  register,  and  the  habit  of  some 
scholars  getting  their  food  and  drink  from  the  town 
instead  of  the  College  butteries.  Discipline  would  also 
seem,  from  the  admonition-book,  to  have  been  well 
maintained.  But,  on  the  other  hand,  he  seems  to  have 
had  somewhat  violent  quarrels  with  the  Fellows.  To 
begin  with,  he  headed  a  moiety  of  the  Fellows  (six)  in 
petitioning  the  Chancellor  to  obtain  the  revocation  of 
the  royal  dispensation  from  the  de  mora  statute.  The 
Chancellor  was  now  an  Emmanuel  man,  Lord  Holland, 
who  had  by  the  King's  injunction  succeeded  Buckingham 
(murdered  in  1628),  and  they  may  have  thought  him 
likely — *  as  the  noble  ornament  of  our  College  ' — to 
support  the  wishes  of  the  majority.  The  petition, 
however,  was  unsuccessful,  but  its  last  clause  indicates 
a  considerable  tension  :  '  Ninthly,  the  statute  standing 
in  its  former  force  would  have  prevented  no  small 
disturbances  of  the  peace  of  the  College  which  have 
lately  happened.'  That  these  '  disturbances  of  the 
peace  of  the  College '  continued,  and  sometimes  in  an 
acute  form,  is  shown  by  the  admonition-book,  in  which 


76  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

for  the  first  two  occasions  a  peremptory  admonition  is 
administered  to  one  of  the  Fellows,  Henry  Salmon, 
afterwards  Rector  of  Standground,  for  countermanding 
orders  of  the  Master.  The  matter  seems  to  have 
gone  before  the  Vice  -  Chancellor,  whereupon  Salmon 
made  some  apology  ;  for  the  admonition  is  crossed 
out,  and  Sandcroft  adds  a  note  that  he  is  content 
upon  that  to  withdraw  the  admonition.  The  entry 
in  the  admonition-book  does  not  enlighten  us  as  to 
the  exact  nature  of  Salmon's  offence,  but  it  is  eloquent 
of  the  discord  reigning  in  the  College. 

'  Mem"'"  y'  upon  y^  8  October,  1632,  Mr.  Salmon  was 
peremptorily  admonished  by  the  M"^  with  y^  assistance  of 
y^  Deane  and  in  y^  presence  of  Mr.  Foster  and  Mr.  Gierke, 
y^  two  senior  fellowes,  for  affronting  and  resisting  the  M*"
