'  2nd.  Because  this  order  postpones  y^  seniority  among 
y®  Fellows  to  y®  seniority  among  y®  scholars,  which  we 
think  is  not  fair  and  equal,  and  directly  contrary  to  y*^ 
customs  of  y«  College,  and  it  may  be  not  altogether  agree- 
able to  y®  statutes,  which  in  cases  of  privilege  give  y® 
preference  to  y®  seniors/ 

As  the  seniors  yield  the  chief  point  at  issue,  it  seems 
scarcely  to  have  been  worth  while  to  insert  this  protest 
and  to  give  such  very  obscure  and  weak  reasons. 

Whether  it  was  financial  embarrassment  or  the  accu- 
mulation of  duplicates  in  the  library  that  prompted  the 
next  order  signed  by  Balderston  (March  20,  1712),  we 
cannot  be  sure,  but  on  that  date  Mr.  JefFerey  is  authorized 
'  to  sell  such  books  belonging  to  the  library  as  the 
society  thinks  fit  to  part  with,"*  and  an  order  of 
January  17,  1716,  seems  to  imply  that  the  proceeds 
amounted  to  .^200,  which  were  '  to  be  put  out  for  y® 
use  of  y®  Library.'  Between  these  two  dates  another 
order  (March  10,  1715)  speaks  of  losses  to  the  College 
owing  to  the  bankruptcy  of  the  tenant  of  the  Brew- 
house  (in  the  paddock),  and  the  need  of  redeeming 
the  various  vessels  in  it  seized  by  the  Excise  officers; 


WILLIAM  LAW  123 

again,  owing  to  the  steward  (Potter)  dying  in  debt 
to  the  College ;  and,  lastly,  owing  to  the  imminent  need 
of  extensive  repairs  to  the  buildings.  In  consequence 
of  these  losses,  two  Fellowships  already  vacant,  and 
three  more  shortly  to  become  so,  are  not  to  be  filled  up. 
Accordingly,  for  some  years  to  come  the  number  of 
Fellows  signing  orders  never  exceeds  eight.  Serious 
consideration  seems  at  once  to  have  been  given  to  the 
dilapidations  of  the  College  buildings,  with  the  result 
that  the  last  order  signed  by  Balderston  (August  7, 
1719)  declares  it  'necessary  to  pull  down  that  part  of  the 
College  called  y^Founders  range,"  and  appoints  two  of  the 
Fellows  (Whitaker  and  Whitehead)  to  superintend  the 
work  and  pay  the  workmen.  Not  many  days  after  this, 
Balderston,  having  retired  to  Peterborough,  of  which  he 
was  a  Prebend,  died  at  his  residence  there  (August 
28,  1719). 

One  other  order  signed  by  him  is  of  more  than 
common  interest.  It  is  dated  January  17,  1716-17: 
'  Whereas  Mr.  Law's  fellowship  was  by  act  of  parlia- 
ment vacated  in  January  last  year  upon  his  refusal  to 
take  the  oaths  mentioned  in  ye  said  act,  Dr.  Fletcher 
was  elected  into  his  place  on  the  31st  day  of  August 
last."*  This  is  the  celebrated  William  Law,  author 
of  the  Serious  Call  and  numerous  other  theological 
works.  He  had  once  before  been  suspended  from  his 
degrees  (1713)  for  a  sermon  which  favoured  the  Pre- 
tender, and  on  the  accession  of  George  I.  declined  the 
oath  of  allegiance  and  abjuration.  From  1727  to  1737 
he  lived  much  at  Putney,  first  as  tutor  to  Edward 
Gibbon,  father  of  the  historian,  whom  he  accompanied 
to  Cambridge  when  he  entered  Emmanuel  in  1727,  and 


1^4  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

afterwards  as  '  the  much-honoured  friend  and  spiritual 
director  of  the  whole  family.'  Law  was  elected  Fellow 
in  1711,  but  seems  to  have  resided  very  little,  and  his 
name  appears  at  the  foot  of  no  College  order.  During 
his  second  residence,  as  tutor  of  Gibbon,  he  of  course 
was  no  longer  one  of  the  governing  body.  He  resided 
finally  at  his  native  village  of  King's  Cliffe,  near  Stamford, 
accompanied  by  Mrs.  Hutcheson  and  Miss  Gibbon,  who 
formed  with  him  a  kind  of  religious  community,  and 
between  them  they  erected  almshouses  and  schools.  He 
died  April  9,  1761.  The  next  year  his  collected  works 
were  published  in  nine  volumes.  They  had  great 
influence  on  many  of  the  early  Evangelicals,  including 
the  Wesleys  and  Whitfield. 

Quite  a  different  character  was  Joshua  Barnes,  who 
entered  the  College  from  Christ's  Hospital  in  1671, 
became  Fellow  in  1678,  and  Greek  Professor  in  1695.  He 
was  a  most  indefatigable  writer  on  many  subjects  in 
English,  Greek,  and  Latin.  His  English  History  of 
Edward  III.  never  had  much  success,  and  has  long  since 
been  forgotten.  A  long  Latin  poem  on  his  wars,  Fran- 
dados,  on  the  model  of  the  Mneid,  was  never  pub- 
lished, though  two  MS.  copies  of  it,  extending  to  eight 
books,  are  in  the  College  library.  He  wrote  some  plays 
— which  also  remain  in  MS. — and  a  small  book  called 
Gerania,  which  is  in  some  degree  a  precursor  of  Swift's 
Lilliput,  and  a  paraphrase  of  the  Book  of  Esther  in 
Greek  verse.  He  wrote  Greek  verse  with  ease,  though 
sometimes  without  much  correctness,  and  many  epigrams 
are  preserved  in  the  library  addressed  to  his  brother 
Fellows,  the  Master,  and  others.  His  chief  works,  how- 
ever, were  editions  of  Euripides  (1694),  Anacreon  (1705), 


JOSHUA  BARNES  125 

and  Homer  (1710).  The  first  is  a  sumptuous  book, 
and  has  many  merits,  but  it,  unfortunately,  involved  him 
in  a  controversy  with  Bentley  as  to  the  genuineness  of 
certain  epistles  of  Euripides,  which  he  ventured  to  print. 
Monk,  in  his  Life  of  Bentley  (vol.  i.,  pp.  52-54),  of  course 
defends  his  hero,  and  says  that  his  criticisms  are  in  '  a 
lively  and  playful  strain.'  However  that  may  be  (the 
letter  is  printed  in  the  Museum  Criticum,  ii.  405), 
the  reasons  alleged  by  Bentley  are  not  as  weighty  and 
convincing  as  Monk  seems  to  think.  At  any  rate, 
Barnes  printed  the  letters,  and  characterized  anyone  who 
doubted  their  genuineness  as  either  'impudent  or 
foolish  **  ('  perfrictae  frontis  aut  iudicii  imminuti),  which 
Monk  accounts  for  by  supposing  that  Barnes  was  so 
strong  a  Jacobite  as  to  'regard  with  contempt  the 
opinions  and  scholarship  of  a  person  patronized  by  the 
deliverer''s  government.'  If  Bentley  had  been  as  polite 
and  reasonable  as  Monk  asserts  on  this  occasion,  he 
made  up  for  it  in  1710,  when  Barnes  published  his  Iliad 
and  Odyssey,  In  his  preface  he  complains  of  the  envy 
and  neglect  of  his  contemporaries,  and  especially  of  one 
Zoilus,  whom  he  calls  homo  inimkus.  This  was  supposed 
to  mean  Bentley,  who  retaliated  in  a  letter  to  Davies 
(Monk's  Bentley,  i.,  p.  293),  conceived  in  his  most 
brutal  vein.  Poor  Barnes  did  not  long  survive.  His 
Homer,  published  at  his  own,  or,  rather,  his  wife's  risk, 
proved  a  failure,  and  he  died  in  considerable  distress 
August  3,  1712.  His  epitaph  in  Hemingford  church- 
yard calls  him  7ro\vT6')(yo<;  and  Aoyoypvcpoyv  <f>epLaio^ 
avdo<i  re  tcop  dot,S  oov  t(ov  laiopcop  fi€yt,crTO<;  Kal  prjTopoyv 
dpi(7T0<i  Kol  /juavTeoyv  ^dOL<TTO<i  ^peravviKrjf;  dpovp7]<;, 
A  greater  scholar  than  Barnes  was  Richard  Dawes, 


126  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

who  entered  the  College  as  a  sizar  in  1725,  and  was  elected 
a  Dixie  Fellow  in  1731,  being  a  native  of  Market  Bos- 
worth  and  educated  at  the  school  there.  He,  too,  broke 
many  a  lance  with  Bentley,  whom  he  attacked  alive 
and  dead  in  his  Miscellanea  Critica  (1745).  But 
Dawes  was  in  other  respects  neither  a  successful  nor  a 
reputable  man.  His  career  as  Master  of  the  grammar- 
school  at  Newcastle  was  unfortunate,  and  he  died 
almost  insane  (1766). 

Edward  Chandler  (M.A.  169S),  after  holding 
numerous  preferments,  became  Bishop  of  Lichfield  in 
1717,  and  of  Durham  in  1730.  He  had  some  reputa- 
tion in  his  day  as  a  controversialist,  especially  as 
against  the  Deistic  writings  of  William  Collins.  He  is 
said  to  have  accumulated  a  large  fortune  by  his  Church 
preferments,  and  was  also  accused  of  having  given  a 
large  sum  for  the  see  of  Durham.  He  died  in  1750. 
Another  divine  of  some  distinction  was  Richard 
Arnald,  elected  Fellow  from  Corpus  in  1720,  and 
presented  in  1733  to  Thurcaston.  He  was  the  author, 
among  other  things,  of  a  commentary  on  the  Apocrypha, 
which  is  still  held  in  esteem. 


CHAPTER  VII 

MASTEESHIPS    OF    WILLIAM    SAVAGE     (1719-1736), 
AND  WILLIAM  KICHARDSON  (1736-1775) 

On  the  death  of  Balderston  on  August  28,  1719,  the 
Fellows  elected  George  Thorpe,  D.D.,  as  Master.  He 
had  been  elected  to  a  Fellowship  from  Caius  in  1663, 
one  of  the  six  selected  under  Sancroffs  influence,  with 
the  direct  purpose  of  modifying  the  Puritanical  character 
of  the  College.  He  does  not  appear  to  have  resided 
long,  and  he  had  since  been  Rector  of  Bishop's  Boum  in 
Kent  and  Prebendary  of  Canterbury.  He  is  best  known 
in  College  as  the  founder  of  the  Thorpe  Scholarships,  for 
which  he  was  for  many  years  (and  till  about  ten  years 
ago)  specially  commemorated  on  the  '  Thorpe  Day,'  in 
the  October  term.  The  founder's  cup  was  passed  round 
after  dinner,  each  member  standing  to  drink  the  port- 
wine  in  it  with  the  words,  'In  piam  memoriam  fun- 
datoris  nostri  et  benefactorum  nostrorum,  praecipue 
Doctoris  Thorpe.'  Under  the  new  arrangement  of  an 
annual  commemoration  his  name  no  longer  figures  so 
prominently.  Thorpe  declined  the  mastership,  probably 
on  account  of  his  age,  for,  as  he  took  his  M. A.  degree  in 
1660,  he  must  now  have  been  at  least  eighty,     There- 

127 


1S8  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

upon  the  Fellows  elected  William  Savage,  a  much 
younger  man,  for  he  was  M.A.  and  Fellow  in  1692. 
Savage  was  born  at  Ickleford  in  Hertfordshire,  was 
Rector  of  Gravesend  and  Stone  in  Kent,  and  had  taken 
his  D.D.  degree  in  1717.  He  was  admitted  Master  on 
September  26,  1719,  but  resigned  in  the  following 
year  in  order  to  be  presented  to  the  living  of  St.  Anne's, 
Blackfriars,  thus  evading  the  statute  forbidding  the 
Master  accepting  preferment,  and  he  was  readmitted 
on  November  4,  1720.  Two  leaves  have  been  torn 
from  the  order-book  referring  to  the  early  months 
of  this  year,  which  might  perhaps  have  thrown  some 
light  on  the  subject.  He  appears  to  have  been 
absent  in  London  much  of  his  first  year  of  office. 
There  is  an  order  of  November  4,  1719,  granting 
him  special  leave :  '  It  being  highly  necessary  that 
the  Master  should  be  in  London  this  winter  to 
sollicite  the  urgent  affairs  of  the  College.' 

Whatever  these  urgent  affairs  were,  whether  connected 
with  the  subscriptions  to  the  new  buildings*  or  with  suits 
at  law,  he  may  have  seen  reason  to  prefer  residence  in 
his  London  living.  The  numbers  were  fairly  maintained 
during  his  mastership,  averaging  about  twenty-five  entries 
in  the  year,  and  he  does  not  appear  to  have  had  much 
trouble  in  regard  to  discipline,  only  two  undergraduates 
being  recorded  as  admonished  by  him,  one  '  for  notorious 
deceit  put  upon  his  Tutor,'  and  another  'for  a  very 
gross  immorality  committed  by  him.'     But  there  are 

*  The  new  buildings  on  the  site  of  the  Founder's  Range,  ordered 
just  before  Balderston's  death,  were  ready  for  use  in  1722,  and 
subscriptions  were  obtained  amounting  to  /2,386  and  over,  of 
which  Lord  Westmorland  gave  /500. 


SAVAGE^S  MASTERSHIP  129 

two  entries  of  an  extraordinary  character,  which  would 
seem  to  show  that  he  had  difficulties  to  contend  with 
among  the  Fellows.  The  first  is  dated  August  24,  1725 : 
'  Mr.  Fuller,  senior  fellow  and  dean  of  the  College,  was 
admonished  by  the  master  in  the  presence  of  all  the 
Fellows  then  in  College  for  great  offences  he  was  guilty 
of.'  Whatever  this  peccant  Dean  had  done  or  left 
undone,  the  admonition  does  not  seem  to  have  had 
much  effect,  for  on  November  11,  1726,  we  again 
read : 

'Mr.  Fuller  being  summoned  did  appear  before  the 
Master  and  Senior  Fellows  and  Dean,  and  the  Master  pro- 
ceeded to  admonish  him  for  his  irregular  behaviour  and 
notorious  neglect  of  duty.  Upon  this  admonition  he 
behaved  himself  in  a  very  insolent  outrageous  manner, 
and  upon  the  Master  mentioning  that  he  admonished 
him  according  to  the  direction  of  the  Statutes  he  replied, 
A  f — t  for  your  admonition,  and  used  other  contemptuous 
language.' 

After  this  Savage  seems  to  have  given  up  admonishing 
anybody.  But  Fuller  was  in  some  way  got  rid  of,  for 
he  ceases  to  sign  College  orders,  which  are  now  more 
regularly  entered  in  the  book,  in  accordance  with  a 
special  order  to  that  effect  dated  March  24,  1720. 
Robert  Fuller,  a  native  of  King's  Lynn,  had  been  a 
Fellow  since  1719,  having  entered  the  College  in  1712, 
one  of  the  leanest  years  in  the  College  history,  when 
there  were  only  nine  admissions.  He  seems  to  have 
remained  in  residence  till  the  October  term  of  1 726,  and, 
as  he  was  Dean  in  the  previous  year,  he  must  have  been 
in  Holy  Orders ;  but  I  cannot  trace  his  after-career,  nor 
is  there  any  indication  of  the   misdemeanours  which 

9 


130  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

brought  down  upon  him  the  second  solemn  admonition. 
He  must  have  ceased  to  be  Fellow  before  January,  1729, 
when  another  Norfolk  Fellow  was  elected. 

During  the  rest  of  the  mastership  of  Savage,  the 
chief  signs  of  animation  in  the  College  is  an  increased 
care  for  the  library.  A  code  of  rules  as  to  the  borrow- 
ing of  books,  the  entries  to  be  made,  and  the  duties  of 
the  Librarian,  especially  as  to  cataloguing  the  books 
and  providing  writing  materials,  was  confirmed  on 
November  4,  1719.  Two  years  later  (April  10,  1721) 
an  order  was  passed  for  buying  books  with  the  money 
due  to  the  library  from  '  Commencing  Batchelors  and 
Masters  of  Arts,"  instead  of  leaving  it  in  the  hands  of 
the  steward,  to  be  spent  in  other  ways.  In  the  next 
year,  however,  an  order  was  found  necessary  for  calling 
in  all  books  and  instituting  a  strict  investigation  of  the 
state  of  the  library.  Apparently,  the  Librarian  was 
paid  so  small  a  salary  that  he  did  not  find  it  worth 
while  to  do  much  in  his  office,  for  in  1724  a  payment 
of  two  shillings  a  quarter  by  every  Fellow-commoner, 
one  shilling  by  every  pensioner,  and  sixpence  by  every 
sizar,  was  ordered,  to  raise  the  Librarian's  stipend  to  ddO 
per  annum,  the  balance  to  be  used  for  other  purposes 
in  the  library ;  and  in  1731  an  order  forbids  the  taking 
out  of  certain  books  of  reference  at  all,  and  limits  the 
time  for  keeping  other  books  to  one  month,  under  a 
fine  of  five  shillings. 

Another  department  of  the  College  equipment 
which  received  some  attention  was  the  plate.  An 
order  was  passed  in  1719  to  limit  the  issuing  of 
plate  to  members  of  the  College  for  their  use,  and 
to   forbid  anyone   'to  set  upon  or  over  the  fire  any 


SAVAGE'S  MASTERSHIP  131 

silver  drinking-pots  or  plates  belonging  to  y^  College 
for  y®  heating  or  warming  of  any  liquor,  or  for  any 
other  purpose';  and  another,  in  1723, directs  that  every- 
one taking  out  plate  must  give  a  written  receipt  for  it, 
and  return  it  clean.  Next  year  an  order  alludes  to  the 
breach  of  an  ancient  custom  of  the  College  by  Fellow- 
commoners  not  giving  a  piece  of  plate.  They  are  to 
do  so  in  future  through  their  Tutor,  to  the  value  of  £6 : 
an  order  confirmed  in  1730,  with  the  proviso  that  it 
is  to  be  done  within  six  months  of  admission.  This 
practice  accounts  for  much  now  in  possession  of  the 
College.  But  that  much  is  of  comparatively  recent 
date  is  partly  explained  by  an  order  of  February  8, 
1719-20,  for  the  'exchange  of  121  ounces  10  penny- 
weight for  new  and  more  useful  utensils.'  A  consider- 
able amount  also  was  lost  by  a  burglary  in  1723,  and 
some  more  in  1800  by  the  same  means. 

The  College  seems  in  this  period  to  have  had 
some  difficulty  with  a  very  important  officer,  the 
butler,  as  to  the  percentages  charged  by  him  on 
bread  and  other  provisions.  This  culminated  in  the 
'  removal '  of  the  manciple  or  butler  in  February,  1726, 
and  before  the  election  of  a  new  one  certain  conditions 
were  laid  down  :  The  new  butler  was  to  give  <^100 
security,  was  to  be  content  with  5  per  cent,  on  the 
price  of  bread  sold  in  the  buttery,  and  was  to  charge 
only  the  market  price  for  coals,  and  give  full  measure. 
If  the  butler  had  been  unsatisfactory,  so  had  the 
bedmakers.  They  seem  to  have  been  appointed  at 
haphazard,  either  by  the  Tutors  or  undergraduates, 
and  an  order  of  October  28,  1735,  remarks  that  '  owing 
to  the  number  lately  serving  in  the  College  many  things 

9—2 


13S  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

have  been  lost  and  other  inconveniences  have  arisen.' 
It  is  therefore  agreed  that  in  future  no  one  is  to  be 
brought  as  bedmaker  into  the  College  without  the 
consent  and  approbation  of  the  Master  and  Fellows. 

About  this  time  also  the  College  had  to  deal  with 
the  matter  of  the  Gillingham  Fellowship,  founded  in 
1728  by  the  will  of  Mr.  Gillingham,  as  interpreted  by 
a  decree  of  Chancery.  It  was  to  be  additional  to  the 
existing  twelve,  and  to  be  paid  by  the  estate  left  ex- 
pressly for  it.  An  order  of  April  19, 1732,  directs  that 
it  should  be  offered  to  the  senior  foundation  Fellows  in 
turn,  and  that  the  holder  should  have  all  the  privileges 
of  a  foundation  Fellow,  but  not  '  any  share  of  the  usual 
constant  and  standing  profits  and  emoluments '  of  the 
remaining  twelve.  This  order,  however,  was  revoked 
upon  a  vacancy  occurring  in  1751,  under  legal  advice, 
and  a  candidate  outside  the  society  was  elected  in  the 
ordinary  way.  This  continued  till  the  revision  of  1861, 
when  the  Fellowship  was  abolished,  and  the  income  of 
the  Gillingham  foundation  was  carried  to  the  general 
funds  of  the  College. 

Savage  died  on  August  1,  1736,  and  on  the  10th  of 
the  same  month  the  Fellows  elected  William  Richard- 
son, D.D.  He  had  been  a  scholar,  but  not  a  Fellow,  of 
the  College.  He  was,  however,  the  son  of  a  former 
Fellow,  John  Richardson  (1674-1685),  who  was  pre- 
sented to  North  Luffenham  in  1685,  and,  being  deprived 
as  a  non -juror  in  1690,  retired  to  AVithamstead  in 
Bedfordshire,  and  occupied  himself  in  composing  a 
learned  reply  to  the  deist  Toland.  His  son  William 
was  born  there  in  1698,  and  at  the  time  of  his  election 
to  the  mastership  was  a  Prebendary  and  Precentor  of 


RICHARDSON^S  MASTERSHIP  133 

Lincoln.  He  had  been  residing  in  Cambridge  in  order 
to  consult  the  libraries  for  his  chief  work,  an  edition  of 
Godwin's  De  Prcesulibus  Anglice  Commentarii  (1743), 
and  in  1735  had  taken  his  Doctor's  degree  and  had 
become  a  F.S.A.  His  election  seems  to  have  been  a 
surprise  to  himself,  and  was  effected  without  consulting 
him.  During  his  mastership  he  kept  up  his  anti- 
quarian habits,  and  set  laboriously  to  work  to  compile 
a  register,  not  only  of  his  own  College,  but  of  the  other 
Colleges  also.  The  book  of  'admissions,'  including 
lists  of  Master  and  Fellows  from  the  earliest  times, 
now  in  the  College  treasury,  embraces  King's  Hall 
and  Michael  House — afterwards  combined  in  Trinity — 
St.  John's,  King's,  Eton,  Pembroke,  and  a  statistical 
comparison  of  the  Colleges  of  Cambridge  and  Oxford. 
It  is  very  careful  and  neat,  and  must  have  cost  him 
great  labour.  For  Emmanuel  it  stops  at  1734,  but  as 
far  as  it  goes  it  was  the  foundation  of  '  Bennet's  Book,' 
and  is  of  great  value.  As  a  ruler  of  the  College  he 
must  have  been  exceedingly  businesslike  and  able,  but — 
as  his  portrait  would  seem  to  show — neither  genial  nor 
conciliatory. 

The  order-book  carefully  kept  during  his  mastership, 
and  mostly  in  his  own  clear  and  upright  handwriting, 
bears  ample  testimony  to  his  activity  and  ability;  but  it 
also  contains  the  record  of  more  than  01.3  contest 
between  him  and  a  large  part  of  the  Fellows,  leading  to 
written  protests,  and  sometimes  to  references  to  external 
arbitrators.  This  is  partly  to  be  accounted  for  by  the 
autocratic  position  of  the  Master.  The  funds  of  the 
College  were  almost  entirely  in  his  hands.  He  was 
practically   Bursar  as  well  as   Master,  and  when  the 


134  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

bursarship  became  important  and  regularly  filled  up, 
the  expression  used  at  first  was, '  The  office  of  Bursar  was 
delegated  by  the  Master  to  so  and  so.'  This  first  occurs 
after  Cory's  election  in  1797,  but  in  previous  master- 
ships the  office  was  not  important,  and  until  1756  was 
unpaid.  An  order  of  May  5  in  that  year  assigns 
£^0  a  year  to  him  for  the  future  '  in  consideration  of 
his  trouble."*  It  was  not,  however,  till  forty-eight  years 
later  that  it  was  raised  to  ^^40  (October  26,  1804),  at 
which  sum  it  remained  for  fourteen  years,  when  it  was 
doubled  (1818).  Still,  it  was  looked  upon  as  naturally 
attaching  to  the  mastership,  and  in  1809  the  Master  is 
compensated  by  £Q0  a  year  as  long  as  he  delegates  the 
office. 

The  increased  importance  of  the  bursarship  began 
with  the  closing  years  of  Farmer's  mastership,  who  was 
a  poor  man  of  business,  and  left  the  accounts  between 
himself  and  the  College  in  such  confusion  that  the 
Bursar  (Tyson)  had  the  greatest  difficulty  in  coming  to 
a  settlement  with  his  heirs.  A  College  order  of  June  20, 
1799,  requests  Tyson  (who  had  just  taken  a  living)  to 
accept  a  piece  of  plate  of  twenty  guineas  value  in  con- 
sideration of  the  extraordinary  trouble  which  he  experi- 
enced in  settling  the  accounts  of  the  College  with  the 
administrator  of  the  late  Master,  as  well  as  his  zealous 
attention  to  the  interests  of  the  society  on  other  occa- 
sions. It  is  not  surprising,  therefore,  that  the  Fellows 
after  Farmer's  death  wished  the  bursarship  to  be 
separated  from  the  mastership. 

But  in  Richardson's  time  the  Master  still  held  the 
money  of  the  society,  and  represented  it  in  all  matters 
of  business.     Nor  did  he  prove  unfaithful  or  unskilful. 


RICHARDSON'S  MASTERSHIP  135 

He  at  once  took  drastic  measures  to  recover  the  finances 
of  the  College.  Among  the  earliest  orders  signed  by 
him  (February  5,  1737)  is  one  to  suspend  the  election 
to  Fellowships  till  the  number  is  reduced  to  seven, 
owing  to  lawsuits  and  debts  contracted  for  rebuilding 
the  Founder's  Range  and  other  causes.  Two  years  later, 
though  three  Fellowships  were  filled  up,  three  were  still 
suspended.  But  in  1742  an  order  of  December  2 
declares  the  College  debt  to  be  'considerably  dimin- 
ished,' and  in  1745  an  order  of  April  15  declares  the 
College  to  be  entirely  out  of  debt,  and  directs  the  two 
vacant  Fellowships  to  be  filled  up.  About  this  time, 
accordingly,  some  minor  improvements  were  made.  In 
1745  money  was  given  by  a  Mr.  Barlow  for  the  use  of 
the  gardens,  with  which  the  bath  was  constructed,  and 
money  which  had  been  borrowed  from  the  library  was 
repaid,  and  the  interest  devoted  to  its  use.  But  though 
the  state  of  the  revenue  was  satisfactory,  there  seems  to 
have  been  some  difficulty  in  meeting  extraordinary 
expenses.  An  order  of  June  22,  1752,  affirms  the 
necessity  of  rebuilding  the  butteries  and  contiguous 
buildings,  which  had  become  '  very  ruinous."*  Mr. 
Burroughs  is  to  be  desired  to  prepare  a  plan,  Mr.  Devie 
(who  managed  the  supply  of  coal)  is  to  be  associated 
with  the  Master  in  carrying  out  the  plan,  and  a 
hope  is  expressed  that  the  expenses  will  be  in  part,  at 
least,  met  by  benefactions.  But  either  the  benefactions 
were  slow  in  coming  in,  or  other  needs  intervened. 

In  1760  the  alterations  in  the  hall,  according  to  the 
plan  of  Essex,  were  agreed  to,  and  in  1762  there  is  an 
order  for  borrowing  ^£^300  to  meet  the  'considerable 
reparations  and  other  works '  going  on,  and  because  the 


136  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

public  stocks  were  so  low  as  to  make  it  imprudent  to 
sell  out  (order-book,  February  3,  1762).      Meanwhile 
Mr.  Devie   had   gone  off  to  Standground  and  Farcet 
(1766),   and    three   years    later   (March   11,   1769)   a 
College  order  again  records  the  ruinous  condition  of  the 
buildings  '  opposite  the  chappel,'  and  directs  that  '  the 
butteries  and  buildings  running   from   thence  to   the 
street  be  immediately  pulled  down,  and  rebuilt  according 
to  the  plan  drawn  by  Sir  James  Burroughs  some  years 
since.'     The  actual  work,  however,  was  not  begun  at 
once,  and  a  new  plan  drawn  by  Essex   was   accepted 
on  July  5,  1769,  and  was  ordered  to  be  carried  into 
execution  '  when  the  College  shall  be  enabled  to  do  it.' 
Probably   some   disputes   arose   as   to  the  method   of 
raising  money.     Over  ,£'2,800  had  been  received  from 
subscriptions  for  the  new  building,  but  when  the  last 
payments  came  to  be  made  in  the  year  after  Richard- 
son's death,  a  heavier  debt  existed   than  the  College 
could   meet   by   its   ordinary   income.      An   order    of 
November  17,  1770,  for  borrowing  ^£^200  is  marked  by 
the  Master  *  Not  done';  but  on  January  24, 1772,  there 
is   an   order  for  selling   out  ^00  Bank   consolidated 
annuities ;  on  March  14, 1773,  the  Master  is  empowered 
to  borrow  ^£^500  on  lives  for  the  new  building ;  and  on 
March  16,  1776,  a  College  order  declares  an  insigne 
detrimentum  occasioned  by  the  expenses  attending  the 
erection  of  the  new  building,  and  suspends  election  to 
Fellowships.     In   1778,  however,  came   a  considerable 
legacy  from  Hubbard,  by  which  an  order  of  May  13 
declares  the   College  income   to   be   'considerably  in- 
creased.'    The  Master  received   an  additional   ^£^40   a 
year,  each  of  the  four  senior  Fellows  ^10.     The  insigrie 


RICHARDSON'S  MASTERSHIP  137 

detrimentum  was  pronounced  at  an  end,  and  the  society 
went  on  almost  immediately  to  elect  four  Fellows. 
Another  heavy  sum' which  the  College  was  called  upon 
to  find  about  the  same  time  was  over  ^£^1,100  for 
the  enclosure  of  the  Manor  of  Shernbourn  in  Norfolk 
(the  Ash  Estate).  This  money  was  advanced  by  a  late 
Fellow  R.  Richardson  (son  of  the  Master),  who  was  not 
paid  off  finally  till  December,  1778.  But  the  Master, 
William  Richardson,  seemed  to  have  steered  the  College 
skilfully  through  these  difficulties,  and  the  value  of  its 
property  continued  steadily  to  increase. 

In  the  matter  of  discipline  he  would  seem  to 
have  found  it  necessary  to  use  a  somewhat  tighter 
hand  than  his  predecessor.  The  number  of  admoni- 
tions, perhaps,  is  not  excessive  (32  in  40  years), 
though  rather  above  the  average  ;  but  they  are 
generally  for  more  serious  offences :  drunkenness,  stay- 
ing out  one  or  more  nights,  entertaining  immoral 
persons  in  College  rooms,*  and  once,  at  least,  for  a 
criminal  assault  upon  a  woman  by  a  scholar — a  case 
which  even  found  its  way  into  the  law-courts,  and  is 
twice  noted :  once  in  the  order-book,  suspending  the 
offender  from  his  scholarship ;  and  later  on  in  the 
admonition-book,  recording  his  expulsion.  Two  orders 
of  October,  1740,  also  refer  to  'the  imprudence  of 
young  scholars  in  contracting  debts  with  persons  of  the 
town.'  The  first  order  threatens  any  servant  of  the 
College  with  loss  of  his  place  if  he  trusts  undergraduates 
and  then  arrests  them  for  debt  without  leave  from  the 

*  A  College  order  of  November  23,  1739,  offers  a  reward  of  £^  to 
anyone  who  shall  discover  such  a  person  in  any  chamber  of  the 
College. 


138  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

society ;  the  second  threatens  a  scholar  with  admonition 
or  rustication  for  contracting  a  debt  of  20  shillings 
at  alehouses  or  inns,  or  with  any  tradesman  without 
the  knowledge  of  his  Tutor.  The  quarrel  as  to  the 
relief  of  the  Dean,  Lecturer,  and  Steward  from  treating 
the  society  to  wine  and  tobacco  on  the  day  of  their 
election  (in  the  course  of  which  Richardson  solemnly 
revoked  an  order  signed  by  four  Fellows)  was  settled  a 
fortnight  later  (May  25,  1747)  by  a  regular  order, 
signed  by  him  and  four  Fellows,  abolishing  the 
practice. 

These  were  yearly  offices,  but  in  this  century  a  change 
had  come  upon  the  system  of  managing  the  under- 
graduates, partly,  perhaps,  because  of  the  reduced 
numbers,  namely,  by  the  appointment  of  a  single  or 
Senior  Tutor.  Up  to  the  end  of  the  seventeenth  century 
it  seems  that  any  of  the  Fellows  could  act  as  Tutor  to 
one  or  more  undergraduates.  They  were  not  formally 
appointed,  and  were  only  recognised  as  the  Tutors  of 
such  and  such  men.  As  late  as  the  first  quarter  of  the 
eighteenth  century  the  appointment  of  a  '  Tutor  of  the 
College '  is  not  entered  in  the  order-book  with  the  other 
officers.  The  Master  probably  assigned  the  men  to  him, 
and  he  thus  became  sole  Tutor.  John  Wilkinson  (Fellow 
1728),  afterwards  Rector  of  Wallington  and  Master  of 
the  Birmingham  School,  seems  to  have  been  the  first  to 
occupy  that  position,  in  which  he  was  succeeded  by  Henry 
Hubbard  in  1736.  Hubbard  remained  Tutor  till  1767, 
and  this  long  tenure  of  the  tutorial  duties  doubtless 
helped  to  confirm  the  arrangement.  He  was  a  Suffolk 
man,  elected  Fellow  in  1732,  and  was  Lady  Margaret 
Preacher,  and  University  Registrary  from  1758  to  his 


HUBBARD'S  TUTORSHIP  139 

death  in  1778.  He  has  left  a  very  complete  record 
of  his  tutorship,  from  which  a  good  idea  of  the  state 
of  the  College  —  then  at  a  very  low  ebb — may  be 
collected. 

He  had  been  a  Conduct  Fellow  of  St.  Catharine's, 
where  his  brother  Edward  was  one  of  the  senior  Fellows, 
but  was  afterwards  elected  to  a  Fellowship  at  Emmanuel ; 
four  years  later  he  was  appointed  to  the  tutorship, 
which  he  held  for  thirty-one  years,  handing  it  over  to 
Dr.  Farmer  in  October,  1767.  His  long  and  active 
tutorship,  and  the  benefactions  which  he  left  at  his 
death,  have  combined  to  make  him  a  considerable  figure 
in  the  domestic  history  of  the  College.  Although  he 
published  nothing  except  one  Charity  sermon,  he  was 
an  active  and  accurate  penman,  and  among  the  books 
which  he  bequeathed  to  the  library  were  a  number 
of  volumes  of  MS.  collections,  which  are  still  pre- 
served in  the  library  and  the  treasury.  Among  these 
there  are  an  elaborate  catalogue  of  his  own  books,  a 
copy  of  the  College  statutes  with  extracts  from  early 
order-books  and  other  sources,  a  copy  of  the  University 
statutes,  a  copy  of  Buck's  Book  of  University/  Cere- 
monies and  Precedents,  a  copy  of  Hare's  Collection  of 
University  Charters  and  other  Documents,  his  accounts 
as  Steward  of  the  College,  and  a  book  entitled  A  Book 
of  Accounts. 

This  last  contains  a  list  of  all  admissions  of  under- 
graduates from  January,  1733,  to  July,  1767.  The 
entries  are  brief  for  the  first  year  or  two,  but  from  1736 
onward  (that  is,  during  the  period  of  Hubbard's  tutor- 
ship) the  list  gives  much  more  information  than  any 
other  early  admission-book  which  has  been  preserved. 


140  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

The  following  may  be  taken  as  a  typical  entry :  '  1753, 
Richard,  son  of  Richard  Farmer,  Woolman  and  Malster, 
born  at  Leicester,  educated  at  Leicester  School,  Mr. 
Andrewes  M*",  aetat.  19,  Pens^**  There  is  a  full  account 
of  the  caution  money  received  and  returned  by  Hubbard 
as  Tutor  from  October  18,  1736,  when  he  took  over  his 
]3upils  from  his  predecessor,  AVilkinson,  until  October  3, 
1767,  when  what  caution  money  he  had  in  hand  was 
handed  over  to  his  successor.  Farmer.  In  a  statement 
of  the  amount  of  tuition  fees  received  in  each  year  of 
his  tutorship  from  1735  to  1742  the  names  of  his 
pupils  are  given.  There  are,  further,  statements  of  the 
receipts  from  his  Fellowship  from  1732  to  1744,  of  his 
income  as  Steward  (a  yearly  office)  1737-38,  1743- 
44,  and  1758-59,  of  his  income  as  Lecturer  1737-38 
and  1743-44,  as  Dean  1737-38,  1744-45,  and  1758-59, 
and  as  Father  (Praelector)  1736-37.  He  held  various 
University  offices  in  his  time,  and  has  included  in  his 
Book  of  Accounts  a  statement  of  his  receipts  as 
Moderator  1732-33  and  1734-35,  as  Taxor  1733-34 
and  1735-36,  and  as  Scrutator  1744-45  and  1757-58. 
Lastly  there  are  included  the  detailed  accounts  of  the 
Registrary's  office  from  July,  1758,  to  October,  1772. 

Taking  the  thirty-five  years  from  1733  to  1767, 
we  find  on  Hubbard's  list  of  admissions  a  total  of 
334  names,  giving  an  average  of  9*5  admissions  per 
year.  The  largest  number  of  admissions  in  any  one 
year  was  18,  in  1759  ;  the  smallest,  1,  in  1756.  It 
ought,  however,  to  be  remembered  that  these  numbers 
do  not  necessarily  represent  the  number  of  freshmen 
actually  coming  into  residence  in  the  years  named,  as 
in  many  cases  names  were  entered  on  the  boards  some 


HUBBARD^S  TUTORSHIP  141 

considerable  time  before  residence  began.  The  numbers 
in  residence  are  also  increased  by  the  incorporation  of 
Bachelors  from  Oxford,  which  explains  the  divergence 
between  the  numbers  given  by  Hubbard  and  those 
given  by  Bennet.  In  Rennet's  register  the  number 
of  admissions  for  the  same  thirty-five  years  amounts  to 
376,  an  average  of  10*7  per  year,  or  1  a  year  more  than 
Hubbard's.  But  in  Bennet's  list  we  find  3  Bachelors 
from  Oxford  in  1737,  3  in  1760,  and  3  again  in 
1761 ;  while  there  are  2  of  them  in  the  lean  year, 
1756.  The  incorporation  of  Bachelors  from  Oxford 
was  caused  by  the  fact  that  at  Oxford  it  was  necessary 
to  sign  the  Thirty-nine  Articles  for  the  M.A.  degree, 
which  it  was  not  necessary  to  do  at  Cambridge. 

In  the  quarterly  statement  of  tuition  fees  Hubbard 
includes  all  those  in  residence  other  than  Fellows  of  the 
College,  although  no  fees  seem  to  have  been  charged  to 
Master  and  Doctors.  In  the  quarter  ending  Lady 
Day,  1743,  there  are  resident  5  Fellow-commoners, 
6  Bachelors,  8  scholars,  13  pensioners,  and  13  sizars,  or 
45  in  all ;  for  the  quarter  ending  Lady  Day,  1753,  the 
numbers  are  1  Doctor  of  Medicine,  5  Fellow-commoners, 
3  Bachelors,  5  scholars,  19  pensioners,  and  5  sizars,  or 
38  in  all ;  and  for  the  quarter  ending  Lady  Day,  1763, 
1  Doctor  of  Medicine,  2  Masters  of  Arts,  10  Fellow- 
commoners,  3  Bachelors,  12  pensioners,  and  6  sizars,  or 
40  in  all.  For  the  twenty-four  years  from  1743  to 
1766  the  average  number  of  residents,  other  than 
Fellows,  was  43,  although  the  number  fell  as  low  as  30 
in  1751. 

Of  the  334  undergraduates  enrolled  in  Hubbards 
admission-register  in  the  years  from  1733  to  1767,  as 


142  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

many  as  254  have  the  names  of  their  schools  recorded. 
During  that  time  18  entered  from  Oakham  School, 
18  from  Bury  St.  Edmunds,  16  from  Eton,  15  from 
Nuneaton,  14  from  Westminster,  and  11  from  Leicester. 
The  connexion  with  Oakham  School  was  probably 
due  to  the  existence  of  the  Johnson  Exhibitions,  but 
Uppingham,  which  is  also  included  in  the  Johnson 
Foundation,  sent  only  2  in  the  thirty-five  years. 
Bury  St.  Edmunds  was  evidently  at  that  time  the 
leading  school  for  Norfolk  and  Suffolk,  from  which 
Emmanuel  has  always  been  largely  recruited;  but  it 
had  evidently  also  a  wider  fame,  as  a  fair  proportion  of 
the  18  from  Bury  were  sons  of  professional  men  in 
London.  Nuneaton  owes  its  position  in  the  list  to  the 
influence  of  Mr.  Thomas  Liptrot,  the  Headmaster,  who 
was  elected  a  Fellow  of  Emmanuel  in  1732,  and  soon 
after  was  appointed  to  that  school. 

Next  in  order  of  the  schools  come  Market  Street 
(near  Luton)  and  Dedham  (Essex),  with  7  each,  Repton 
with  6,  and  Mitcham  with  5.  It  may  be  mentioned 
that  of  the  5  from  Mitcham  3  are  born  in  Barbadoes, 
the  sons  respectively  of  the  Hon.  Walter  Caddell, 
Treasurer  of  the  island,  of  Samuel  Adams,  Esq.,  and 
of  John  Thomas,  merchant.  A  fourth  was  Edward 
Evanson,  the  son  of  Thomas  Evanson,  mercer,  of 
Warrington,  and  a  nephew  of  Mr.  Evanson,  the 
Headmaster  of  Mitcham.  Edward  Evanson,  whose 
age,  on  admission  in  1746,  is  entered  as  *  15  next  April,' 
became  later  on  a  noted  theological  controversialist, 
and  was  deprived  of  his  preferment  in  the  Church  '  for 
omitting  part  of  the  Litany  and  delivering  doctrines 
against    the    Incaiiiation."'      He   was    for  a  time   an 


HUBBARD^S  TUTORSHIP  143 

assistant-master  in  his  uncle^s  school  at  Mitcham,  and 
after  leaving  the  Church  he  himself  canied  on  a  suc- 
cessful school  there. 

Eight  schools  sent  each  4  representatives  to  Em- 
manuel in  these  thirty-five  years  :  Harrow,  Felstead, 
Winchester,  Sherborn,  Market  Bosworth,  Norwich, 
Ipswich,  and  Brewood  (Staffs).  John  Gayland,  the 
Headmaster  of  Sherborn,  was  elected  a  Fellow  of 
Emmanuel  in  1713,  was  presented  to  the  College 
living  of  Winsford  in  Somerset  in  1714,  and  was 
soon  after  appointed  to  Sherborn,  where  he  died  in 
1743. 

Nine  schools  sent  3  each,  among  them  Newcastle 
— where  Richard  Dawes  was  Master — Derby,  Bishop 
Stortford,  and  Clitheroe ;  while  among  the  seventeen 
schools  which  are  represented  by  2  entries  appear 
St.  Paul's,  Charterhouse,  Tunbridge,  Merchant  Taylors'*, 
Uppingham,  and  Wakefield.  Forty-four  schools  appear 
with  one  representative  only — many  of  them  no  doubt 
small  grammar-schools,  many  probably  private  schools, 
but  on  this  list  also  appear  Rugby  and  Sedbergh. 

Another  point  of  interest  is  the  geographical  distri- 
bution of  the  members  of  the  College  at  this  time.  We 
can  assign  the  native  county  to  300  out  of  the  334 
on  Mr.  Hubbard's  list.  A  rough  classification  of  the 
counties  gives  the  following  result : 

Midland  Counties,  95  :  Leicester  42,  Stafford  13,  Derby  10, 
Notts  9,  Northants  9,  Warwick  5,  Rutland  4,  Oxford  1, 
Bucks  1,  and  Berks  1. 

Eastern  Counties,  11 :  Suffolk  28,  Lincoln  14,  Norfolk  11, 
Hunts  6,  Hertford  6,  Essex  4,  Cambridge  4,  and  Bed- 
ford 4» 


144  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

Middlesex,  53. 

Noiihern  Counties,  27:  Yorks  12,  Lancashire  6,  North- 
umberland 4,  Westmorland  2,  Cumberland  2,  and  Dur- 
ham 1. 

South-Western  Counties,  18;  Somerset  6,  Dorset  5, 
Devon  4,  Wilts  2,  and  Cornwall  1. 

Soidh- Eastern  Cotmties,  17 :  Kent  8,  Surrey  Q,  and 
Sussex  3. 

West  Indies,  5:  Barbadoes  3,  Jamaica  1,  Antigua 
(Antegoa)  1. 

Scotland,  2. 

India,  2 :  Bombay. 

Ireland,  1. 

Wales,  1  :  Denbigh. 

Portugal,  1  :  Lisbon. 

America,  1  :  Maryland. 

It  is  not  easy  to  explain  the  predominance  of  the 
Midland  counties  in  this  list,  but  probably  one  of  the 
causes  was  the  existence  of  special  relations  with  the 
schools  or  schoolmasters  at  Oakham,  Nuneaton,  and 
Leicester. 

The  entry  relating  to  the  solitary  representative  from 
Cornwall  is  worth  quoting.     It  is : 

'1738,  July  7.  John  Taperall,  born  in  Cornwall,  an 
exciseman,  27  years  old,  formerly  of  Exeter  College,  Sizar. 
N.B.  This  person  was  admitted  in  an  hurry  to  save  the 
year,  but  upon  further  talking  with  him  I  had  some  reason 
to  think  him  a  little  disordered  in  his  senses,  and  there- 
fore advised  to  take  out  his  name,  which  he  did  y®  next 
day.' 

Another  instance  of  hurry  '  to  save  the  year '  is  to  be 
found   in    1739,  when   on   July   6   Neville  King   was 


HUBBARD^S  TUTORSHIP  145 

admitted  'before  y^  Bell  rang,'  but  John  Adcock  on 
the  same  day  was  admitted  '  after  y^  Bell  rang.' 

The  usual  age  for  admission  was  seventeen  or  eighteen, 
but  one  or  two  were  entered  at  an  earlier  age.  Edward 
Evanson,  who  was  to  be  *  1 5  next  April,'  has  already 
been  mentioned,  and  the  following  entry  occurs  in 
1745:  'Sept.  26,  Robert,  Son  of  y«  Rev.  D^  William 
Richardson,  Master  of  Emmanuel  College,  born  in 
London,  Middlesex,  educated  at  Westminster  School, 
Dr  Nichols,  Master,  anno  aetat.  15*°.'' 

In  one  case  at  least  Hubbard  was  Tutor  to  both 
father  and  son.  On  July  6,  1735,  Mr.  John  Hind 
Cotton  was  admitted  as  Fellow-commoner.  This  was 
just  a  year  before  Hubbard  took  up  the  office,  and  in 
1766,  a  year  before  he  laid  it  down,  he  admitted 
'  John,  son  of  Sir  John  Hynde  Cotton,  Bart.,  born  in 
St.  James'  Parish,  Westminster,  educated  at  West- 
minster School,  D""  South  M^",  aet.  18.' 

Nonconformists,  though  not  unknown,  were  rare  in 
the  University  in  those  days,  and  the  form  in  which 
Hubbard  recorded  the  admission  of  Edward  Calamy  is 
some  evidence  of  this.  In  1763,  on  September  30, 
there  was  admitted  as  Fellow-commoner  '  Edmund,  son 
of  Edward  Calamy  (a  Non-conformist  Divine),  bom  in 
Middlesex,  educated  at  Warrington,  Lancashire,  M^ 
Akin  Mr,  aetat.  21.'  Bennet,  who  was  admitted  to 
Emmanuel  in  the  same  year  as  Calamy,  notes  him  in 
the  register  which  he  afterwards  compiled,  as  the 
grandson  of  the  celebrated  Puritan.  Calamy — who  was 
afterwards  a  barrister  at  Lincoln's  Inn — was,  in  fact,  the 
fifth  Edmund  in  the  direct  line  of  descent;  the  first 

10 


146  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

(1600-1666)  was  a  noted  disputant  in  the  days  of  the 
ParHament,  and  was  one  of  the  authors  of  Smec- 
tymnuus.  He  was  a  Pembroke  man.  His  grandson, 
the  grandfather  of  our  Edmund,  was  the  well-known 
historian  of  Nonconformity.  The  sixth  Edmund 
Calamy,  son  of  the  barrister,  was  bom  in  1780,  and 
died  in  1850. 

According  to  Hubbard,  the  stipend  of  an  ordinary 
Fellow  of  the  College  from  the  general  revenues  of  the 
College  was  £5  in  each  half-year.  The  four  senior 
Fellows  had  an  additional  £S  in  each  half-year  from 
the  Pinchbeck  Estates.  The  revenues  from  certain 
trust  estates,  as  those  left  by  Dean  Sudbury  and 
Dr.  Whichcote,  and  the  annual  payment  by  the  Borough 
of  Lynn,  are  divided  in  four  proportions  between  the 
Master,  the  Fellows,  and  the  Scholars.  Fines  for  the 
renewal  of  leases  and  sealing  fees  are  also  divided. 
Under  the  old  leases  several  of  the  College  tenants  are 
obliged  to  furnish  to  the  College  a  certain  quantity  of 
corn  or  a  certain  number  of  capons,  usually  four  in  the 
half-year,  or  to  pay  an  equivalent,  at  half  a  crown  a 
capon  in  cash.  This  was  called  '  capon  money ,"*  and  was 
similarly  divided.  The  revenue  from  underwoods  at 
Gransden  and  Ellerby  was  also  kept  separate,  and 
dealt  with  in  this  way  ;  and  the  admission  fees,  the 
buttery  profits,  and  certain  payments  made  by  Bachelors 
on  attaining  their  degrees,  were  also  divided.  These 
explanations  may  help  to  make  Hubbard's  statement 
of  his  income  intelligible :

Hubbard  has  left  a  complete  statement  of  his  in- 
come from  all  these  sources  for  each  year  from  1732 
to  1733.  His  average  income  as  Fellow  during  that 
time  was  less  than  .^38  a  year.  It  was  as  much  a^  ^50  in 
1769  and  in  1773,  and  sank  as  low  as  £9.^  6s.  7d.  in 
1748.  In  1764  it  increased  suddenly  to  .^48  10s.  4Jd., 
a  circumstance  which  is  alluded  to  in  Bennet's  note 
quoted  below.  Though  this  seems  very  small,  it  is  not 
so  when  compared  with  the  whole  College  income.  In 
a  letter  of  Dillingham's  to  Sancroft  of  1665,  he  mentions 
that  at  the  time  of  the  Ash  benefaction  (1654)  the 
revenues  of  the  College  amounted  to  ^550.  Nor  does 
it  seem  to  have  risen  much  in  the  next  100  years, 
for  in  a  statement  of  income  for  taxation  in  April, 
1799,  it  *  was  estimated,  according  to  the  directions  of 
39  George  III.,  that  the  sum  of  forty-eight  pounds  is 
not  less  than  one  tenth  thereof.'  The  first  indication 
of  a  substantial  advance  is  in  1822,  when,  owing  to  '  the 
progressive  improvement  of  the  income  of  y®  College,' 
the  Master  received  an  additional  ^120  and  each  Fellow 
<£>30  per  annum. 


HUBBARD'S  TUTORSHIP  149 

Hubbard  also  held  the  Algebra  Lecturership  from  1746 
to  1768,  with  a  stipend  of  about  £24*.  He  was  Steward 
thrice,  Dean  thrice.  Lecturer  twice,  Praelector  once. 
As  an  instance  of  the  care  with  which  he  kept  these 
accounts,  it  may  be  mentioned  that  in  1763  appears  the 
first  entry  of  a  receipt  of  Is.  8d.  for  '  Visiting.'  To  this 
entry  there  is  the  following  note:  *N.B.  I  have  not 
brought  this  article  to  account  in  this  book  before,  but 
deducted  it  for  Pocket  Expenses."*  During  a  part  of 
1741  he  records  that  he  was  allowed  ^1  for  commons 
*  as  being  in  College,  but  obliged  to  live  at  Catherine 
Hall."*  The  reason  of  his  temporary  exile  does  not 
appear,  but  he  was  actively  engaged  in  College  business 
throughout  the  year,  and  signed  all  the  orders. 

Reference  has  been  made  to  the  dividend  arising  from 
profits  in  the  buttery.  An  entry  in  the  order-book  of 
date  March  1,  1734,  says:  '  Agreed  that  no  fellow  shall 
be  entitled  to  his  Dividend  in  the  Butteries,  who  is  not 
in  commons  and  actually  resident  the  major  part  of  the 
month.'  Bennet  in  his  register  has  a  note  on  the 
'  Money  divided  among  the  Fellows  from  the  savings  in 
the  Butteries  and  Degree  Money  for  twenty  years,  1760 
to  1779.  (N.B.  The  College  for  half  this  time  was 
very  thin,  and  the  other  half  very  full.)'  He  states  the 
average  for  that  time  as  £35  from  the  butteries  and 
£S2  from  degrees,  and  says  that  the  increase  in  1764 
was  attributed  to  the  fact  that  the  College  raised  the 
price  of  beer.     He  goes  on  to  say : 

'By  an  average  this  makes  a  difference  to  a  Resident 
Fellow  of  £10  per  annum;  so  that  supposing  a  Man  to 
reside  the  whole  twenty  years  he  will  receive  an  addition 
to  his  Fellowship  of  £200,  no  contemptible  sum.    Yet  it  is 


150  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

curious  enough  that  a  great  part  of  the  Society  are  ignorant 
from  what  these  Dividends  arise,  and  whether  they  are 
imposed  upon  or  no  by  the  Butler  in  stating  them.' 

As  to  the  University  offices  of  Moderator,  Taxor, 
and  Regis trary  which  were  held  by  Hubbard,  it  is  of 
interest  to  know  that  at  Stourbridge  Fair  in  1730  the 
Taxor  received  £4i  5s.  3d.  for  'weighing  Hops  and 
measuring  Mustard  Seed,'  that  of  this  15s.  4|d.  was 
spent  on  'Bottled  Ale,  Ale,  and  Small,'  and  6d.  in 
coach  hire  from  the  fair. 

At  the  end  of  the  volume  which  contains  the  catalogue 
of  his  books,  Mr.  Hubbard  entered  a  careful  inventory 
of  the  furniture  of  his  College  rooms.   The  list  includes  : 

'Picture  of  Mr.  Canning,  by  Gainsborough,  valued  at 
£5  5s.,  the  frame  i>l  lis.  6d. ;  small  picture  of  Mr.  Canning 
in  black  and  white  by  Ferguson,  15s.;  my  picture  by 
Heines  £5  5s.,  frame  £l  lis.  6d.  ;  a  long  Telescope,  present 
from  Mr.  Canning,  cost  2"^*^  hand  £3  3s.  ;  cane  with  gold 
head,  present  from  Sir  Richard  Chase,  1757  ;  and  a  Stick 
with  amber  head  mounted  in  silver,  present  from  Green 
of  Preston  near  Shields,  1758.* 

Sir  Richard  Chase  was  an  old  pupil  who  came  up 
from  Bishop's  Stortford  School  in  1739,  the  son  of 
Richard  Chase,  gentleman,  of  Much  Haddam,  Hert- 
fordshire. Mr.  Canning  was  Hubbard's  great  friend,  a 
member  of  Catharine  Hall,  where  he  graduated  B.A.  in 
1728,  perpetual  curate  of  St.  Lawrence,  Ipswich,  Rector 
of  Harkstead  and  of  Freston,  and  Vicar  of  Ruston.  He 
was  a  keen  student  of  local  history,  translated  and 
edited  the  Ipswich  charters,  and  rewrote  and  edited  a 
county  history,  published  as  The  Suffolk  Traveller.    He 


HUBBARD^S  TUTORSHIP  151 

died  in  1775.  Among  the  admissions  in  1758  is 
recorded  that  of  'Richard,  son  of  the  Revd.  Richard 
Canning,  born  at  Ipswich,  Suffolk,  educated  at  Bury 
School,  Mr  Garnham  M%  aet.  18."  The  younger 
Richard  succeeded  his  father  as  Rector  of  Harkstead  in 
1769,  and  died  twenty  years  later.  A  letter,  which 
lies  loose  in  the  catalogue  of  Hubbard'^s  books,  refers 
to  his  will :  '  And  I  give  and  bequeath  to  the  Master, 
Fellows  and  Scholars  of  Emmanuel  College  in  Cam- 
bridge, my  picture  of  the  late  Rev.  Henry  Hubbard, 
together  with  the  books  bequeathed  by  him  to  me.' 
The  picture  is  now  in  the  gallery. 

At  his  death,  January  23,  1778,  Hubbard  left  ^400 
to  provide  an  augmentation  to  the  Fellowship  held  by 
the  senior  Fellow  of  the  College ;  also  .^400,  the  interest 
of  which  is  to  be  paid  to  the  best  proficient  in  classical 
learning  among  Dr.  Thorpe's  scholars ;  and  i;^200,  the 
interest  to  be  added  to  the  £6  left  by  Dean  Sudbury,  for  a 
piece  of  plate  to  be  bestowed  upon  the  most  pious  and 
best  learned  of  the  commencing  Bachelors  of  Arts  in 
the  College.  He  also  made  the  College  his  residuary 
legatee,  and  thus  it  received  some  ^4,000  in  addition 
to  the  <£1,000  given  to  special  objects,  and  this,  as 
Bennet  puts  it, 

'  by  mere  good  fortune,  for  it  was  always  Mr.  Hubbard's 
intention  (having  no  near  relations)  to  have  left  that  £1,000 
only  to  the  College,  and  the  bulk  of  his  fortune  to  his 
friend  Mr.  Canning  of  Ipswich,  who  luckily  dying  first  the 
whole  was  left  to  us.  The  College  disposed  of  X2,700  in 
increasing  the  Mastership  £40  per  annum,  and  the  4 
Senior  Fellowships  £10  per  annum,  and  applied  the  rest 
to  the  public  uses  of  the  College.* 


CHAPTER  VIII 

MASTEESHIP  OF  BICHARD  FARMER  (1775-1797) 

The  last  College  order  signed  by  Richardson  is  dated 
February  14,  1775,  and  before  another  month  was  over 
he  was  dead.  Hubbard  was  elected  to  succeed  him,  but 
declined  the  office,  and  on  March  21,  1775,  Richard 
Farmer  was  unanimously  elected.  He  was  a  great 
contrast  both  to  his  predecessor  and  to  Hubbard, 
whom  he  had  succeeded  in  the  tutorship  in  1767,  for  as 
Tutor  he  had  a  poor  reputation  for  business  habits  ;  he 
often  neglected  to  send  in  his  accounts,  and  for  many 
years  had  considerable  sums  owing  to  him.  But  he  had 
already  attained  a  distinct  position  in  the  literary  world 
outside  the  College.  He  was  an  F.S.A.,  and  nine  years 
before  his  election  had  published  the  work  for  which 
he  is  best  known  now — the  Essay  on  the  Learning  of 
Shakespear.  This  essay  showed  an  extraordinary  know- 
ledge of  old  English  literature,  and  established  once 
for  all  the  fact  that  the  plays  could  be  accounted  for 
without  attributing  to  Shakespeare  a  learning  which  all 
his  contemporaries  denied  him.  His  reputation  was 
the  cause  of  Dr.  Johnson's  one  visit  to  Cambridge 
in  1765,  of  which  an  interesting  account  exists.     He 

152 


FARMER^S  MASTERSHIP  153 

was  also  known  to  have  been  long  engaged  on  a 
history  of  Leicester,  in  which  town  he  was  born  in 
1735.  But  on  his  election  to  the  mastership  he  re- 
turned the  subscriptions  and  handed  over  the  materials 
which  he  had  collected  to  Nichols,  who  embodied  them 
in  his  History  of  Leicester  shire.  Farmer  held  several  pre- 
ferments in  the  Church — Whitehall  Preacher  in  1769, 
Prebend  and  Chancellor  of  Lichfield  in  1780,  Canon 
of  Canterbury  in  1782,  and  of  St.  PauPs  in  1788.  In  all 
situations  of  life  he  seems  to  have  exhibited  the  same 
characteristics  :  he  was  careless  of  dress  and  appearance  ; 
devoted  to  literature,  though  too  indolent  to  produce 
any  work  of  importance ;  enthusiastic  as  a  collector  of 
books,  yet  careless  as  to  their  preservation  or  integrity ; 
keen  as  a  Churchman  and  Tory  politician,  yet  never 
taking  an  active  part  either  in  Church  work  or  politics. 
He  refused  a  bishopric,  and  was  content  to  enjoy  the  life 
of  the  combination-room  and  of  the  literary  clubs.  Three 
things  it  was  said  he  loved  above  all  else — old  port,  old 
clothes,  and  old  books.  He  appears  to  have  retained 
the  tutorship  for  some  years  after  his  election  to 
the  mastership,  but  becoming  University  Librarian  in 
1780,  he  resigned  the  tutorship,  and  was  succeeded  by 
Rennet.  Though  thus  careless  and  ease-loving,  in- 
vincibly averse  from  getting  out  of  bed  or  going  to  bed, 
or  settling  an  account,  his  kindness  and  good-nature 
preserved  the  peace  in  College,  which  had  so  often 
been  disturbed  under  his  predecessors,  and  secured  the 
affection  and  admiration  of  the  Fellows. 

The  account  of  him  given  by  Gunning  throws  some 
light  upon  the  social  habits  of  the  day  in  the  College, 
which   had  become  on  the  one  hand  extremely  Tory 


154  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

and  orthodox,  and  on  the  other  noted  for  a  certain 
moderate  conviviality,  tinged  in  Farmer's  time,  at  any 
rate,  with  an  interest  in  letters.  As  an  illustration  of 
the  latter,  it  may  be  noted  that  in  a  list  of  persons 
helped  from  the  offertory  money  collected  in  the 
chapel  occurs  the  name  of  Mrs.  Chatterton,  mother  of 
the  unfortunate  poet  (1778).  Dinner  in  those  days  was 
at  half-past  one  or  two,  and  a  little  later  at  three ; 
it  was  after  the  evening  supper  that  pipes  and  tobacco 
and  cheerful  conversation  made  the  parlour  of  Emmanuel 
a  favourite  place  of  meeting.  Before  his  mastership 
Farmer  served  the  curacy  of  Swavesey  in  the  manner 
thus  described  by  Gunning : 

'  He  began  the  service  punctually  at  the  appointed  time 
and  gave  a  plain  practical  sermon,  strongly  enforcing  some 
moral  duty.  After  service  he  chatted  most  affably  with 
his  congregation,  and  never  failed  to  send  some  small 
present  to  such  of  his  poor  parishioners  as  had  been  kept 
from  church  by  illness.  After  morning  service  he  repaired 
to  the  public-house,  where  a  mutton-chop  and  potatoes 
were  soon  set  before  him  :  these  were  quickly  despatched, 
and  immediately  after  the  removal  of  the  cloth,  Mr.  Dobson 
(his  churchwarden)  and  one  or  two  of  the  principal  farmers, 
made  their  appearance,  to  whom  he  invariably  said,  "  I  am 
going  to  read  prayers,  but  shall  be  back  by  the  time  you 
have  made  the  punch."  Occasionally  another  farmer 
accompanied  him  from  church,  when  pipes  and  tobacco 
were  in  requisition  until  six  o'clock.  Taffy  was  then  led 
to  the  door,  and  he  conveyed  his  master  to  his  rooms  by 
half-past  seven ;  here  he  found  his  slippers  and  nightcap, 
and  taking  possession  of  his  elbow-chair,  he  slept  till  his 
bedmaker  aroused  him  at  nine  o'clock,  when  resuming  his 


FARMER'S  MASTERSHIP  155 

wig  he  started  for  the  Parlour,  where  the  fellows  were  in 
the  habit  of  assembling  on  a  Sunday  afternoon.' 

When  Farmer  became  Master  be  presided  over  these 
symposia  with  great  geniality,  and  the  parlour  was 
'  always  open  to  those  who  loved  pipes  and  cheerful  con- 
versation.' These  gatherings  were  specially  frequent  at 
the  end  of  the  long  vacation,  when  the  theatre  (standing 
where  the  Shakespear  Tavern  now  is)  was  open  for  about 
three  weeks  at  the  time  of  Stourbridge  Fafr.  Farmer 
constantly  had  a  party  staying  at  the  Lodge  for  this 
festivity,  and  with  other  well-known  Shakespearian 
scholars  —  such  as  Stevens  and  Malone — occupied  a 
particular  part  of  the  pit,  which  thus  became  known 
as  'the  critics'  row.'  They  came  nearly  every  day, 
stopped  to  the  end,  and  then  walked  to  the  Emmanuel 
parlour,  and  finished  the  evening  with  tobacco  and 
conversation. 

The  parlour  had  always  played  a  great  part  in  the 
social  life  of  the  College.  We  hear  of  it  from  the  first : 
it  was  noted  as  a  special  feature  of  the  College  by 
John  Evelyn  when  he  visited  'that  zealous  house'  in 
1654,  and  it  has  always  been,  as  it  continues  to  be,  the 
common  meeting  ground  of  the  society,  whether  for 
College  business  or  relaxation.  Early  in  the  eighteenth 
century,  when  dinner  ceased  to  be  the  early  noontide 
meal,  and  was  taken  as  late  as  three  or  even  four  in  the 
afternoon,  it  seems  to  have  been  regarded  as  the  end  of 
the  working  day  by  most,  and  was  followed  by  a  fairly 
prolonged  session  over  the  College  port,  which,  in 
the  summer  at  least,  was  followed  by  an  adjournment 
to  the  garden  and  a  game  of  bowls.     It  was  in  the 


156  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

evening  after  supper  that  the  members  met  again  to 
talk  and  smoke.  The  daily  meeting  in  the  parlour  led 
to  a  kind  of  organization.  A  body  of  rules  was  formed, 
which  were  enforced  by  fines,  always  in  bottles  of 
wine.  Some  of  the  rules  are  rather  fantastical,  but  on 
the  whole  they  served  their  purpose  of  keeping  up  a 
good  standard  of  manners  and  preventing  petty  quarrels 
and  personalities.  Penalties  were  imposed  for  coming 
late  into  Hall,  or  with  '  rolers  in  his  hair,'  for  leaving 
bowls  on  tlfe  lawn,  for  coming  into  Hall  in  a  new  coat 
(the  Lord  Westmorland  of  the  day  frequently  incurred 
this),  for  putting  one''s  fork  into  the  dish,  for  taking 
salt  out  of  the  salt-cellar  with  anything  but  the  salt- 
spoon,  for  using  nasty  or  offensive  words  at  dinner, 
or  taking  snuff  at  table,  or  '  reflecting  "*  on  a  member 
of  the  parlour,  and  other  such  social  offences.  Bets 
(always  in  bottles  of  wine)  were  also  of  nearly  daily 
occurrence.  The  parlour  books  recording  these  bets 
are  preserved  from  early  in  the  eighteenth  century. 
Many  of  them  are  now  unintelligible  (having  been  pur- 
posely put  obscurely),  many  more  are  upon  subjects 
that  could  never  have  had  much  interest,  and  have 
long  lost  what  they  had.  But  scattered  among  frivoli- 
ties or  officialisms  are  some  that  touch  on  public  events 
of  first-rate  interest  and  importance.  A  few  will  show 
how  contemporaries  viewed  these  great  events  : 

1770.  Lord  North  will  resign  before  next  Michaelmas. 

1779.  No  place  whatever  in  America  (except  Canada) 
in  the  hands  of  the  Loyalists  by  March  17,  1780. 

1780.  A  naval  engagement  between  Rodney  and  the 
French,  in  which  the  latter  are  beaten,  on  or  before 
June  30,  1781. 


FARMER'S  MASTERSHIP  167 

1787,  October  3.  Some  censure  passed  by  the  House  of 
Lords  against  Mr.  Hastings. 

1791,  Mai/  29.  Hastings  will  be  found  guilty. 

1792,  March  2.  The  majority  will  double  the  minority 
in  the  censure  of  Pitt. 

1792,  Juli/  13.  Neither  Austrians  nor  Prussians  at  Paris 
in  two  months'  time. 

1792,  November  13.  The  King  of  France  will  be  con- 
demned, but  not  executed. 

1793.  January  5.  If  the  King  of  France  is  ordered 
out  of  the  kingdom,  he  does  not  reach  the  frontiers  in 
safety. 

1793,  March  28.  The  Dauphin  not  put  to  death  in 
Easter  week. 

1793,  June  18.  Marat  will  be  murdered  before  the  end 
of  next  Lent  term. 

1793,  Jtme  26.  Valenciennes  in  the  hands  of  the  allied 
Powers  in  six  weeks. 

1793,  December  3.  Barrere  and  Robespierre  either  dead 
or  out  of  France  in  six  months. 

1794-,  March  27.  The  Convention  deposed  in  less  than 
six  months. 

1794,  April  26.  That  at  the  conclusion  of  the  war  the 
executive  power  in  France  will  not  be  in  the  hands  of  an 
individual. 

1794,  April  30  and  May  3.  The  war  will  be  over  in  two 
years. 

1794,  May  l6.  The  Duke  of  York  in  Paris  on  or  before 
June  20. 

These  optimistic  views  of  the  great  war  just  be- 
ginning were,  of  course,  destined  to  cost  their  backers 
sundry  bottles  of  wine.  We  presently  trace  the  progress 
of  the  war  by  taxes  and  falling  funds.     On  February  25, 


158  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

1795,  a  bet  is  made  that  '  there  are  not  more  than  six 
resident  members  of  the  College  that  will  not  take  out 
a  licence  to  wear  powder  within  three  months  after  the 
day  fixed  by  the  Act  of  Parliament/  A  truer  prophet 
(if  rather  previous)  bets  on  March  6  that  'there  will 
not  be  a  wig  in  two  years."*  The  effect  of  the  mutiny  at 
the  Nore  combined  with  the  war  is  shown  by  a  bet  on 
October  14,  1797,  that '  the  three  per  cents,  will  not  be 
52f  in  this  day's  paper.'  Meanwhile  confidence  in  the 
success  of  England  was  diminishing.  In  spite  of  the 
victory  of  St.  Vincent  (February  14,  1797)  having 
frustrated  the  Franco-Spanish  scheme  of  invasion,  there 
is  a  bet  on  the  28th  that  the  French  will  land  100  men 
within  a  month;  on  May  20,  that  Ireland  will  be  a 
department  of  France  within  two  years.  On  June  11 
the  execution  of  the  mutineer  Parker  is  betted  upon. 
Next  year  (1798),  on  January  22,  a  bet  is  made  that 
'the  French  will  not  land  1,000  men  in  Great  Britain 
or  Ireland  within  six  months.'  Next  the  national  sub- 
scription is  noticed,  with  a  list  of  Cambridge  contribu- 
tions, and  a  bet  that  Oxford  will  do  less.  The 
University  gave  1,000  guineas.  Trinity,  St.  John's,  and 
King's  500  guineas,  Emmanuel  and  Caius  coming  next 
with  200  guineas.  On  October  22  the  effect  of  the 
Battle  of  the  Nile  (August  1)  is  seen  by  a  bet  'that 
Bonaparte's  head  will  be  on  the  walls  of  the  Seraglio 
within  three  months.'  The  next  change  is  shown  by  a 
bet  on  November  19,  1799,  that  'Bonaparte,  Sieyes, 
and  Ducos  if  they  remain  in  office  six  months  will 
restore  royalty'  (after  the  coup  cTetat  of  November  9). 

During  the  next  fourteen  years  the  great  events  in 
the  Napoleonic  wars  are  only  occasionally  referred  to, 


FARMER'S  MASTERSHIP  159 

and  I  can  only  find  one  allusion  to  the  Peninsular  War. 
But  on  April  6,  1814,  there  are  two  pages  of  almost 
lyrical  exultation  over  the  entry  of  the  allies  into  Paris 
on  March  31 — news  of  which,  it  seems,  had  taken  a  week 
to  reach  Cambridge.  Many  members  of  the  parlour 
presented  bottles  of  wine  to  '  congratulate  the  world/ 
and  bets  are  made  that  Bonaparte  will  be  dead  or  in 
prison  in  a  month.  More  bottles  were  given  on 
April  14,  on  the  occasion  of  the  '  illumination  '  and  for 
the  downfall  of '  Signor  Bonaparte,**  and  on  'Louis  XVIII. 
being  called  to  the  throne  of  his  ancestors,"  and,  again, 
on  June  24  on  the  proclamation  of  peace.  The  escape 
from  Elba  (March  1,  1815)  is  indicated  by  a  bet  on 
March  13  that  Napoleon  *  will  be  either  a  prisoner  or 
sent  out  of  France  in  six  weeks."*  But  the  hundred  days 
and  Waterloo  itself  are  otherwise  unrecorded,  owing  to 
the  absence  of  most  Fellows  from  a  serious  fever  that 
occurred  at  that  time,  and  no  notice  of  public  affairs  is 
found  till  October  24,  when  a  somewhat  mysterious  bet 
is  made  :  '  Bonaparte  no  command  in  the  Northumber- 
land within  three  months."*  By  that  time  Napoleon  had 
been  more  than  a  week  in  St.  Helena,  but  some  timid 
member  of  the  parlour  seems  to  have  had  doubts  as  to 
his  being  safely  conveyed  there. 

These  specimens  will  be  sufficient  to  illustrate  the  social 
side  of  the  College  life  at  this  time.  Farmer  took  his 
full  share  in  it,  and,  in  spite  of  his  canonry  at  St.  PauFs, 
passed  most  of  his  time  in  College.  He  was  Vice- 
Chancellor  in  1775-76,  in  which  year  the  Senate  voted 
an  address  to  the  King  on  the  American  War ;  but  among 
a  minority  of  dissentients  was  one  which  had  the  key 
of  the  room  in  which  the  common  seal  of  the  University 


160  EMMANUEL  COLLEGE 

was  kept,  and  who  refused  to  produce  it  to  seal  the 
address,  whereupon  Farmer  is  said  to  have  broken  open 
the  door,  using  a  crowbar  with  his  own  hands.  But  if  he 
had  such  contentions  in  the  University,  his  mastership 
was  one  of  peace  in  College,  and  apparently  of  financial 
improvement,  the  Fellowships  being  generally  kept  up  to 
their  full  number,  and  their  value  not  being  less  than 
about  ^50 ;  while  the  debt  to  the  Rev.  R.  Richardson,  in- 
curred originally  for  including  the  Manor  of  Shemboum, 
and  which  amounted  in  time  to  ^1,100,  was  being 
gradually  paid  off.  Farmer  appears  never  to  have  aban- 
doned his  passion  for  book-collecting,  and  his  library 
contained  many  rarities — twelve  Caxtons,  a  First  Folio 
of  Shakespeare,  several  copies  of  the  first  edition  of 
Paradise  Lost,  and  hundreds  of  rare  tracts  and  plays. 
He  was  careless  as  to  the  preservation  of  these  treasures, 
and  his  copies  are  said  to  have  been  usually  in  a  bad 
condition.  Still,  the  collection  fetched  over  <^2,000  at 
his  death,  having  cost  him  little  more  than  ^500.  He 
belonged  to  many  clubs  in  London,  but  it  was  only  two 
years  before  his  death  that  he  was  elected  to  the 
greatest  of  them  all,  the  *  Literary  Club '  of  Johnson 
and  Reynolds.  The  portrait  of  Farmer — now  in  the 
combination-room — is  by  Romney,  with  whom  he  was 
personally  acquainted.  It  has  lately  been  cleaned  and 
restored,  and  gives  a  very  striking  illustration  of  the 
cheerfulness  attributed  to  him  by  his  contemporaries.  In 
his  mastership  the  College  celebrated  its  two-hundredth 
anniversary.  Of  the  first  centenary  (1684)  there  is 
no  record  of  any  celebration,  and  the  College  at  that 
time  was  at  a  low  ebb  in  all  respects.  But  in  1784? 
things  were  better,  and  a  great  banquet  was  given  on 


FARMER'S  MASTERSHIP  161 

September   29    to   celebrate    the   bicentenary   of    the 
foundation. 

The  account  in  the  Cambridge  Chronicle  of  October  2 
speaks  of  a  sermon  and  Te  Deum  and  a  Latin  speech 
in  the  chapel,  commemorating  the  benefactors,  with  a 
special  anthem  composed  by  the  Professor  of  Music, 
followed  by  a  banquet  in  Hall  and  a  dessert  in  the  long 
gallery.  The  founder's  family  was  represented  by  the 
Earl  of  Westmorland,  and  among  the  past  members 
invited  are  mentioned  the  Hon.  Mr.  Cockayne,  Sir 
Edmund  Bacon,  Sir  John  Cotton,  Sir  Edward  Lyttleton, 
Sir  Richard  Chase,  Sir  J.  B.  Warren.  It  was  also 
attended  by  William  Pitt,  recently  elected  member  for 
the  University  and  just  appointed  Chancellor  of  the 
Exchequer,  as  well  as  by  the  Vice-Chancellor  and  other 
University  officials.  The  paragraph  goes  on  to  speak 
with  admiration  of  the  'attention  of  the  Master  and 
society  to  accommodate  their  numerous  guests,'  and  of 
'the  decency  and  propriety  mixed  with  the  convivial 
good-humour  of  the  whole.'  Gunning  in  his  reminiscences 
gives,  after  his  wont,  a  rather  more  frivolous  account 
of  the  festivity,  recording  the  lively  turtles  to  be  seen  in 
tubs  at  the  Lodge,  and  the  song  of  the  Music  Professor 
in  the  character  of  a  drunken  man.  He  had  heard 
also  from  his  father,  who  seems  to  have  been  a 
guest,  that  Pitt  was  'the  life  and  soul  of  the  party.' 
The  celebration  seems  to  have  been  little  more 
than  a  somewhat  elaborate  commemoration  of  bene- 
factors, and  no  allusion  to  it  found  its  way  into  the 
order-book.  It  was  on  this  occasion  that  it  was 
arranged  to  have  engravings  made  of  Farmer's  portrait, 
which  gave  rise  to  a  rather  poor  J^m  (Tesprit,  which,

however,   has   the   merit   of   telling    us    one    or    two 
facts : 

'  As  to  the  eating  part,  of  that 
Good  plenty  was  at  hand  ;

When  the  College  had  lived  through  another  century, 
the  anniversary  was  celebrated  on  a  much  more 
elaborate  scale.  It  may  be  as  well  here,  once  for  all, 
to  notice  this  later  celebration  side  by  side  with  the 
older  one.  The  Master  was  then  the  Rev.  S.  G. 
Phear,  the  Senior  Tutor  was  Mr.  W.  Chawner  (since 
Master),  the  Steward  Mr.  W.  N.  Shaw,  and  the  Bui-sar 
the  Rev.  A.  Rose.  These  officers  took  great  and  most 
successful  pains  to  make  the  celebration  effective.  In- 
vitations were  sent  to  Harvard  College,  Massachusetts, 
which  was  accordingly  represented  by  Professor  Charles 
Eliot  Norton,  and  to  the  American  Minister,  James 
Russell  Lowell,  who  also  attended.  The  founder's 
family  was  represented  by  Sir  Henry  Mildmay,  the  Earl 
of  Westmorland  being  unable  to  come.  About  209 
members  of  the  College  also  accepted  the  invitation, 
an4  w^ere  accommodated  in  the  College  rooms  or  in  those
